#3055. 3 Dirt Cheap Artificial Intelligence (AI) Stocks to Load Up on Before 2027

AI spending continues to rise, and three of its biggest beneficiaries still trade at bargain valuations.

By Prosper Junior Bakiny – Nov 19, 2026 at 2:14PM EST

Many investors and analysts still aren't convinced that the artificial intelligence (AI) industry will continue expanding rapidly. That's partly why some AI companies, particularly those spending a small fortune on capex, haven't performed that well this year. But what if Wall Street is wrong and the AI market maintains its recent momentum over the medium term? In my view, there is a good chance that will happen. And if it does, several AI stocks could be great bargain buys right now. Let's consider three of them: Alphabet (GOOG -1.65%) (GOOGL -1.73%), Amazon (AMZN -3.56%), and Micron Technology (MU -1.15%).

Alphabet's shares are down 11% this year. Investors are worried about the company's capex and declining free cash flow, which has been negative for two straight quarters. However, Alphabet's AI-related investments are paying off. The company's core advertising business remains strong, and cloud computing sales continue to accelerate.

In the third quarter, Alphabet's total revenue came in at $127.6 billion, up 25% year over year. The company's Google Cloud sales were $30.7 billion, up 103% compared to the year-ago period. The tech leader's earnings per share declined to $2.12, down from $2.87, while its free cash flow was a negative $9.1 billion.

That said, Alphabet ended the quarter with $561 billion in cloud backlog, up 262% year over year -- and it expects to recognize 55% of it, or $308.55 billion, over the next 24 months. The company will likely receive new orders as well. That suggests Alphabet's cloud sales could continue to grow at a good clip in the foreseeable future, especially given the AI industry's continued growth.

The near-term negative free cash flow is worth the massive opportunities Alphabet is tapping into. The stock may have lagged broader equities this year, but Alphabet's prospects look bright from current levels. The company's price/earnings-to-growth (PEG) ratio of 0.5 is well within the undervalued range, which typically starts below 1.

Amazon has remained the leader in the cloud computing market, despite stiff competition from Alphabet and Microsoft (MSFT -1.24%) in recent years. What's more, AI is also helping boost the company's Amazon Web Services revenue. In the third quarter, Amazon's net sales increased by 12% year over year to $201.9 billion.

AWS revenue was $46 billion, up 39% year over year -- the strongest growth rate it has recorded in 18 quarters. Amazon's EPS decreased 3% year over year to $1.89. AWS continues to account for most of Amazon's operating profits, and demand for the company's services remains strong, as evidenced by its $578 billion AWS backlog, which the company said grew in the triple digits year over year.

Amazon could make significant margin gains in the medium term by doubling down on custom AI chips, which tend to be more cost-effective than comparable GPUs (graphics processing units). Further, the company's AI initiatives could also improve its e-commerce business, which remains its largest source of sales. Lastly, Amazon's PEG ratio is only about 0.6, which makes the stock look like a steal at current levels. Long-term investors bullish on AI's prospects can hardly go wrong with this stock.

Micron Technology designs memory chips, which are critical components in the AI value chain. In fact, a shortage in this industry has granted the semiconductor leader significant pricing power. Its revenue and earnings have skyrocketed as a result. In the fourth quarter of its fiscal year 2026, which ended on Sept. 3, Micron's revenue grew by 379% year over year to $54.23 billion.

The company's non-GAAP earnings per share were $33.42, up 1,003% compared to the year-ago period.

How long will the memory chip shortage last? Perhaps until 2028 or 2029, according to some projections, potentially providing a strong tailwind to Micron through the medium term. And although the company faces the risk of slowing top- and bottom-line growth -- as well as potential regulatory problems -- the market has already factored these in. Micron's PEG ratio is only 0.2, which makes the stock dirt cheap right now.

#3052. What to Buy (and Skip) on Black Friday 2026

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Electronics, small appliances, toys and clothes make the list, along with some out-of-the-box buys.

Black Friday comes but once a year — although sometimes it feels like it lasts for a month. The big day is Nov. 27, but the sales have already started.

If you’ve been sitting on a holiday shopping list and waiting for prices to drop, we’ve got good news: It’s time to start your shopping engines! (So to speak.)

You don’t need a calendar full of sale dates to navigate the shopping frenzy. Focus on the categories likely to offer the biggest savings. Here’s what to buy, and what can wait.

If you’ve got top-priority toys on your list, buy them at a discount if you can find them, says Kristin McGrath, senior editor at The Krazy Coupon Lady, “just in case they don’t go down in price later.”

Non-electronic toys are a good bet, says Julie Ramhold, consumer analyst with DealNews.com, because they’re less affected by tariffs on electronic components. That includes Legos, dolls and playsets.

NeeDoh squishies are a hot stocking stuffer this year and may be in short supply. At authorized retailers like Target, they should be relatively affordable, but try to avoid unauthorized resellers who may crank up the price, Ramhold says.

While you may see mattress stores offering a few doorbusters on Black Friday, don’t feel pressured to jump on a deal.

“Mattresses tend to see some of their strongest discounts around major sales events like Labor Day and Presidents Day,” says Ashley Feinstein Gerstley, shopping and savings strategist with Rakuten. “So if you’re not in a rush, it may be worth waiting for one of those windows.”

Similarly, bedding and towels are often on deep discount during January white sales, Feinstein Gerstley says.

Got some counter space and an itch for a new food processor? Black Friday sales are a great time to price surf for countertop appliances, including blenders, juicers and air fryers.

“Those things we see sometimes half off or more,” McGrath says. “And they’re all on sale at the same time, so any one you’re looking for is likely to have a discount.”

Holiday decor is only a must-buy during Black Friday sales if you need it for this holiday season.

While you’ll still see sales on holiday items like artificial trees, drinkware and garlands, “if you can wait until after Christmas, that’s when discounts should reach up to 80% or more at stores like Target, Walmart and department stores like JCPenney and Macy’s,” Ramhold says.

Electronics are typically a slam-dunk buy at this time of year. “Every store is competing for your business, so televisions, headphones, tablets, laptops — they’re all going to be on sale at the same time,” McGrath says.

Gaming consoles, in particular, see some of the better discounts. And they often come bundled with a gift card, gaming accessories or video games. “Consoles are common gifts,” McGrath says. “The stores know that, so they’re offering competitive deals.”

That said, the traditional deep discounts you usually see on electronics for Black Friday may be a little lackluster this year due to high prices on parts, Ramhold says.

For best results, research the exact specs of the product you want and put it in your cart ahead of time, says consumer savings expert Andrea Woroch. That way, if a similar (but not identical) product goes on sale, you won’t accidentally buy the wrong thing.

You’ll usually find deeper discounts on items that are associated with fitness resolutions after New Year’s Day.

“Big-box retailers like Target and department stores like Macy’s will offer up to 40% off or more on both fitness equipment and activewear during early January sales,” Ramhold says.

Many fashion retailers offer Black Friday sales, McGrath says. So if there’s an item you stock in multiple colors or a work staple you need to replace, keep an eye on your favorite sites for the markdowns.

Shoes, too, are a category to eyeball, including seasonal buys and everyday options like running shoes, Ramhold says. “Some retailers may offer a BOGO promotion of some kind, but it’ll likely just be a ‘buy one, get one for 50% off,’” she says.

Just shop early for your size and color before inventory runs low, McGrath says. “You don’t want to miss out if there's a great deal.”

One caveat: Winter apparel will usually see bigger discounts toward the end of the year, Woroch says. So wait on your boot and coat purchases if you can.

Black Friday also offers the chance to snag deals on things you can use throughout the year:

Photo gifts: Whether you’re ordering holiday cards or putting together a photo book for your in-laws (or yourself), you can usually find sales from vendors like Snapfish and Shutterfly, among others.

Fitness classes: Try your local gym, yoga or Pilates studio for discounts on class packs. “I’ll buy a 20-class package, and it’s always a great deal,” Woroch says.

Spa services: Check local businesses for deals on massages, manicures and other self-care treatments.

Streaming subscriptions: If you’re a new customer (or canceled at least a month ago), some streaming platforms offer deep Black Friday discounts.

Black Friday deals are as much about strategy as savings, so make sure you’re maximizing your spending with these tips:

Let AI help. Tools like ChatGPT and Gemini can do a lot of the legwork for you. Write out a product name and try prompts like “Find the lowest price for X product by comparing every retail site that it’s sold on and including coupons and cash back offers,” Woroch says. (Just note that AI searches can be limited and may not find every deal available.)

Check the price, not just the percentage: “Sometimes the base price is inflated or it was listed initially for a higher price,” Feinstein Gerstley says. “Another retailer might have the same item for less, even if their discount doesn't look as impressive.”

Use a browser extension or cash-back app. As long as you’re spending money, recapture some of it with a browser extension or cash-back app that sends a percentage of your purchase back to you.

#3049. The Identity Crisis At The Heart Of The Agent Economy

Rohan Pinto is CTO/Founder of 1Kosmos and a strong technologist with a strategic vision to lead technology-based growth initiatives.

​Every time an AI agent introduces itself with a name, model version or friendly handle, it makes a claim that proves little on its own. Anyone can copy a name or impersonate another agent. In a world where autonomous agents negotiate contracts, move money, delegate tasks and act on behalf of people and organizations, a self-declared identity is not enough. The claim must be verifiable, and the authority behind it must be enforceable.

The agent economy faces a problem familiar from the early internet. Caller ID appeared trustworthy until technology made it easy to spoof a number. Agent networks face a similar challenge. The question is no longer whether an agent can claim an identity. It is whether that identity can be verified and trusted despite stolen credentials, compromised runtimes, malicious delegation and model changes.

Cryptography provides the foundation for trustworthy agent identity. A signature over a fresh challenge can demonstrate control of a private key. A trusted enrollment process can bind that key to a registered agent and its accountable sponsor.

But control of a key does not establish good intent, prove that the execution environment is uncompromised or authorize a specific action. Identity and authorization are separate layers. Identity helps establish who is making a request. Authorization determines whether that actor may perform a particular operation against a specific resource.

An agent can have a valid identity and still attempt an unauthorized transaction. It can also be manipulated through prompt injection or compromised tools into requesting an action its sponsor never intended.

Identity establishes a basis for trust. It does not eliminate the need to enforce boundaries.

Trustworthy agent identity requires resistance to credential theft and cloning, tamper evident records, protection against impersonation and verifiable accountability.

These are security objectives, not absolute guarantees. Public keys and identifiers can be copied without compromising the associated private key. The real concern is whether an unauthorized party can use that information to impersonate the agent or obtain its authority.

Hardware-backed keys, secure enclaves, trusted platform modules and managed key services can make private key extraction substantially harder. Attestation can provide evidence about the execution environment, while short-lived credentials can limit the exposure window after a token is compromised. However, a compromised runtime may still abuse a legitimate signing interface.

The goal is not to promise an identity that can never be compromised. It is to make impersonation harder, constrain misuse and ensure that security failures can be detected and attributed.

Consider Agent A delegating a task to Agent B, which then calls Agent C to access sensitive information. If all three use the same credentials, investigators may struggle to establish which agent initiated an operation or whether the delegation was authorized.

Real delegation must preserve the relationship between the original authority and the downstream action. Each grant should define the permitted scope, recipient, duration and relevant conditions. An agent should receive only the authority necessary for its task, not unrestricted access inherited from another agent.

Recording a delegation chain is not enough. The relevant enforcement point must validate the authority before permitting access.

Revocation matters just as much. It should prevent new authorization and limit the usefulness of credentials already issued. Depending on the architecture, it may also stop work in progress. It cannot undo a completed transaction.

Short-lived credentials, runtime authorization checks, cancellation mechanisms and tamper evident audit records help reduce exposure. A log supports accountability, but only enforcement mechanisms can prevent unauthorized operations.

AI models, context windows, tools and execution environments can change frequently. If identity is tied directly to model weights, every model update risks disrupting continuity and historical accountability.

A stronger approach attaches logical identity to the registered agent and its accountable sponsor, rather than to a particular model checkpoint.

However, continuity of identity must not mean continuity of trust without reassessment. The system should preserve the agent’s identity while recording the model version, runtime, relevant configuration and policy revision associated with consequential operations. Material changes may require additional evaluation or approval.

The identity of the accountable actor and the provenance of the system performing the work are related, but they are not interchangeable.

If an AI agent makes a million-dollar mistake, accountability should not begin with a forensic investigation after the damage occurs.

Before receiving consequential permissions, an agent should have an established sponsor and approval path. For important operations, the evidence chain should connect the agent identity, execution context, delegation, authorization decision, resource accessed and resulting action.

Cryptographically protected records can help establish what the system permitted and which registered identities participated. They do not automatically determine legal liability, which depends on applicable law, contracts and circumstances. They provide a stronger evidentiary foundation for accountability.

A named sponsor without enforceable boundaries is insufficient. A secure key without attributable operations is insufficient. An audit trail without reliable evidence is insufficient. Trustworthy systems need all three: accountable sponsorship, enforceable limits and verifiable evidence.

Organizations evaluating agent identity platforms should examine how credentials are protected, sponsorship is established, delegation is constrained, authorization is enforced, revocation is handled and evidence is preserved across model changes. They should distinguish published design principles from implemented capabilities, evaluating security claims against actual deployment configurations and available evidence.​​

The agent economy requires more than names or keys. It requires verifiable identity, enforceable authorization and delegation that preserves accountability. An identity that can be impersonated without detection is not trustworthy. An identity without enforceable boundaries is not enough to make an autonomous agent safe.

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3048. Anthropic vs. SpaceX: How 2026's Two Biggest IPOs Stack Up.

One company is the clear "winner," but its investors may not be...

By John Bromels – Nov 19, 2026 at 2:15PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Anthropic ›

For months, one question has dominated coverage of the Anthropic (ANT +17.65%) IPO. No, not "How can I buy shares?" or "When will it start trading?"

The big question has been, "Will its debut be bigger than SpaceX's (SPCX -4.19%)?"

Well, now that shares have begun trading, we can give a definitive answer. And that answer is, "It depends on what you mean by 'bigger.'"

Here's how the Anthropic and SpaceX IPOs stacked up, which company came out on top by each metric, and -- most importantly -- which stock's shareholders should be happiest (for now).

Before this year, Saudi Aramco held the record for the largest IPO in stock market history (measured by the amount of money raised through the sale of IPO shares, not by the company’s total market capitalization at its IPO price). It raised $25.6 billion in its Dec. 2019 IPO.

Of course, Saudi Aramco isn't traded on U.S. exchanges, so the biggest U.S. IPO was still Alibaba Group’s (BABA +0.07%) in Sept. 2014, which raised $21.8 billion.

SpaceX shattered both records in June, offering 555.6 million shares at an IPO price of $135 each, for a total IPO of $75 billion. Until Anthropic priced its shares on Nov. 18, it wasn't clear whether the company would take the crown from SpaceX or fall just short.

Anthropic ended up the winner by pricing its IPO shares at $83/share. That's higher than the $70-$78/share range it set at the start of its roadshow, but much lower than its debut trading price (more on that later). At that price, it raised $81.3 billion, a full $6.3 billion ahead of SpaceX, and is -- for now, at least -- the biggest IPO in history.

Anthropic's IPO shares were priced at $83/share, but that was only the price for investors who were lucky enough to get in on the IPO. Everyone else needed to wait for shares to begin trading on the open market, which they did at 11:51 a.m., at $111.50/share. That's an impressive 34.3% premium to the IPO price.

Although SpaceX's per-share IPO price was higher than Anthropic's at $135/share, its shares debuted on the NASDAQ at $150/share, just an 11.1% premium to the IPO price. That means Anthropic IPO investors did better... at least as of the moment their shares started trading.

With about 13.2 billion Class A and Class N shares outstanding, Anthropic's $111.50 debut price values those shares alone at $1.5 trillion, but the company's fully diluted market cap at that price -- including unexercised stock options, restricted stock units, and convertible notes -- is roughly $2 trillion.

SpaceX's debut market cap was about the same, at roughly $1.95 trillion, but the inherent difficulty of calculating full dilution means the two companies are roughly tied by this metric.

Thus far, Anthropic has dominated SpaceX by most metrics, but here's where it may notch its first loss.

Of course, the trading day isn't over yet, but Anthropic's shares stumbled out of the gate. They rose only slightly in the first few moments of trading, to $114.99/share, and then quickly dropped below $100/share.

As of 1:30 p.m., less than two hours after their debut, Anthropic's shares are trading at about $95/share. That's still about 14% above their IPO price... but it's 15% below their market debut price.

SpaceX, meanwhile, saw its shares rise significantly above their debut price during their first two hours of trading in June. They reached an intraday high of $176.52/share (a 17.7% gain from their debut) at 1:10 p.m. They eventually closed at $160.95/share, but they never dropped below their debut price during the day (and in fact continued to move higher over the next few days).

Of course, the trading day isn't over yet, so Anthropic shares could still stage a late rally and pull out a win. But at least for now, on the metric that matters most to retail investors without IPO access, SpaceX's IPO is the winner.

#3047. Mortgage Rates Today, Thursday, November 19: Kind of a Big Jump

Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates rose enough today that you might be feeling some sticker shock.

If you're here looking for lower mortgage interest rates, today is definitely not your day.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage jumped to 7.38% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 25 basis points higher than yesterday and 12 basis points higher than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

Keep in mind that mortgage rates are always on the move, and that if you're tracking rates day-to-day, you're going to see a lot of volatility. Zooming out and looking at the bigger picture — like a graph showing at least a month's worth of rate data — can help you see the overall trend.

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

On Friday, the Bureau of Labor Statistics released the Producer Price Index for October, which tracks the change in prices that U.S. producers are able to get for their products. This is one way to measure inflation on the market end, rather than the consumer end.

The results were mixed. While prices didn’t rise quite as high as economists anticipated month-over-month (0.3% vs. 0.4%), year-over-year price growth did reach 6.5% — the largest increase in four years. This points to even higher costs for consumers coming in the near future.

Investors are convinced that the Federal Reserve will raise overnight borrowing rates again in December to pull inflation down. And higher overnight borrowing rates — called the federal funds rate, which is what banks pay to fund products like mortgages — usually lead to higher mortgage rates.

» MORE: How the Federal Reserve affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.88% or higher — but there aren’t many people for whom that’s true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

#3046. Natera vs. Tempus AI: Which Blood Test Pioneer Has the Better Stock Buy for 2027?

Natera's revenue growth tops 37%, but Tempus AI trades at a lower forward multiple, and the balance sheets and cash flow stories diverge sharply.

By Brendan Coffey – Updated Nov 19, 2026 at 2:20PM EST

Can modern diagnostics and artificial intelligence reshape the future of medicine? Investors looking for growth must choose between Natera Inc. (NTRA +0.19%) and Tempus AI (TEM -5.75%) as they target the next era of healthcare.

Natera focuses on liquid biopsy and genetic testing to provide insights into oncology and reproductive health. Tempus AI applies large-scale data analysis and machine learning to help physicians personalize patient treatments. Both companies sit at the intersection of biology and technology, but their financial structures and risk profiles differ significantly for those looking toward 2027.

Natera is a global leader in molecular diagnostics, specializing in cell-free DNA testing for oncology, women's health, and organ health. It serves a global network of partners, relying on Illumina Inc. (ILMN -4.43%) for sequencers and Amazon.com Inc. (AMZN -3.56%) for data hosting to manage its genomic data. The company is actively expanding its reach through collaborations, such as its partnership for minimal residual disease testing in China, while targeting a large total addressable market among healthcare stocks focusing on precision diagnostics.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached about $2.9 billion. This figure represented growth of approximately 37.4% compared to the previous twelve-month period, driven by strong adoption across its testing platforms. Despite the strong growth, the company reported a net loss of about $164.7 million as it continues to invest in scaling its laboratory operations and research pipeline.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the current ratio stands at roughly 3.4x, which measures a company's ability to pay short-term obligations with liquid assets. Its debt-to-equity ratio, which compares total debt to shareholder equity, is approximately 0.1x, suggesting a relatively low level of leverage. In the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, free cash flow was nearly $109.1 million, but stock-based compensation represented roughly 164.6% of operating cash flow, meaning reported cash generation is heavily inflated by this non-cash add-back.

Tempus AI operates a sophisticated platform that integrates clinical and molecular data to help physicians personalize patient care across oncology and other therapeutic areas. It works extensively with pharmaceutical companies like AstraZeneca (AZN -2.24%) and biotechnology firms to support drug development and clinical trials. The company also utilizes Google Cloud from Alphabet Inc (nasdaq:goog) to maintain its vast multimodal data library, which serves as the backbone for its AI-driven diagnostic tools.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached about $1.5 billion. The company achieved revenue growth of roughly 36.6% over the preceding year, highlighting the rapid expansion of its data-as-a-service and clinical testing businesses. During this same time frame, the company recorded a net loss of approximately $189.3 million, reflecting the high costs associated with developing and maintaining its proprietary technology platform.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the current ratio is roughly 3.1x. Its debt-to-equity ratio is approximately 2.7x, which compares total debt to the value owned by shareholders. In the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, free cash flow was negative at around $245.4 million, which represents the cash a business generates after accounting for the money spent to maintain or expand its asset base.

Natera faces risks from its heavy reliance on a small number of core tests, including Signatera and Panorama. It also navigates intense competition in the molecular testing market from rivals like Guardant Health (GH +1.43%). Legal challenges and patent litigation with other diagnostic firms remain a persistent concern for the business as it seeks to protect its proprietary technology.

Tempus AI is subject to risks related to the accuracy and bias of its artificial intelligence models in clinical settings. The company also deals with high revenue concentration within its oncology product line. Furthermore, federal regulators have issued inquiries regarding its Medicare billing practices, which could lead to future legal or financial complications that impact shareholder value.

Based on the Forward P/E and P/S ratio, Tempus AI appears to be the more affordable option, while Natera trades at a premium.

Valuation metrics include those sourced from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from those of other data providers.

Natera built its early reputation on Panorama, a noninvasive prenatal blood test that screens for a broad range of fetal conditions. Widely adopted across the U.S., Panorama recently received an upgrade designed to close its primary clinical limitation: detecting chromosomal abnormalities even when levels of placental DNA fragments in maternal blood are too low for the prior version to read. Meanwhile, the company's broader DNA-testing momentum is accelerating, supported by roughly 70 active clinical trials focused on earlier cancer detection, identifying additional tumor types, and pinpointing patients who need expedited treatment.

Today, Natera's flagship revenue driver is Signatera, which generates roughly half of total sales. Both Medicare and commercial insurers have been expanding coverage and raising payouts for Signatera--including in breast and ovarian cancers--pushing average reimbursement per test up nearly 40% to almost $1,300 earlier this year. Adding further clinical validation, a U.S. regulatory committee granted Signatera a Category 1 recommendation for use in muscle-invasive bladder cancer this summer.

All that has the business seeing revenue growing at a fast clip, with 2026 expected to come in 30% higher than 2025 and 2027 rising another 23% to about $3.7 billion. While profitability isn't expected until fiscal 2028, the net losses are narrowing and free cash flow is rising.

Meanwhile, Tempus AI CEO Eric Lefkofsky said late this summer that he expects top-line growth of at least 25% annually over the coming years. Tempus AI's data unit has been a standout driver, closing $200 million in deals this summer with partners including Daiichi Sankyo, LevelSet Bio, and Insight Pharmaceuticals, up from over $100 million in each of the prior two quarters. Tempus argues that combining a massive data catalog with continuously learning AI gives its genomic testing an edge over traditional laboratory diagnostics. Raising per-test payouts is another top goal that is gaining traction: Q1 FY2026 revenue per test rose $130 from the prior-year period to $1,850. Following May approval of its tumor-only xT CDx test, analysts see room for meaningful revenue gains through higher pricing across Tempus's diagnostic suite--potentially reaching $400 million next year contingent on approval of a related xF test.

For fiscal 2026, sales should come in around $1.6 billion, a 26% rise from 2025, with a narrower net loss of about $187 million.

Both blood test pioneer stocks come at high premiums, as shown by their forward P/E ratios, but the businesses appear to justify the high hopes. Natrera's revolutionary cell-free DNA (cfDNA) test continues to show promise in detecting more cancers and becoming a standard of care in multiple situations. Sometimes the best stocks are the ones worth paying up for.

#3044. Hope Bancorp: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Lower deposit costs and a variable-rate loan portfolio could position the bank to defend margins through interest-rate cycles.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 19, 2026 at 2:30PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Hope Bancorp ›

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 28, 2026, Hope Bancorp (HOPE -1.38%) announced a 7% quarter-over-quarter revenue increase to $157 million following its acquisition of SMBC MANUBANK, a commercial banking unit. Expanding into complementary commercial lending niches and optimizing funding costs directly impacts long-term earnings power for regional bank shareholders.

Management offers three key insights into where the business is heading.

Integrating new commercial banking operations broadens regional market coverage and deepens corporate client relationships. Reaching distinct customer segments creates organic cross-selling opportunities without adding heavy overhead costs.

Replacing expensive time deposits (certificates of deposit with set expiration dates) with non-maturity deposits (flexible checking and savings accounts) reduces overall funding expense. Lower borrowing costs help expand net interest margin (the difference between interest earned on assets and interest paid on deposits).

Concentrating new loan originations in variable-rate structures protects interest income when market benchmark rates shift. Having nearly three-quarters of the loan book in variable or hybrid structures reduces exposure to fixed-rate repricing lags.

Revenue momentum and operating leverage are expanding into year-end, and leadership isn't hiding its satisfaction with the trajectory.

An expanded Southern California footprint, lower deposit costs, and a broader commercial client network support long-term earnings resilience. Leadership is prioritizing integration discipline and loan pricing oversight.

#3042. Asked on Reddit: I Lost My Job; What Should I Do First?

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Income loss is stressful; the first steps involve cutting costs and focusing on essentials.

A Redditor recently shared that they were struggling to decide which financial steps to take first after a job loss.

Should they withdraw money from retirement savings to have more cash on hand — but pay an early withdrawal penalty? Or is it better to rely on the severance payout to handle expenses?

Many users responded with good ideas: File for unemployment, try to avoid tapping into retirement savings, and cut expenses as much as possible.

For more ideas, I asked three financial experts how to manage money in the immediate aftermath of job loss.

While it’s tempting to make quick decisions about tapping into a retirement fund or selling a house, it’s better to wait on any big moves, says Parker Wine, retirement planning advisor at the financial firm Daniel A. White & Associates in Lewes, Delaware.

That’s because you need time to digest the situation and weigh the pros and cons of different options, he says. “People can get impulsive and panic, and it’s usually a bad thing to make decisions when you’re panicking,” he says.

Take your time, get second opinions and think through all of your options first, Wine suggests.

If you’re fortunate enough to receive a severance package, wait to sign it until you’ve reviewed it carefully and understand all of the details, says John Rafferty, partner and investment advisor representative at Solomon Financial in Carmel, Indiana.

For example, make sure you know about your health insurance options, such as whether you’ll receive insurance through COBRA or need to take out a new policy in the health insurance marketplace.

Filing for unemployment and checking into any other available workplace benefits — such as help with your new job search — is also an important part of understanding what support to expect in the weeks and months ahead, Rafferty adds.

Creating a bare-bones budget stripped of “extras” like restaurant meals or travel can help the severance payment last as long as possible, Rafferty says.

“Now that you don’t have income coming in, ask yourself, ‘How much can I spend and where?’”

You might be able to cut back on entertainment costs, coffee runs and clothing purchases, for example.

At the same time, it’s important to prioritize the essentials like mortgage payments, rent, utilities and food. Rafferty recommends calling your mortgage company, utilities provider and any other lenders to ask if they have a hardship program for those experiencing financial challenges.

Opting in could give you more time to pay your bills or temporarily lower payments.

It’s hard to stick to a budget that eliminates all entertainment, so Rafferty suggests finding enjoyable activities that come with a low price tag. Hiking, discovering a new park and participating in free community events are all ways to enjoy downtime on a budget.

“Having that release is mentally a big thing so you can stay prepared for the job search,” he says. “Otherwise, it’s easy to get down and feel negative, and we need things to help us feel positive.”

Similarly, connecting with friends and family during this time is crucial, says Laura Adams, accredited financial counselor and financial coach in Wilmington, North Carolina.

People often feel a sense of shame or embarrassment after job loss, and Adams encourages people to talk about their feelings and ask for help if needed. “Financial shame can cause us to make emotional rather than confident financial decisions,” she says.

Starting some kind of side hustle to bring in extra cash can also help bridge the gap between the job loss and your next job, Wine says. Substitute teaching, working at a local public golf course and driving for a rideshare service are all options he’s seen people try.

Doing something new also helps stave off boredom while unemployed, but he cautions that you’ll still owe taxes on any side income you bring in, so plan accordingly.

All three financial experts also emphasized that a job loss is easier to handle with an emergency savings fund you can turn to, and suggested building one up — ideally three to six months’ worth of expenses. But starting small helps, too — while employed, if possible.

The Redditor who posted the question mentioned that, like many Americans, they lacked emergency savings. This added urgency to the question of what to prioritize.

Reddit is an online forum where users share their thoughts in “threads” on various topics. The popular site includes plenty of discussion on financial subjects like how to deal with job loss, so we sifted through Reddit forums to get a pulse check. People post anonymously, so we cannot confirm their individual experiences or circumstances.

#3041. AI Agents Will Need A Credit Score For Companies: How Contradictory Records Get Suppliers Dropped From AI Shortlists

Joseph Byrum is an accomplished executive leader, innovator and cross-domain strategist with a track record across multiple industries.

​A supplier can be dropped from a buyer’s shortlist by software, and no one at the company will ever know.

The AI agent building that list has to decide whether each company is who it claims to be. One supplier was acquired last year, and its website carries the new name while an official registry still shows the old one and a public file names a former owner. The agent has no neutral way to settle the conflict, so it moves to the next supplier.

The company never learns it was evaluated.

That scene is a projection, and the pieces that would make it routine are being built now.

Advertising offers a public window; its agent registry and standards are open to inspection and comment. The same logic reaches procurement, where Gartner predicts that by 2028, 90% of B2B buying will be AI agent intermediated, with verifiability a prerequisite.

The infrastructure is moving faster than adoption. IAB Tech Lab went from naming its agentic framework to publishing a third-version draft in about seven months, while an IAB survey found the share of U.S. buyers planning more focus on agentic buying fell from 66% to 54% during 2026.

An agent decides who a company is by comparing what independent sources say about it. When they agree, the match is fast and confident. When they conflict, the agent can guess or move on. In my judgment, a procurement agent has little reason to guess.

Human buyers already behave this way. McKinsey’s 2026 B2B survey names inconsistent information across a supplier’s teams as the top reason buyers switch suppliers, and ranks generative AI among the top five channels for researching them. I expect an agent to apply the same intolerance for mismatch, only faster.

The mechanism favors the company whose name, address and owner read the same everywhere and penalizes the one that changed records in one place only, the normal state after a merger.

In a recent advisory engagement, a private equity-backed data company acquired a second firm and merged it under its own name. AI systems confused the combined company’s leadership and products, and favored the acquired firm’s descriptions over those of the surviving brand.

Three conditions combined: By my count, 87% of the independent sources still described the acquired firm, old product and leadership pages stayed live and were never updated, and the acquired firm’s machine-readable records stayed intact and separate, so the systems saw two companies where there was one.

The name on the door did not decide whose description the systems believed.

Each system verifies a slice. None verifies the whole.

Visibility dashboards answer a different question: Is the company mentioned? Because AI answers vary from run to run, a single ranking partly measures noise, and a preprint review of 45 studies notes low source overlap and substantial run-to-run variability in commercial audits. None of it tells a machine whether a company’s facts agree with each other.

Credit scores settled who to trust with money. Machines will need an answer to whom to trust with a purchase. The shorthand here is an entity authority score, a measure of whether the facts about a company agree and whether an independent party has checked.

• Independence: Builders should not grade their own work, and platforms carry a conflict because they would grade their own market.

• A Range Of Results: The result should arrive as a range, since one run’s number inherits that run’s noise.

• A Way For Companies To Contest: A wrong score nobody can correct is a shortlist drop with no appeal.

The inputs are plain: whether official records, app-store accounts and the website agree on name, address and owner, and whether the company’s own AI agent repeats the public record.

In my view, some form of entity authority score arrives within one to three years. The best-prepared companies will inventory every place their identity is recorded, from registries and app stores to domains and public files. They will assign an owner to each, correct the mismatches and run an identity check before any acquisition or rename closes.

The forecast fails if buyers and platforms never require such a check by 2028, or if free platform-verified profiles make a separate score unnecessary.

The question is whether your company’s records will agree when the first score is computed.

Forbes Technology is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3037. Warren Buffett Has Backed This Consumer Stock for Decades, and Greg Abel Is Still Holding It. Here's Why It's a Buy in November.

It's a shareholder-friendly company that has stood the test of time.

By Stefon Walters – Nov 19, 2026 at 2:30PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Coca-Cola ›

For decades, Warren Buffett was synonymous with Berkshire Hathaway (BRKA -0.85%)(BRKB -0.68%), the company he helped turn from a modest textile manufacturer into a trillion-dollar conglomerate. Berkshire has dozens of businesses under its umbrella, but its stock portfolio under Buffett became a true moneymaker and a source of investment inspiration for everyday investors.

One of Berkshire's long-standing holdings is beverage giant Coca-Cola (KO -1.81%). It's Berkshire's third-largest holding, accounting for 11% of its stock portfolio as of Nov. 18, and that's likely to continue. Even with current CEO Greg Abel taking over for Buffett at the beginning of this year and adjusting Berkshire's portfolio, Berkshire has kept its Coca-Cola stake.

Here's why I'd double down on the stock for the long haul.

The main reason it's a buy in November is the same reason Buffett has loved the stock, and why Berkshire has held onto its 400 million shares for decades: its dividend. Coca-Cola is one of the stock market's premier dividend stocks. It's a Dividend King (a company with at least 50 consecutive years of dividend increases), with 64 consecutive increases under its belt.

When Coca-Cola pays its next $0.53 quarterly dividend, that will be the last payout from its $2.12 commitment this year. That means this year alone, Berkshire would've received $848 million in dividend payouts from Coca-Cola. That's more than many mid-sized companies make in a year, and with Coca-Cola consistently increasing its annual dividend, it's only a matter of time before the payout reaches the billion-dollar mark.

The everyday investor obviously won't be in the same position as Berkshire to receive that much, but Coca-Cola can be a great dividend stock for most portfolios. With Coca-Cola's stock up over 41% this year (as of Nov. 18), its dividend yield has shrunk a bit to around 2.1%, but its average over the past decade has been around 3%.

Coca-Cola has one of the world's most notable brands, and distribution is a major reason why. Its products are in over 200 countries, and its flagship Coca-Cola soda is one of the most successful consumer products ever.

Instead of trying to do the groundwork in all parts of the world, Coca-Cola relies on third-party bottlers and distributors, selling them syrups and concentrates, which they then package and sell to local retailers. This asset-light business model is why it routinely makes close to half of PepsiCo's (PEP -2.21%) revenue while bringing in more net income.

At its size, Coca-Cola won't produce eye-popping growth, but the third quarter was impressive. Revenue grew 7% year over year, operating income (profit from core operations) grew 12%, and earnings per share (EPS) grew 8% to $0.93.

The solid third-quarter results were mainly due to higher volume, but when that's not the case, Coca-Cola has used its pricing power to keep cash flowing. Many consumers are brand loyal when it comes to their go-to beverage, so slight price hikes won't necessarily make them stop buying it. However, that should be more of a nice-to-have for Coca-Cola than a growth driver.

Coca-Cola's 41% surge this year is impressive, no doubt, but it's far from the norm. Since Berkshire made its first Coca-Cola investment in 1988, the stock has only had six years where it produced total returns of over 30%:

We can't predict how Coca-Cola's stock will finish the year, but if it winds up among this group, it would be its best year in decades. That's great news for investors who have been around, but the one "problem" now is that the stock is flirting with expensive territory, trading at 29.1 times its earnings. At the start of the year, it traded around 22.2 times earnings.

Regardless, Coca-Cola's stock is a buy for long-term investors. It has one of the market's most reliable dividends, a stronghold in its industry with unmatched customer loyalty, and continues to be a shareholder-friendly cash cow. It's a trifecta you can't beat.

#3034. With Mortgage Rates Rising, Should You Hibernate Until Spring?

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Higher borrowing costs aren't the whole story. Here's what to consider before putting your house hunt on hold.

Mortgage rates are back up this week, wiping out modest relief from a monthlong drop that began in mid-October.

The average rate on a 30-year fixed-rate mortgage shot up 29 basis points to 7.28% APR in the week ending Nov. 19, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.) We calculate our weekly average using daily APRs recorded over the past five business days.

A mortgage is a big ol’ loan, so when rates go up, those fractions of a percentage point add up fast. Say you’re borrowing $400,000 on a 30-year fixed-rate mortgage. Compared with last week, your monthly principal and interest payment would be about $78 higher today.

One bad week doesn't make a trend, but a jump like this can make putting your house hunt on hold until spring feel like a safer bet. So … is it?

Before deciding whether to buy a house now or wait, it helps to know what's pushing mortgage rates higher.

Right now, mortgage rates are still feeling the aftershocks from two major inflation reports last week. First, the Consumer Price Index (CPI) showed consumer prices rose more than expected in October. Then, the Producer Price Index (PPI) showed wholesale inflation climbed 6.5% over the past year — its biggest annual increase in nearly four years.

While CPI shows how prices have changed, PPI can offer clues about what's coming next. Faced with higher costs, businesses may raise their own prices to compensate, keeping inflation elevated. Federal Reserve policymakers aren't expecting much relief anytime soon, either. According to meeting minutes released Nov. 18, they expect inflation to remain elevated above their 2% target for a while.

To cool rising prices, the Fed can raise the federal funds rate, which is the benchmark interest rate banks charge each other for overnight loans. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates, but lenders anticipate its decisions, so mortgage rates often move before the Fed acts. A rate hike is widely expected at the Fed's Dec. 8-9 meeting.

Even after a Fed rate hike, there’s no telling how quickly inflation will cool — or whether mortgage rates will follow. (Inflation is just one of many forces that affect mortgage rates, after all.) So if you do wait until spring to buy a house, don't do it just because you're betting on a cheaper mortgage.

If you're not in a rush to move, winter can be a good time to regroup. Fewer homes typically go up for sale from November through February, so waiting until spring could give you more options.

But new data from the National Association of Realtors suggests this year's slowdown goes beyond the usual seasonal lull. High mortgage rates and home prices are making it tough for many buyers to make the numbers work. For some buyers, waiting isn't a strategy — it's the only option.

Pending home sales, a measure of homes under contract, fell 16.7% in October compared with a year earlier, NAR reports. All four U.S. regions saw declines. At this rate, NAR chief economist Lawrence Yun warned that annual home sales could fall to their lowest level since the mid-1990s.

“Either sizable housing demand is building in the background, or the dream of homeownership is increasingly out of reach for many Americans,” Yun said in a news release.

Whether you're waiting by choice or necessity, you can use the time to strengthen your finances. Paying down debt, strengthening your credit score or saving for a bigger down payment could help you qualify for a better mortgage deal.

Every little bit helps when today’s mortgage rates are this high.

Mortgage preapprovals typically expire after 60 to 90 days. If you're already preapproved, why not keep an eye on listings this winter? (Hey, I’m an optimist. All it takes is one house.)

Think like a seller for a second. If you're listing your house in the dead of winter, you're probably not doing it just for fun. You might have a deadline, and you’re probably more willing to negotiate. In the winter, buyers have less competition — and more room to negotiate a lower price, closing cost credits or repairs. Those savings on the sticker price might help offset today's higher borrowing costs.

Spring typically brings more homes for sale, but it also brings more buyers competing for them. And if mortgage rates end up dropping this spring, you won't be the only one coming out of hibernation.

#3033. AI May Write The Code, But Real Enterprise Value Is In Understanding It

Rob Kim, CTO at Presidio, driving innovation and digital transformation through AI, cloud and next-gen infrastructure solutions.

​We’re watching a dynamic play out with AI at enterprise scale. Organizations that race ahead with AI agent deployments are discovering an uncomfortable truth: velocity without understanding isn’t transformation—it’s accumulation (AI slop). Every AI workflow shipped without a clear mental model of what it does, who owns it or how it evolves adds to a balance sheet nobody is tracking.

The following phrase from Notion design engineer Jeffrey Litt has reshaped how I think about both coding and corporate AI strategy.

“Understanding isn’t a verification step. It’s the substrate for participation.”

This reframes the entire AI conversation—both on the keyboard and in the boardroom. Verification is being automated (so stop trying to impede). What can’t be automated is the human capacity to generate new ideas, weigh tradeoffs and steer direction, and that capacity depends entirely on whether we understand the systems we’re building with (namely AI output).

In IT, we talk about technical debt constantly but rarely talk about cognitive debt—the slow erosion of a team’s mental model as code accumulates faster than our ability to comprehend it.

When developers can’t reason about their own systems, they stop being authors and become passengers. It’s the essence of vibe coding; shipping changes that work, but for reasons nobody on the team can articulate. The impact may not show up until several months after the first sprint, with a critical bug that surfaces without anyone knowing where it came from.

The immediate response is to gate everything behind review—in the form of human approval. That’s a losing strategy in the AI era because agents outproduce human reviewers by exponentially. The more productive (but challenging) response is to redesign how teams learn what their tools have built.

For decades, the default model of human participation in software has been approval:

This worked when humans wrote every line of code, because the approver generally understood what they were approving.

In the AI era, that assumption breaks. Approving output you cannot evaluate is not governance, but theater. Understanding is the only form of participation that scales: when a team genuinely comprehends what their agents are building, they won’t just “rubber-stamp it,” but instead they reshape it, steer it and teach the system what good looks like.

The human element doesn’t act as a gate of control (and slow things down) but instead is the force that guides a more accurate output—zeroshot. This approach can be simplified with the following three techniques (at least to start):

A traditional PR shows you what changed but a curriculum-style explanation shows you why it changed, what system it lives in and what mental model you need before the diff makes sense.

Picture a change that converts a 2D top-down game into an isometric view. A flat diff is unreadable. A curriculum-style explainer starts with the rendering engine, then the coordinate system, then a plain-English summary (“we’re faking 3D with 2D drawing tricks”), then an interactive widget where you drag objects around to see the effect—only then the annotated line-by-line change.

Litt built a tool that generates these documents automatically and drops them into his agentic software as collaborative pages. Quizzes at the end of each section force active recall—a technique borrowed straight from educational research. You can’t merge until you pass.

The unlock is to treat explanations as a first-class artifact, not a comment thread.

This pattern borrows from Seymour Papert, the MIT researcher who argued decades ago that children learn math best by “living in Mathland.” Now, just replace “Mathland” with the codebase, and you get the concept of microworlds: interactive simulations where developers explore code behavior rather than reading about it.

A prolog interpreter debugger lets you step through state changes manually, but a clickable simulation allows you to walk through migrating a website between frameworks, converting to playable environments vs. docs.

Microworlds collapse the gap between reading code and running code. They turn comprehension from a passive activity into an interactive one.

Most software is built by teams, but most AI-coding workflows are deeply individual.

Litt’s team inverted this. AI agents live in shared chat channels, and the explanations live in shared documents. Additionally, the coding assistants are integrated into team workspaces versus living in individual IDEs, resulting in a collective cognitive map where everyone sees the same model of the system. This allows “disagreements” to surface early instead of in production. We have applied this procedurally across our own development teams.

This isn’t an AI challenge as the technology already exists. The real challenge is shifting cultural where treating “understanding” as a team deliverable, not a personal one.

Alan Kay (pioneer of object-oriented programming) succinctly captures this argument:

“The computer is a tool to help you do what you want to do, not to tell you what you should do.”

In essence, “level humans up.” AI doesn’t have to erode that, rather, it can scaffold it. Don’t automate away understanding in trade for manual (human) control. The temptation in any productivity revolution is to mistake output for progress—heck, we did it with tokens (remember tokenmaxxing?). We can ship lines of code, merge PRs and close tickets infinitely faster with AI, but none of it matters if the team that built it can’t explain it six months from now.

​The teams (and enterprises) that win the next decade will be those who treat AI as a learning partner versus a replacement for thought.

Cognitive debt compounds, while understanding compounds faster—if you willingly invest in it.

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3031. CoreWeave vs. Nebius: Which Artificial Intelligence (AI) Stock Will Deliver Bigger Gains by 2028?

Both neocloud companies have been growing at an incredible pace, and they can sustain their impressive growth over the long run.

By Harsh Chauhan – Nov 19, 2026 at 2:30PM EST

10 stocks we like better than CoreWeave ›

Neocloud providers CoreWeave (CRWV -4.88%) and Nebius Group (NBIS -7.59%) have been growing at a phenomenal pace thanks to their business model of renting out dedicated artificial intelligence (AI) data center capacity. However, the market has been skeptical about their prospects.

This explains why both Nebius and CoreWeave have pulled back substantially over the past six months. CoreWeave stock has lost 54% of its value during this period, while Nebius is down 46%. However, the market seems to be underestimating the potential of both AI infrastructure stocks.

We will closely examine the financial performance, prospects, and valuations of both companies in this article to see how much upside they can deliver over the next couple of years.

The primary reason Nebius and CoreWeave have dropped sharply in recent months is concerns that they are taking on significant debt to build AI data centers. However, both companies are now taking smart steps to ensure that they can continue their build-out while keeping debt under control.

CoreWeave, for instance, is collecting advance payments from customers to avoid taking on unsecured debt and reduce the cost of financing. The company points out that it has "collected prepayments on 70% of our deals with customers funding up to 100% of the contract value upfront" so far in 2026.

CoreWeave is now in a position to fund the majority of its 2027 capital expenditure internally and through customer prepayments. The company believes that it will raise only "a limited amount" of capital through debt or equity.

Nebius has adopted a similar approach. The company notes that "prepayments on new contracts now cover more than 100% of the capital needed to bring this contracted capacity online." That's an improvement over the 50%-60% prepayment coverage that Nebius witnessed in the second quarter. The increase in prepayments has reduced the payback time for Nebius' capital investments by three months to 1 year and seven months.

The company says that its customers are "paying more, they pay earlier and they're committing for longer, and we are getting our capital back faster." Even better, the company expects $17 billion to $20 billion in customer prepayments next year, covering a large chunk of its estimated capex of $40 billion to $45 billion.

Additionally, both CoreWeave and Nebius are charging customers higher rents, benefiting from strong demand and tight supply of AI data center capacity. CoreWeave, for example, is charging $40 million per megawatt (MW) per year for short-term contracts, well above the $20 million to $25 million per MW per year it charges for long-term contracts.

Nebius is also charging higher prices, with the average annual price per MW increasing to more than $25 million last quarter, up from the earlier range of $20 million to $25 million. So, it is easy to see why the investment payback periods for both companies are shortening.

What's more, both companies have a solid backlog that should sustain healthy revenue growth over the next couple of years, paving the way for further upside in their stock prices.

CoreWeave is a much larger company than Nebius. CoreWeave's Q3 revenue increased by 168% year over year last quarter to $3.66 billion. What's more, the company finished the quarter with a revenue backlog of $132.5 billion, up by 138% from the prior year.

Nebius, meanwhile, reported a much stronger increase of 571% in revenue to $981 million in Q3. The important thing to note is that Nebius has received more than $12 billion in new contracts since the beginning of Q3. This explains why the company expects a 4x increase in revenue in 2027 to $12 billion to $13 billion.

In fact, both companies are poised to deliver red-hot growth through 2028.

CRWV Revenue Estimates for Current Fiscal Year data by YCharts

However, the chart above clearly tells us that Nebius is on track to grow significantly faster than CoreWeave. So, Nebius looks like the ideal growth stock to buy for the next two years, though potential investors shouldn't forget that it trades at a significant premium to CoreWeave.

The U.S. tech sector has an average price-to-sales ratio of 6.5. Assuming a similar multiple for CoreWeave at the end of 2028 points to a market cap of $292.5 billion (based on the $45 billion 2028 estimate), a potential jump of almost 11x from the current market cap. Applying a similar sales multiple to Nebius' 2028 revenue estimate suggests a market cap of $170 billion in two years, implying a 5.5x increase from current levels.

So, both Nebius and CoreWeave can become multibaggers by 2028, and investors can consider buying either of these two stocks based on their risk profile and how much valuation they are willing to pay.

#3029. Salesforce vs. Palantir Technologies: Which Software Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

Salesforce's AI agent push and Palantir's commercial surge carry very different price tags, and that gap may decide the debate.

By Robert Izquierdo – Updated Nov 19, 2026 at 2:32PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Salesforce ›

Salesforce (CRM -3.22%) and Palantir Technologies (PLTR -1.17%) represent two distinct ways to play the artificial intelligence boom. Which software provider offers the most compelling path for your portfolio over the next three years?

Salesforce provides a massive cloud platform for managing customer relationships, while Palantir builds operating systems that integrate disparate data for decision-making. Investors compare them because both are aggressively integrating generative AI into enterprise workflows to drive efficiency for global businesses.

Salesforce provides customer relationship management (CRM) tools to businesses through its Agentforce 360 platform. The company is currently focused on deploying autonomous AI agents that can handle sales and service tasks without constant human oversight for its more than 10,000 customers. In its latest annual report, it also highlighted its expansive partner network and recent acquisitions like Braze (BRZE -0.43%) to bolster its data management and AI offerings.

In the 12 months ended July 31, 2026, revenue reached $43.9 billion, which is an 11.2% increase over the previous twelve-month period. Net income for this time frame was $9.7 billion, demonstrating the company's ability to maintain high profitability while transitioning to new AI products. This growth highlights the steady adoption of cloud-based tech stocks that focus on enterprise efficiency and customer retention.

As of its January 2026 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio was 0.3x, suggesting the company maintains a conservative level of borrowing relative to its equity. The current ratio, which compares short-term assets to liabilities, was 0.8x. In the fiscal year ended Jan. 31, 2026, free cash flow reached $14.4 billion. Note that stock-based compensation (SBC) represented 23.4% of operating cash flow, which inflates reported cash generation since SBC is a non-cash expense added back in the cash flow statement.

Palantir Technologies builds software platforms that allow organizations to integrate massive amounts of data with AI models for real-time decision-making. The company serves both government agencies and commercial enterprises, recently announcing a partnership with AmeriLife Group to apply AI in the insurance distribution market. Its Artificial Intelligence Platform (AIP) is a core driver of its current strategy as more businesses look to move beyond simple chatbots toward operational AI tools.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached $7.3 billion, representing growth of 87.1% as demand for its data platforms accelerated. Net income during this period was $3.7 billion, reflecting the scalability of its infrastructure software model. This sharp increase in revenue aligns with a rapidly expanding customer base that grew to over 1,000 total customers by the middle of 2026.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio was zero, meaning the company carries no debt relative to its equity. The current ratio was 7.1x, suggesting a high level of short-term liquidity to fund its growth initiatives. In the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, free cash flow was $2.1 billion. Note that stock-based compensation represented roughly 32% of operating cash flow.

Salesforce faces intense competition in the enterprise software market from established giants like Microsoft (MSFT -1.24%) and a new wave of AI-native start-ups. The company also manages operational risks involving its AI deployments, where errors or biases could lead to regulatory scrutiny or reputational damage. Furthermore, integrating major acquisitions such as Braze or Informatica requires significant execution focus to avoid disrupting existing customer relationships.

Palantir deals with significant customer concentration, where the loss of a few major government or commercial contracts could impact overall financial performance. The company also relies on third-party cloud infrastructure from providers like Amazon (AMZN -3.56%) to deliver its services to clients. Additionally, the intensive and lengthy sales cycles common in the software infrastructure industry can make it difficult for investors to predict quarterly revenue with high precision.

Salesforce carries a lower Forward P/E, based on future earnings estimates, and P/S ratio, which measures value against sales over the past twelve months, than its peer.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

Deciding whether to invest in Salesforce or Palantir comes down to a classic trade-off between cash-flow value at a reasonable price and hyper-growth AI momentum at a steep valuation. Salesforce looks like a far better value, and in fact, its P/S and forward P/E ratios are both at low points for the past year, suggesting now is a good time to buy.

Certainly, the CRM leader's low valuation makes its stock more likely to deliver a solid return, while Palantir carries greater risk, because its elevated share price valuation means if its business does not maintain high sales growth, the stock will sink. Moreover, while Salesforce's top-line growth is not as strong as Palantir's, the CRM leader pays a dividend, yielding 0.92% as of Nov. 19, which adds to its total return.

These factors make Salesforce the better stock for conservative investors. For those with a high risk tolerance, I recommend Palantir over Salesforce. Palantir's AIP is attracting commercial customers, leading to soaring sales. The company generated $2.3 billion in third-quarter revenue, an impressive 96% year-over-year increase.

Q3 also marked the first time its U.S. commercial sales surpassed U.S. government revenue, signaling a key inflection point in its business. I believe Palantir is poised for more growth in 2027 as its commercial customers continue to expand amid strong demand for AI.

#3028. ​Your Pharma Contract Is Approved. But Is It A Competitive Deal?

Nithin Mummaneni is the founder and CEO of Infinity Loop.

A 300-page pharmaceutical outsourcing agreement can clear legal, quality, regulatory and technical review without anyone asking whether the price is competitive.

​Each department reviews the agreement through its own responsibilities. Legal looks at liability, quality reviews manufacturing obligations, and regulatory and technical teams cover their respective areas. What can get lost is the commercial picture: the rates, annual increases, payment arrangements and scope that determine what the business will actually pay.

I spent years as a management consultant running contract negotiations for Fortune 500 companies and private equity portfolios. In pharmaceuticals, I rarely met a team that had benchmarked its payment terms or escalation provisions against comparable agreements. We could provide that analysis, but the cost of the analysis could outweigh potential savings, making it difficult for companies to justify. As a result, procurement teams remain in the dark about whether an agreement is commercially competitive. A contract can be fully approved without anyone knowing whether the company is overpaying or leaving savings on the table.

The most expensive parts of a pharmaceuticals contract are usually the change-control clauses.

​The FDA recommends keeping quality agreements separate from the commercial contract, or structured so they can be separated. This keeps manufacturing responsibilities from getting tangled up with pricing, delivery and liability limits. That makes sense for compliance. It also means a quality team can approve a document that affects costs without anyone in the room reviewing the pricing implications.

​Before signing, procurement should understand which required activities are included in the quoted price, what triggers additional charges and how a change affects delivery dates and payments. Quality requirements and change-control obligations both affect what ends up on the invoice, whether or not the teams reviewing them consider that cost.

Timing matters, too. When scientists or operations teams select the supplier and set the scope first, procurement often joins after the decisions that shape the deal have already been made. By the time the contract reaches procurement, the team is left trying to improve pricing on a deal it had little opportunity to influence.

​This often happens with incumbent suppliers. There is value in staying with a provider that has already been qualified, and few pharmaceutical companies want to take on continuity risk unless they have to. Suppliers understand that and often price accordingly.

​A proven contract development and manufacturing organization (CDMO) can earn a premium. Procurement’s job is to understand how much of that premium reflects the provider’s capability and how much reflects the difficulty of switching.

Even when switching is off the table, there is still room to negotiate payment arrangements, annual increases, scope and change orders. Understanding those constraints helps procurement decide what to ask for and how to approach the conversation.

Organizations keep outsourcing more. The average organization now works with 286 third-party vendors, up 21% year over year, according to Whistic’s 2025 third-party risk management report.

​Lonza’s 2025 annual report describes continued outsourcing in biologics as demand grows and more molecules move toward commercialization. Each new relationship adds contracts, amendments and a performance history that someone needs to understand before the next renewal. Those records often sit across different functions and systems.

​Procurement managers preparing for a renewal may have the signed contract and little else. To understand what the supplier actually delivered or what has changed in the new proposal, they have to request information from other teams and compare documents manually.

​That work is slow and expensive. If the evidence only comes together after the renewal has been signed, it is too late to use it to shape that negotiation.

At one global biopharmaceutical company, my company analyzed $350.3 million in spend across its network of outsourced research and development providers. We identified $24.6 million in actionable savings, about 7% of spend. We found those opportunities by bringing contract, supplier and market data together, giving procurement a clearer picture of where it had leverage, what to ask for and how to approach the negotiation.

​Paying more for a proven partner may make sense. But the team should understand the rates it is paying and how they compare to the market before making that decision. It should be a deliberate choice.

Before the next agreement is signed, settle three things: who owns the price review, when it happens and what evidence it needs. Then bring procurement in early enough to act on what it finds. The review matters most when there’s still time to change the deal.​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?
