Anthropic shares open on the Nasdaq and soar 21% above their IPO price. Evan Schlossman, vice president of investor relations at Neostellar Capital Corp., says this is an exciting day. He speaks to Bloomberg's Scarlet Fu. (Source: Bloomberg)

#3026. Anthropic Was Worth $111.50 on Its First Day of Trading. Is That a Problem for Investors?

Anthropic opened trading today at 34% above its IPO price. A great company can still be a bad buy if you pay too much, and Warren Buffett warned about exactly that.

By Patrick Sanders – Nov 19, 2026 at 2:39PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Anthropic ›

Investors who bought Anthropic (ANT +17.65%) stock when it began trading just before noon on Nov. 19 ended up paying much more than investors who got shares in the record-setting initial public offering (IPO).

Anthropic priced its IPO at $83 per share, but the stock opened at $111.50 -- a whopping 34.3% premium. Let's put that into real dollar terms -- someone who bought 100 shares at the IPO price would have paid $8,300. But if you didn’t qualify to be one of those early investors and had placed an order to buy shares as soon as the company hit the open market, if you got that order filled, your shares would have cost $11,150.

That was an extra $2,850 for the same ownership stake.

Anthropic didn't suddenly become a better company overnight. Its financial results didn't change, and neither did its business prospects. The only thing that changed was the price that investors were willing to pay.

So did investors get carried away and buy a great company at a bad price? Already, it looks like some did. Shares began to retreat almost immediately, and by 2:15 p.m. ET, the stock was trading below $95 a share. That’s still comfortably above the IPO price, but more than 17% below the peak of near $115 it reached in its first few minutes.

Warren Buffett addressed the mis-valuation issue in Berkshire Hathaway's (BRKA -0.85%) (BRKB -0.68%) 1982 annual letter to shareholders.

"For the investor, a too-high purchase price for the stock of an excellent company can undo the effects of a subsequent decade of favorable business developments," Buffett wrote.

That's a particularly relevant observation for Anthropic.

There's plenty to like about the company behind the Claude artificial intelligence (AI) models. Anthropic reported $17 billion in revenue during the third quarter, representing extraordinary growth from a year earlier.

The company also has the financial backing of some of the biggest names in technology. Amazon (AMZN -3.56%) and Alphabet (GOOG -1.65%) (GOOGL -1.73%) are major shareholders, while Nvidia (NVDA -2.21%), Advanced Micro Devices (AMD -8.27%), and several other technology companies committed billions to its shares through private placement deals accompanying the IPO.

Those investments reflect substantial interest in Anthropic's future.

But here's something investors should understand. A company can deliver years of outstanding revenue growth without necessarily producing outstanding stock returns.

When investors pay a premium for a stock, they're effectively paying in advance for some of the company's hoped-for future success. And the higher that premium climbs, the harder it becomes for the business to deliver results that justify the price.

Warren Buffett's investing lessons from more than 40 years ago still hold true today. Image source: Getty Images.

That's the problem with buying into the excitement surrounding an IPO. Investors can end up paying tomorrow's price for today's business.

Anthropic's IPO prospectus disclosed that it had taken on spending commitments for compute infrastructure and other needs to the tune of approximately $900.9 billion over a period that extends through 2045.

That's an extraordinary amount of money, even for a company competing in the rapidly growing AI market.

Some of its biggest commitments involve its deals to lease cloud computing capacity from Amazon, Alphabet, and Microsoft. Anthropic also has arrangements involving equipment supplied by Broadcom (AVGO -1.63%), AMD, and Nvidia.

These investments could help Anthropic expand its computing capacity and serve more customers. But the scale of its expenses can't be ignored. Anthropic will need to generate enough revenue — and, eventually, cash flow — to support its spending.

Anthropic's third-quarter revenue figure signals that the company can make money, but it is still in the growth stage. And investors are assigning considerable value to growth that hasn't happened yet.

Anthropic could grow rapidly enough to justify that premium, or even more. But the early jump in stock price made the investment proposition less attractive than it was at $83. And I think the market realizes that, as shares slid to less than $100 after the first hour of trading, and less than $95 by mid-afternoon.

The lesson that Buffett imparted more than four decades ago holds true today. Anthropic might turn out to be an exceptional company, and it could dominate important parts of the AI market and generate enormous revenue for years.

But today, the market has been charging a premium for its stock — one that may take a meaningful toll on long-term investors’ returns. And that's what investors should be thinking about before chasing Anthropic shares in these early days of trading.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 790%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 202% for the S&P 500.

They just revealed what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy right now, available when you join Stock Advisor.

#3024. UGI (UGI) Q4 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Management outlines a tax-free separation into two standalone companies with distinct capital structures.

By Motley Fool Transcribing – Nov 19, 2026 at 2:39PM EST

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

UGI (UGI -8.06%) announced a plan to separate its Global LPG business into a standalone, publicly traded company. Management reported that the transaction is expected to be completed in the second half of calendar 2027 and is intended to be tax-free to the company and its shareholders. The separation will create two distinct entities: a natural gas infrastructure company focused on regulated utilities and midstream assets, and a liquid petroleum gas distribution company optimized for cash generation. The company stated that each independent business will pursue its own capital structure and dividend policy tailored to its specific operational and financial characteristics.

Operator: Good day, and welcome to the UGI Corporation Q4 2026 Earnings Conference Call. [Operator Instructions] Please note, this call is being recorded. I would now like to turn the call over to Tameka Morris, Vice President, Investor Relations and ESG. Please go ahead.

Tameka Morris: Good morning, everyone. Thank you for joining our Fiscal '26 Fourth Quarter Earnings Call. With me today are Robert Flexon, President and CEO; and Sean O'Brien, CFO. Yesterday, we announced that our Board of Directors has unanimously approved the plan to separate our Global LPG business into a standalone, publicly traded company. On today's call, we will discuss the planned separation, review our fiscal '26 financial results and key accomplishments, and share our financial outlook for fiscal '27, before concluding with a question-and-answer session. Before we begin, let me remind you that our comments today include certain forward-looking statements, including statements regarding the proposed separation of our Global LPG business.

Management believes these are reasonable, but only as of today's date. Actual results may differ significantly because of risks and uncertainties that are difficult to predict. Please read our earnings release and our annual report for an extensive list of factors that could affect results. We assume no duty to update or revise forward-looking statements to reflect events or circumstances that are different from expectations. We will also describe our business using certain non-GAAP financial measures. Reconciliations of these measures to the comparable GAAP measures are available within our presentation. And now I'll turn the call over to Robert.

Robert Flexon: Thanks, Tameka, and good morning. Yesterday, we announced a transformational step for UGI and our shareholders. Our Board of Directors has unanimously approved a plan to separate our Global LPG business into a standalone, publicly traded company, creating 2 premier companies, each a leader in its respective industry. Upon completion, Global LPG is expected to be the world's largest publicly traded LPG distributor and the New UGI will be a premier, high-growth natural gas infrastructure company built around our regulated gas utilities and complementary midstream assets. Each of these businesses has distinct strengths, growth opportunities and capital needs which may appeal to different types of investors.

We believe that operating them as 2 independent companies will allow each company to pursue its own strategy and better allow investors to recognize their full value. Importantly, this decision is the culmination of the significant work we have done over the past 2 years to strengthen the foundation of the company. Of note, we've exited our noncore European energy marketing business and divested LPG operations in noncore markets, and UGI International is now focused on core markets where it holds market-leading positions. At AmeriGas, the operational improvements have resulted in significantly improved safety and service quality, and leverage has come down from 4.9x at the end of fiscal 2025 to its lowest level in a decade.

And with the announced sale of our electric utility, the New UGI will focus on natural gas. These actions allow us to take this next step from a position of strength. The separation is intended to be tax-free to UGI and its shareholders for U.S. federal income tax purposes, with shareholders retaining their UGI shares and receiving shares of Global LPG on a pro rata basis. At its core, and as shown on Slide 6, the rationale for the separation reflects the different capital profiles of these 2 businesses. The New UGI is a capital-deploying business with a long runway of infrastructure investment ahead and regulated or contracted returns on those investments.

It will be served by a strong investment-grade balance sheet. Global LPG, on the other hand, will be a capital-return business because of its low capital intensity and significant free cash flow generation. Today, those 2 businesses compete for capital within a single portfolio. As independent companies, each will be able to establish a capital structure, capital allocation framework and dividend policy tailored to its own business, while giving investors a clearer view of the operational and financial characteristics of each company. Moving to Slide 7 and starting with the New UGI. The New UGI will include our regulated natural gas utilities: UGI Utilities and Mountaineer Gas Company, and the complementary contracted Midstream & Marketing business of UGI Energy Services.

This is a company with strong earnings base, generating approximately $470 million of net income in fiscal 2026, and having a line of sight to significant runway for growth. We expect to invest more than $5 billion between fiscal 2027 and 2031, primarily in safety, reliability and growth projects across our utility and midstream systems. That investment drives our projected 8% to 10% annual rate base growth, which in turn supports a targeted EPS growth rate of 6% to 8%. The difference between rate base and EPS growth reflects how we fund the capital plan over the long term, with a balanced mix of debt and equity that supports our credit ratings.

However, in the initial years, we expect EPS growth to track closer to rate base growth as we expect no equity issuance for 3 years following the transaction, assuming UGI retains a 19.9% stake in Global LPG and exits that stake within 12 months after the separation. The New UGI will also be positioned to offer a dividend payout ratio in line with its peers. A competitive yield, combined with strong earnings and dividend growth, creates a premier total return profile. The Global LPG business will offer a different but equally compelling investment case built on cash generation. The Global LPG business will include UGI International and AmeriGas.

The business generated approximately $740 million of EBITDA in fiscal 2026, and we are targeting competitive earnings growth and EBITDA margins between 17% to 20%. Because it is a capital-light business, a significant portion of those earnings converts to cash, and we are expecting between $250 million to $300 million of annual free cash flow in the near term with significant growth expected through fiscal 2031. With net leverage targeted at 3.25 to 3.75x, which we expect to be industry-leading, free cash flow can be returned to shareholders through dividends and buybacks. Global LPG will have an investor-focused capital return policy with a top-tier dividend complemented by share buybacks.

Ultimately, the separation creates 2 premier companies, each with a clear path to value creation, one offering strong earnings growth and an attractive dividend, and the other, compelling cash returns to its shareholders. Next, on Slide 8, I'll spend a few minutes on each company, beginning with the New UGI. With an asset base of approximately $10 billion, the New UGI is a scaled natural gas infrastructure company with a highly predictable earnings profile given the concentration of regulated and contracted margins. Our Utilities hold market-leading positions in Pennsylvania and West Virginia, 2 historically constructive jurisdictions.

Mechanisms such as the DSIC in Pennsylvania and IREP in West Virginia provide timely recovery of infrastructure investment, while weather normalization supports earnings stability and protects customers from bill volatility. At the Utilities, we have a long runway of investment ahead, including the replacement of bare steel by 2041 in Pennsylvania as well as vintage plastic, advancing our commitment to pipeline safety, reliability and modernization. Our Midstream business is highly contracted with roughly 80% of margins fee-based and a weighted average contract life of approximately 8 years as of the end of fiscal 2026. Looking ahead, we see attractive opportunities to deploy capital in this business.

As customer and peak day demand at our Utilities continue to grow, our storage, LNG and pipeline assets provide the reliable supply needed on the coldest days, and capacity beyond what our Utilities require can be sold to third parties who need dependable supply during peak demand periods. As shown on Slide 9, what differentiates the New UGI from its peers is the complementary nature of its Utility and Midstream businesses, which together form an integrated natural gas system from wellhead to customer. More than 95% of our utility supply is sourced from the low-cost Marcellus and Utica basins located within our region.

And our midstream assets gather, store and deliver that gas, including on the coldest days, through our LNG peaking assets. A good example is our Carlisle LNG storage and vaporization facility, which was placed in service this year under a long-term contract with our utility. This investment enhances reliability for our Utility customers while generating contracted returns for our Midstream business, and it demonstrates the value of this integrated footprint. The New UGI is a growth-oriented company, as highlighted on Slide 10, well positioned to capitalize on the significant energy expansion happening across our region.

Starting with our Utilities, we have a significant addressable market within our existing territory with more than 200,000 residential conversion prospects located within 150 feet of our Pennsylvania gas mains. This builds on our track record of adding roughly 12,000 new residential and commercial customers each year across Pennsylvania and West Virginia. Our pipeline replacement program continues to drive rate base growth while improving the safety and reliability of our system. And with more than 90% of capital spend recovered within 12 months under the current regulatory framework, our investments translate efficiently into earnings growth. New UGI's broader region is experiencing meaningful demand growth.

PJM expects winter peak load to grow 4% per year on average through 2036, and its last 2 capacity auctions cleared at the FERC price cap. This underscores the need for new generation in the region, much of which we expect to be natural gas fired and located near low-cost supply. With our proximity to Marcellus supply and PJM load, our system is well positioned to serve new power plants, data centers and large industrial loads. In addition, serving these large new loads can also benefit our existing customers as new demand helps spread the fixed cost of our system across a broader base.

Our natural gas platform further enhances this opportunity, creating optionality to serve that growth, such as through the conversion of the Auburn pipeline to a FERC-regulated pipeline. Moving to Global LPG, Global LPG brings together 2 market-leading platforms to create the world's largest publicly traded LPG distributor. AmeriGas is the largest retail propane distributor in the U.S. and UGI International holds the #1 position in France, Belgium, Denmark, Luxembourg and Finland. Together, the business distributed approximately 1.5 billion gallons in fiscal 2026. What sets this business apart is its ability to generate cash. In fiscal 2026, Global LPG delivered approximately $740 million of EBITDA and roughly $270 million of free cash flow. That performance is built on a lasting foundation.

A broad customer base supported by contract terms and hedging practices, which provides resilient unit margins. Strong supply security: for example, more than 90% of our fiscal 2027 LPG supply is already contracted. A long track record of operational excellence at UGI International and the transformation at AmeriGas, which continues to deliver safer, more reliable service. And a strengthening credit profile and balance sheet. Global LPG is not only a cash generator, it also has clear tangible levers for growth in both of its markets. In Europe, the heating oil market is roughly 4.5x the size of the LPG market across our footprint.

More than 130 million rural Europeans live in areas where many homes and businesses are off the gas grid and electricity often costs about 3x as much as gas. As oil boiler phaseouts take hold, LPG is the natural alternative. As an example, a typical business customer converting from heating oil can save approximately 60% per year while significantly reducing CO2 emissions. As we begin to target growth in this area, we are already seeing early wins and each conversion increases the size of the LPG market. In the U.S., approximately 9 billion gallons of propane are sold each year by roughly 3,000 retail marketers, and the top 3 distributors together hold less than 25% share.

As the largest player in a highly fragmented market with approximately 1,300 locations in 49 states, AmeriGas is uniquely positioned to serve national accounts and capture share. With the operational foundation now strengthened, the team is turning its focus to profitable growth, including emerging opportunities in data center construction and fleet conversions from diesel. All of this comes together in a business built to return cash to shareholders, as shown on Slide 13. Investors in Global LPG are expected to benefit from a strong, growing dividend with excess cash directed to share buybacks that further support dividend per share growth. A key reason we have confidence in the durability of that cash flow is our contract mix.

At both AmeriGas and UGI International, roughly half volume is index-linked, with pricing that moves with the underlying LPG benchmark. The remainder is either stated price aligned with movements in commodity costs or fixed price and fully hedged. As a result, changes in commodity prices are largely passed through, protecting unit margins and supporting consistent cash generation. And because this is a capital-light business, that cash is available to shareholders. Global LPG is expected to have an investor-focused capital return policy, with a top-tier dividend complemented by share buybacks of excess cash, giving investors a compelling combination of income and capital return. Now on Slide 14, I'll walk through the transaction details and expected timing.

We are targeting completion of the separation in the second half of calendar 2027. The transaction is subject to final approval by our Board of Directors, the effectiveness of a Form 10 registration statement with the SEC and other customary conditions. UGI expects to retain a stake of up to 19.9% in Global LPG, which we intend to exit in a tax-efficient manner. We are designing the capital structure of each company so that each stands on its own with a balance sheet matched to its business and cash flow profile.

We will communicate additional details on capital structure, capital allocation, dividend policy as well as the management teams and Board of Directors for each company as the separation planning progresses and those details are finalized. In the meantime, our focus remains on disciplined execution across each of our businesses, which brings me to our fiscal 2026 performance. Fiscal 2026 was a year of disciplined execution against the strategic priorities we laid out at the start of the year. We delivered adjusted EPS of $3, surpassing our previously revised guidance range, while continuing to reshape and strengthen the company. Sean will walk through the financial results in more detail, but I'd like to highlight a few of the accomplishments.

We delivered core earnings growth led by our Utilities, where the investments we continue to make in our system are translating into higher earnings. That growth more than offset the impact of portfolio actions and lower earnings at AmeriGas. Our financial performance combined with the debt actions we took this year also strengthen our balance sheet. Our credit ratings were reaffirmed or upgraded across all segments, and AmeriGas' balance sheet is now at its strongest point in a decade. Of importance, we continue to drive safety, operational and cultural improvements across the business, and this was a remarkable year with stronger safety metrics in every segment when compared to the prior year.

We completed our cast iron main replacement program nearly a year ahead of our commitment. That same operational focus is taking hold at AmeriGas where the transformation of our core processes continues to progress and the team's attention is now turning to driving profitable volumetric growth. Together, these accomplishments delivered a 12% total shareholder return for the year and position both companies well as we move towards the separation. Moving to Slide 17. At our Utilities, this year we achieved constructive outcomes in both our gas rate cases.

In Pennsylvania, the settlement approved by the Public Utility Commission provides a 2-step rate increase of $65 million, with $40 million effective in October 2026 and $25 million in October 2027 and a stay-out provision through January 2029. In West Virginia, Mountaineer Gas received approval for a $17 million net revenue increase effective in December 2026, with a stay-out until February 2028. These outcomes provide the revenue needed to continue investing in our system while providing substantial benefits and protections to our customers. Combined, they are expected to add approximately 350,000 dekatherms of capacity, 85% plus supported by minimum volume commitments. Beyond our core gathering business, we're also seeing real momentum around data centers. Interest remains strong with more than 100 NDAs in place. More importantly, several have progressed to engineering, construction feasibility or routing studies. We're still in the early stages, but this pipeline gives us promising new avenue for demand growth. Turning to the Global LPG business. EBIT also increased on a year-over-year basis despite the impact of divestitures and warmer weather, which speaks to the quality and resilience of this business. We are also seeing early results from organic growth initiatives, including wins in heating oil to LPG conversions, the launch of new autogas stations in France, and a 65% increase in volumes in Finland through organic market consolidation. At AmeriGas, while fiscal 2026 earnings were pressured by lower volumes from continuing customer attrition, the leading indicators continue to demonstrate a strength in business. AmeriGas achieved record safety results this year, and its safety and operational metrics continue to show significant improvement over fiscal '24 and '25.

Since reshoring our call center in March, we have achieved our lowest gross attrition in 5 years. With these improvements in place, the team is now focused on driving profitable volumetric growth, with early commercial wins, including new data center construction accounts beginning this winter representing approximately 5 million gallons annually, and a new national customer with approximately 200 cylinder exchange locations. In November, we also began operating propane-powered bobtails running on our own product instead of diesel, now using a cleaner, lower-cost and lower maintenance energy solution.

Now I'll hand the call over to Sean to walk through the financial results and fiscal 2027 guidance.

Sean O'Brien: Thanks, Robert, and good morning. For fiscal 2026, UGI delivered total reportable segments EBIT of $1.198 billion, up $22 million over the prior year, as higher Pennsylvania gas base rates and solid performance at UGI International more than offset the impact of LPG divestitures, warmer weather in some of our LPG service territories and continuing customer attrition at AmeriGas. Other operating income decreased $25 million, reflecting $14 million in higher realized losses on foreign currency exchange contracts and lower cylinder deposit income. On a weather-adjusted basis and excluding the Hawaii divestiture, retail gallons decreased 3% versus the prior year. As Robert mentioned, this year we saw the lowest gross attrition in over 5 years.

Total margin decreased $27 million, reflecting lower retail volumes sold, partially offset by higher average LPG unit margins. Operating and administrative expenses increased $9 million, reflecting increased marketing, professional service expenses, higher compensation expense and key investments to return the business to growth. Moving to the balance sheet. This year, we made strong progress against our objectives, building financial strength and flexibility. During the year, we completed several strategic debt transactions at AmeriGas, UGI International and UGI Energy Services to extend our maturity profile and reduce borrowing costs by approximately $30 million on an annualized basis. We closed the year with available liquidity of $1.8 billion and net leverage of 3.8x at UGI Corporation.

And as Robert mentioned, our credit ratings were affirmed or upgraded across all segments during the year. In October, we amended the UGI Corporation credit agreement to increase the size of our revolver and overall liquidity of the company by $225 million, further strengthening our financial flexibility. Yesterday, we announced our fiscal 2027 guidance range for adjusted diluted EPS of $2.90 to $3.20, which assumes the current business structure and normal weather. The midpoint of this range represents year-over-year growth even as we absorb the impact of divestitures, and reflects the continued strength of our core businesses.

Looking at each segment specifically, in our regulated Utilities, we expect continued earnings growth driven by rate base investment in Pennsylvania and West Virginia, including the effect of new gas base rates, partially offset by the pending sale of our electric utility. In Midstream & Marketing, we expect a similar pattern as 2026, but we view the softness in gathering volumes as a near-term dynamic that does not change our long-term view of the business. Midstream remains underpinned by highly fee-based margin and is well positioned to benefit from strong demand we see across the region and the long-term growth outlook. For UGI International, organic growth initiatives and strong margin management are expected to offset the impact of divestitures.

And at AmeriGas, we expect a return to earnings growth as the operational improvements underway translate into improved customer retention and volumetric growth. At the corporate level, we expect lower interest expense, reflecting the benefit of debt transactions completed this year, partially offset by an effective tax rate of 20% to 22%. We also expect to deploy approximately $1.1 billion of capital in fiscal 2027, roughly 80% of which will be in our natural gas businesses. And we remain committed to maintaining leverage at or below 3.75x at UGI Corporation. Taken together, this outlook reflects a business with solid momentum as we move towards the separation. And now I'll hand the call back over to Robert.

Robert Flexon: Thanks, Sean. Before we open the line for questions, I want to leave you with 3 key takeaways. First, over the past 2 years, we have fundamentally transformed this company while earning back investor confidence. We simplified our portfolio, stabilized and strengthened AmeriGas, rebuilt our balance sheet and improved safety across all of our businesses, while consistently delivering on our financial commitments. That disciplined execution has generated total shareholder return of 76% since I joined on November 1, 2024, and has established a stronger foundation from which both companies can grow, making this planned separation possible. Second, the planned separation is intended to unlock long-term shareholder value by creating 2 companies, each a leader in their respective industry.

The New UGI will be a premier natural gas infrastructure company offering attractive EPS growth of 6% to 8% and a peer-aligned dividend yield. And Global LPG is expected to be the world's largest publicly traded LPG distributor, generating resilient free cash flow to support compelling shareholder returns. Finally, our focus remains on execution as we work toward completing the separation. As you know, these are highly seasonal businesses where winter matters, and our teams are focused on safely and reliably serving our customers through the heating season. I want to thank our employees for their dedication and hard work this year. Thank you for your time with us today, and we will now open the line for questions.

Operator: [Operator Instructions] Our first question comes from Constantine Lednev with Wells Fargo.

Constantine Lednev: Appreciate the discussion this morning. As we're looking at the kind of value unlock with the spin, how are you looking at the regulated business mix on the NewCo just over the 5-year plan? #3019. Why Broadcom Stock Took a Wild Ride on Thursday

By Danny Vena, CPA – Nov 19, 2026 at 2:41PM EST

Shares of Broadcom (AVGO -1.63%) were all over the map on Thursday. After starting the day as much as 4.3% higher, the stock reversed course, down 1.5% as of 2:25 p.m. ET.

The semiconductor specialist stood to profit from a record-breaking initial public offering (IPO).

All eyes were on artificial intelligence (AI) start-up Anthropic (ANT +17.65%) when the company made its public debut today. After initially proposing a price range of $70 to $78, Anthropic priced the stock above the high end of that range at $83 per share. The company sold more than 980 million Class A shares, raising more than $107 billion after accounting for several private placements with some of the biggest names in technology (more on that in a minute).

Investors were keen to get in on the action, with the stock opening at $111.50 and climbing to nearly $115 per share, pushing its market cap briefly above $2 trillion. Then, the luster began to fade, and Anthropic gave up some of its early gains, trading at about $97 as I write this.

So what does this have to do with Broadcom? The chipmaker was one of the parties to Anthropic’s private placements. Broadcom agreed to purchase more than 13 million shares of Anthropic's Class N common stock for a cool $1 billion. When the stock spiked more than 38% in early trading, Broadcom's position was worth roughly $385 million more. However, as Anthropic's gains moderated, so too did Broadcom's stock price.

Eventually, the excitement preceding the IPO gave way to profit-taking and concerns about the broader economy and the sustainability of AI.

Those concerns aside, at less than 18 times forward earnings, Broadcom stock is worth a look.

#3016. Beyond Detecting Fakes: Assuring Identity And Verifying Authorization

Krishnaveni Palanivelu, SVP Cybersecurity Architect, Financial Services.

​Imagine a bank’s fraud team reviewing two incidents. In the first, an applicant cleared onboarding using a synthetic identity assembled from stolen personal details and fabricated supporting documents. In the second, a finance employee released a payment after hearing an AI-cloned version of a familiar executive’s voice. The executive never requested it.

Both involved convincing fakes. But they exposed different failures: accepting an identity that did not represent a real applicant and accepting instructions that did not come from the person supposedly giving them. Stronger onboarding addresses the first. It cannot, by itself, prevent the second.

Generative AI reduces the time, effort and expertise required to create believable documents, faces, voices and messages. The FBI has warned that criminals use these capabilities to expand fraud and make their approaches more convincing. The Financial Crimes Enforcement Network has also reported suspicious activity involving fraudulent identity documents used to bypass verification.

Attackers can vary their material, repeat attempts and exploit whichever channel offers the weakest challenge. Training employees to notice an unnatural pause or a distorted image may help today, but it is a fragile foundation for tomorrow’s payment controls.

Detection still matters. However, making it the deciding control leaves the organization dependent on recognizing the next forgery before accepting it. Leaders should ask: What independent evidence must exist before we establish a relationship or allow a consequential action?

Identity assurance establishes confidence that someone is who they claim to be. It connects the applicant, supporting evidence and subsequent credentials to a real identity. The next checks concern authorization and intent: Is this person permitted to act, and what evidence shows they knowingly approved this specific request?

A customer can authenticate successfully and still be manipulated into paying a criminal. An employee can recognize a voice and mistake familiarity for permission. Authentication establishes control of a credential, but it does not establish informed intent.

In my article on non-human identity, I argued for attributable actions, defined permissions and accountable ownership across machines and agents. The same discipline must govern human interactions: establish who is acting, preserve that identity through the workflow and verify authority at the point of consequence.

In banking, account opening and authorized-push-payment fraud require different interventions. Onboarding should corroborate identity evidence against trustworthy sources and examine whether the applicant, device and submitted material belong together. A payment from an established customer requires transaction context: a new beneficiary, an unusual amount or signs that someone is directing the customer through the process. Another login challenge may add little protection against persuasion.

In healthcare, identity assurance can help prevent an impostor from gaining access to a patient portal, but releasing records or changing a caregiver’s access requires a separate authorization decision. Confirming the patient’s identity does not automatically establish that another person should receive their information.

Benefits administration presents a similar split. Establishing that a claimant exists does not prove eligibility, and verifying eligibility does not authorize every later change. Redirecting a benefit payment should trigger verification tied to that change, using contact details established before the request.

Across these settings, assurance belongs at the decision where harm becomes possible.

I would start with the highest-consequence journeys: opening an account, recovering access, changing payment details, releasing sensitive records and moving money. For each, identify the evidence required, the accountable owner and the conditions that should pause execution.

Layer identity checks deliberately. Document validation, authoritative-source checks, liveness, device signals and behavioral history should contribute different evidence. Five checks derived from the same compromised document do not create five independent reasons for confidence. Liveness detection, which confirms a real, live person is present (rather than a photo, replay or synthetic video), also needs protection against injected media; a live-looking face is insufficient if the capture process itself can be bypassed, for example, by injecting a pre-recorded or AI-generated video directly into the system rather than presenting it to a real camera. NIST’s identity-proofing guidance explicitly addresses forged media and injection attacks.

Consequential actions require confirmation through a separately established channel. A voice or video call should never, by itself, authorize a payment or sensitive change. Present the actual recipient, amount and action in a trusted application, and require approval bound to those details.

Independence is essential. Calling a number supplied during the suspicious conversation simply returns the organization to the attacker. Account recovery and contact detail changes deserve equivalent scrutiny because they can undermine every later confirmation.

Even a transaction-specific approval cannot prove that someone understands a scam. Unusual payments may require a cooling-off period, a targeted warning or a conversation with a trained specialist.

I previously argued that security reviews should follow information through its lifecycle. Fraud assurance needs that perspective too.

Govern your own generative AI with equal care. Inventory customer-facing assistants and internal agents, restrict their access and prevent conversational output from becoming authority to change records or release funds. Test whether manipulated inputs can persuade an assistant to bypass verification or expose information useful for impersonation.

These controls carry costs. Additional checks can delay customers, exclude people with limited documentation and generate false positives. Provide accessible alternatives and measure abandonment and mistaken rejection alongside fraud outcomes.

As I argued in my ORF America commentary, cybersecurity affects economic security because failures undermine the services and confidence on which commerce depends.

The fraud metrics reported to the board should lead with realized fraud losses reported both before recoveries and after recoveries, measured against transaction value and broken out by fraud type. Track customer harm, recovery time and legitimate activity blocked alongside them. Detection rates remain diagnostic measures; they are not the outcome. The practical test is whether fewer fraudulent actions succeed, less money is lost and legitimate people can still get things done.​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

→ Baca artikel asli

#3015. Alibaba (BABA) Q2 2027 Earnings Call Transcript

Management reported that external cloud revenue growth continued to accelerate and reiterated long-term financial targets for the cloud segment. The company indicated that its proprietary T-Head chip architecture will serve as the primary engine for future compute capacity as it navigates evolving international export regulations. Additionally, loss reductions in quick commerce and stable core e-commerce margins contributed to a significant increase in total adjusted EBITDA.

We will discuss certain non-GAAP financial measures. Reconciliations between GAAP and non-GAAP measures are included in today's earnings press release and investor presentation. Our comments will be on year-over-year comparisons unless otherwise stated. A replay of the call will be available on our website later today.

Speaker 3: Greetings, and welcome to Alibaba Group's earnings call for the second quarter of fiscal year 2027. This quarter, Alibaba Group revenue growth accelerated, and profitability continued to improve. Group total revenue grew 10% year over year. In AI Cloud and compute services, external revenue growth accelerated to 64%. At the same time, the year-over-year growth rate of recurring revenue from external customers increased to 67%. Adjusted EBITDA margin rose to 14.5%. AI-related product revenue grew at a triple-digit rate for the 13th consecutive quarter, and its annual revenue run rate exceeded $10 billion. Profitability improved significantly across our e-commerce businesses, and in quick commerce, we continued to narrow losses substantially while maintaining market share.

The rapid improvement of AI model capability is accelerating the adoption of AI applications across industries and driving demand for training and inference. The deployment of AI agents at scale is further driving demand for compute. The combined result of these factors is that global compute demand will outstrip supply over the next several years. This profound demand is providing a solid foundation for AI commercialization at scale. Next, I'll discuss four areas in more detail: AI and cloud commercialization, T-Head AI chips, models, and AI applications, and our consumer business. First, both revenue and profit growth in AI and cloud are accelerating. This quarter, revenue from external customers in AI Cloud and compute services grew 64% year over year.

That was the 10th consecutive quarter of accelerating growth. Adjusted EBITDA increased 121% year over year, and EBITDA margin expanded to 14.5%. This performance was driven by growth across compute, storage, MaaS, and AI applications and synergies. AI-related products generated CNY 17.9 billion in quarterly revenue and accounted for 49% of Alibaba Cloud's external recurring revenue. AI is now a core revenue pillar of our cloud business. The annual revenue run rate for AI-related products exceeded $10 billion. The strong revenue growth reflects broad demand for our AI plus cloud services.

Beyond demand from AI labs for training and inference, we continue to see strong demand from customers across various industries in China, including large state-owned enterprises as well as companies in intelligent manufacturing, automotive, embodied AI, and biotechnology. Second, we continue to scale up T-Head AI chip volumes and strengthen our supply chain capabilities. Starting with chips, T-Head has established a portfolio of proprietary chips spanning the full stack of GPU, CPU, storage, and networking chips. Since launching in August, supernode instances powered by Zhenwu M890 have ramped up in volume and are now being commercialized at scale. We expect to begin mass production of the next generation Zhenwu V900 chip in the first quarter of calendar 2027.

The Zhenwu V900 delivers 3x the computing performance of the M890. Next-generation supernodes powered by the Zhenwu V900 support large-scale network interconnections, enabling workloads such as training and inference for ultra-large-scale models. These supernodes will be the cornerstone of Alibaba Cloud's AI infrastructure. Zhenwu V900 supernode clusters will launch at commercial scale in the second quarter of 2027. T-Head is accelerating its three-generation GPU roadmap. We fully rolled out Zhenwu M890 this year. We plan to ramp up the supply chain and move into large-scale production for the V900 in 2027 and the J900 in 2028. Infrastructure powered by our own domestically developed chips is becoming the foundation of Alibaba's full-stack AI capabilities.

It will increasingly support model training, inference, and agentic workloads for both Alibaba Group and Alibaba Cloud customers, supporting growth in the group's AI revenue. Qwen is already training its next-generation model on T-Head's domestic chips, and Model Studio is already providing model inference on domestic chips. Our full-stack AI infrastructure will give us a clear advantage in the domestic compute supply chain. Deep integration between our proprietary chips and models will continue to lower the cost per unit of compute and help us gain greater efficiency in AI application development. Taking all of these factors into account, our targets for calendar year 2030 remain unchanged, achieving $100 billion in external cloud revenue with a margin exceeding 20%.

Third, we continue to advance our models and AI agent applications. Our newly released foundation model, Qwen4-Max, has 2.4 trillion backbone parameters. #3012. The Inventory Accuracy Problem Smart Fulfillment Centers Still Need To Solve

Amruth Puppala | Senior Engineering Manager | Walmart.

​Walk into a modern fulfillment center, and you will see how much warehouse operations have changed. Robots move products, software coordinates thousands of orders and inventory systems track merchandise across receiving, storage and shipping.

Yet, even with these advances, a familiar problem remains.

A system shows that an item is available, but when someone goes to pick it, the product is not there. The inventory record may be correct on paper, but the physical reality is different. What seems like a minor discrepancy can quickly turn into additional searches, picking delays, order reallocation or missed shipping commitments.

This raises an important question. Instead of simply knowing how much inventory we have, shouldn’t we also know how reliable that information is?

Fulfillment centers and distribution centers handle enormous volumes of inventory movements every day. Products are received, stored, replenished, picked and shipped, often through a combination of manual processes and automated equipment.

Even with well-designed systems, discrepancies can occur.

A pallet may be placed in the wrong location. A receiving transaction might be completed before the merchandise is physically verified. Damaged inventory may not be removed from available stock immediately. Sometimes, a picking exception is recorded without the underlying inventory issue being resolved.

Warehouses already have processes to investigate these situations. Cycle counting, reconciliation and exception management are essential parts of daily operations.

However, many discrepancies are discovered only after they begin affecting order fulfillment.

By that point, the operations team is responding to a problem rather than preventing it.

One idea worth exploring is assigning a confidence score to inventory records based on the operational evidence supporting them.

Consider two storage locations, each showing 100 units available. The first location was recently verified, and its last several picking transactions were completed successfully. The second location has experienced repeated picking failures, unexplained quantity adjustments and an unresolved exception.

Both locations show the same available quantity, but they clearly do not carry the same level of operational confidence.

Today, many inventory decisions focus primarily on quantity, location and availability. Adding a confidence score could give warehouse systems another useful signal when making those decisions.

The score would not represent a new inventory balance. It would indicate how much confidence the system has in the existing record.

This could help teams identify which locations need verification and which inventory discrepancies are most likely to affect upcoming orders.

Recent advances in AI create an opportunity to evaluate these signals continuously rather than relying entirely on scheduled checks.

A system could monitor receiving transactions, inventory movements, picking results and adjustment history to identify patterns that deserve attention.

For example, repeated picking failures at the same location might suggest a discrepancy. An unusual sequence of inventory adjustments could indicate a process issue. A location that has not been physically verified for an extended period could warrant additional scrutiny, particularly if the inventory is needed for upcoming shipments.

Rather than treating each event independently, the system could examine the broader history and determine whether further verification is justified.

Newer AI models can also help interpret exception notes and other information that traditional rule-based systems may struggle to analyze consistently. Where cameras or robotic sensors are available, their observations could provide additional supporting evidence.

The important point is that AI should help identify where to investigate, not make inventory adjustments without verification.

Cycle counting is one area where this approach could have immediate practical value.

Most distribution centers already use scheduled counting programs, often based on inventory value, product movement or operational requirements. Those methods are useful and should continue.

However, a fixed schedule does not always account for problems developing during the day.

Imagine a location that reports 100 units in stock. Several picking attempts fail within a short period, but the inventory quantity remains unchanged. Instead of waiting for the next scheduled count, the system could recommend that the location be checked sooner. The priority could increase if those units are needed for orders approaching their shipping cutoff.

This would allow inventory teams to focus attention where a potential discrepancy is most likely to cause operational disruption. It could also help avoid unnecessary checks in locations where recent transactions consistently support the recorded inventory.

I would not recommend introducing another large platform to solve this problem. Most warehouses already generate much of the information needed. The opportunity is to make better use of it.

A practical starting point would be to combine inventory movement history, picking exceptions, recent count results and upcoming order demand.

Simple rules could first identify obvious discrepancies. Machine learning could then help recognize less predictable patterns, while newer language models could assist with interpreting operational notes and explaining why a location has been flagged.

The warehouse management system would remain responsible for inventory records and transactions. Any quantity correction would continue through established verification and approval processes.

For an initial rollout, I would focus on a limited number of high-volume locations and measure whether the approach reduces repeated picking failures, shortens discrepancy resolution time and improves verification productivity.

Accuracy matters, but so does the amount of additional work introduced. If the system generates too many unnecessary checks, it has not solved the underlying problem.

Fulfillment technology has made progress in improving speed, automation and visibility. But visibility alone does not guarantee accuracy.

A warehouse can have a view of its recorded inventory and still make the wrong fulfillment decision when those records do not match physical conditions.

The next opportunity is to make inventory reliability a continuous part of warehouse operations, rather than something evaluated through periodic reconciliation.

The goal is not to eliminate inventory discrepancies. That would be unrealistic in a continuously operating facility. The goal is to recognize problems earlier, direct people to locations and prevent disruptions before they affect customer orders.

#3010. Where Will SpaceX Stock Be in 2030?

The company is growing fast and has tons of opportunity.

By Jennifer Saibil – Nov 19, 2026 at 2:43PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Space Exploration Technologies ›

There may have been enormous demand for Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX -4.19%) stock when the company went public this past June, but the excitement has petered out. SpaceX stock is down 15% from its first-day closing price despite a fantastic third-quarter performance.

Is there any hope that the trend will change? Let's consider where the stock might be in 2030.

SpaceX's three segments all demonstrated outstanding performance in the third quarter, and both the Starlink satellite broadband business and the artificial intelligence (AI) business, which it now calls “super intelligence (SI),” reported operating profits, leading to the company's first quarter with a net profit.

The space business lost $7 million, but it recently achieved an important milestone when it successfully landed a Starship rocket on its return from orbit last week, catching the rocket with giant mechanical arms on its launch pad. This was the first time SpaceX had performed that maneuver with the Starship. SpaceX expects to have fully reusable rockets eventually, and plans to have Starship making two trips weekly by the second half of 2027, which will result in tremendous cost savings.

Management announced that it's developing a second Starbase in Louisiana, and it expects to have 20 launch towers and fly thousands of flights a year from there starting in 2029. It flew 165 flights in 2025, and by 2030, with thousands of flights, revenue should be much higher so long as there's demand for the flights.

The company's most established business is Starlink, the leading global satellite broadband business. It had 13.6 million customers as of the end of the third quarter, up 1.6 million from three months prior, and more than 11,000 satellites in low Earth orbit covering 160 countries.

It plans to have 100,000 communications satellites to meet growing broadband demand and offer coverage all over the world, including on airplanes and over water. It also plans to eventually have a million Starmind mini data centers in orbit, powered by the sun, to meet the world’s growing AI compute needs.

While there are big ambitions for the space business, including trips carrying 100 passengers for interplanetary travel, the biggest opportunities are in SI.

SI revenue increased 830% year over year in the third quarter, driven by new cloud agreements for compute power from its Colossus I and Colossus II data center clusters, the acquisition of AI coding company Cursor, and high use of the company's Grok AI agent.

Management has said that it sees a $26.5 trillion addressable market for AI, and with more than $7 billion in revenue in the third quarter, it already accounts for roughly half of the total.

There's uncertainty in this business, since several governors of states that are in the midst of the most intense data center build-outs have pledged to put construction of new data centers on hold while they review the impacts of those developments, and SpaceX is still far from being able to deploy solar-powered satellite data centers profitably or at scale. However, the likelihood is that AI will continue to grow and generate higher revenue. In fact, AI is already highly advanced from what it was when ChatGPT launched four years ago, and four years from now, it should be doing things people can't even imagine right now.

As for the stock, what's holding it back from further growth right now seems to be its astronomical valuation. It trades at almost 56 times trailing-12-month sales, and that ratio keeps getting lower.

I think you'd have to imagine SpaceX's growth slowing down over the next four years and its valuation getting lower. Revenue increased 161% year over year in the third quarter, and if it continues at a compound annual growth rate of 50% over the next four years, it will have $165 billion in annual sales. At a price-to-sales ratio of 23, or half of today's, its market cap would be $3.8 trillion, or just more than double today's value, in 2030.

#3007. Beyond Tokens: Why Intelligence Per Watt Is The Metric That Matters

By Dr. Steven Woo, fellow and distinguished inventor at Rambus.

​AI progress centers on tokens: tokens processed, tokens generated, tokens per second and tokens per watt. And because tokens are easy to count, they’ve become the default unit of AI performance. But token-based metrics are only part of the story and miss what users really want. A more useful metric, and one closer to the industry’s goal, is how much intelligence a system delivers per watt.​

“Intelligence Per Watt: Measuring Intelligence Efficiency Of Local AI,” published in November 2025 by a Stanford team, found that small local models can accurately answer 88.7% of single-turn chat and reasoning queries. Intelligence efficiency—capability delivered per watt—improved 5.3 times between 2023 and 2025, roughly 3.1 times from better models and 1.7 times from better hardware—factors that compound rather than add. This efficiency, not pure token throughput, will determine how well AI can keep scaling.​​

Ask a CFO, CIO or CTO running AI workloads what worries them, and it’s rarely whether they’re generating enough tokens. Although tokenmaxxing has become a metric that some companies use to measure AI engagement, others see the dangers of simply measuring token consumption independent of productivity and delivered value. Several companies are cutting back on token spending, with Uber among others stating in May 2026 that it had already spent its 2026 AI budget, and their COO adding it was becoming “harder to justify” their internal AI costs. There’s a growing call for valuemaxxing, which directs teams toward measurable outcomes based on the value being generated by these tokens.​

Concerns about measuring token usage are compounded by the fact that reasoning models don’t produce a single response per query; they work through an internal chain of intermediate tokens the user never sees. Published work on model development puts these generations in the thousands to tens of thousands of tokens. That means a query can consume far more resources than its visible output suggests.​

The pressure to continuously improve value and reduce the cost per token extends beyond inference bills. TrendForce expected server DRAM contract prices to rise 13% to 18% quarter over quarter in Q3 2026, so memory, one of the critical workhorses for AI processing, is getting more expensive as it’s being asked to do more.​​

For hardware companies, that changes the questions being asked. It stops being how many tokens a chip can generate and becomes how much bandwidth memory can deliver per dollar and per watt, and how these metrics will improve in the future.​

For decades, the semiconductor industry relied on Dennard scaling to improve power efficiency. As transistors shrank, they drew less power, so more compute arrived without a matching jump in energy use. That relationship broke down in the mid-2000s as transistors continued to shrink, but the automatic power benefit that used to come with each new process node no longer followed.​

With this lever gone, the industry had to substitute architectural changes for what used to be a by-product of process scaling. Memory is where much of that effort is going: Moving data is a major consumer of system power, making it one of the largest levers to pull.​

The case for stacking DRAM directly on top of a processor has been argued for years. The energy needed to move a bit scales with distance. A DRAM stack beside a processor sits 10 to 12 millimeters away. But stacking the DRAM on top of a processor cuts the distance to about a millimeter. Bandwidth improves separately: Memory beside the processor feeds data through the die’s edges, so interconnect scales with perimeter; stacking can move data through the whole face of a die, allowing bandwidth to scale with area.​

Stacking DRAM has become a focus topic of the semiconductor industry. At Hot Chips 2026, Samsung released more details about zHBM, which places the processor beneath the DRAM stack and claims roughly 70% lower I/O power than the previous generation. That’s what more bandwidth per dollar and per watt looks like.​

Stacking has manufacturing and engineering challenges. Although silicon conducts heat reasonably well, a stack of DRAM sitting on a hot processor acts like a blanket and prevents heat from being moved away. Samsung’s answer is thinner dies and smaller four-high stacks rather than the 12-high or 16-high configurations conventional HBM supports. The energy saved on data movement is coupled with less thermal resistance to achieve a high performance, thermally viable architecture.​

The same constraint is reshaping infrastructure above the die. Enterprise racks that once drew 20 to 30 kilowatts are heading toward hundreds of kilowatts, and eventually toward a megawatt. Power Electronics Magazine reported in January 2026 that 54 volt in-rack distribution “isn’t designed to support the megawatt-scale racks,” and that the transition to 800 volt DC begins in 2027. Copper is expensive and limits current, so when power demand climbs, voltage is the remaining variable that can rise.​

Delivering the most intelligence for a given dollar and a given watt used to be determined by two scaling trends running together: Moore’s law delivered more transistors per dollar, and Dennard scaling delivered them at lower power. Moore’s law continues, albeit at a slower pace. Dennard scaling doesn’t, so the power portion of that result now has to be engineered deliberately, architecture by architecture.​

The industry has hit walls like this before, and the solution was rarely a single breakthrough. It was a return to fundamentals: measuring the right thing and re-architecting around the real constraint instead of the metric that’s easiest to report.​

For the CFOs, CIOs and CTOs approving AI budgets, and for the hardware and software teams underneath them, the number worth tracking isn’t simply how many tokens a system produces. It’s how much usable intelligence and value these tokens return for the dollars and the watts spent producing them.

#3003. Big bets against AI debt, from CoreWeave to Oracle and the Mag 7, could soon hit the ETF market

Concerns about balance sheets and valuations in the artificial intelligence sector have hit the market hard since October, and investors who have shorted publicly traded tech companies in the AI trade have profited. Most investors don't think as much about "shorting" debt, but there will be a way through exchange-traded funds to profit from widening credit default swap spreads of top AI companies.

Both Tuttle Capital and Defiance ETFs have filed for funds that target the credit default swaps of companies closely linked to the AI trade, from CoreWeave and Oracle to the Magnificent Seven: Alphabet, Amazon, Meta Platforms, Microsoft Corporation, Nvidia, Tesla, and Apple.

Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier. CDS is a closely watched indicator of issuer health in the debt markets, especially in the AI market this year. The ETFs are designed to gain when the CDS spreads of these companies widen, either as a group in the case of a Magnificent Seven CDS fund, or individually in the case of proposed ETFs targeting Oracle, CoreWeave and Meta. The funds would lose value when the market assesses the creditworthiness of these debt issuers as improving.

Both ETF managers filed for CoreWeave and Oracle CDS funds, specifically, among broader AI and semiconductor company CDS portfolios.

If the exchange-traded fund managers get approval from the Securities and Exchange Commission, these portfolios would be the first of their kind in an ETF market that has become more aggressive in recent years about launching higher-risk strategies revolving around more complex financial instruments than just public stocks and bonds. The ETF managers plan to employ leverage in the portfolios, which both increases risk and the potential for outsized returns on any given day, similar to the way existing single-stock leverage and inverse ETFs function.

The ETF managers say these CDS funds can serve an important role in a market on edge about AI debt.

"As AI infrastructure investment accelerates and companies take on significant capital commitments to fund that growth, we see an opportunity to give investors more targeted tools to manage credit risk and hedge potential financial stress across some of the biggest names driving the AI buildout," Sylvia Jablonski, CIO of Defiance ETFs, said in a statement to CNBC.

Neocloud companies like CoreWeave are a major focus for AI skeptics, an AI bubble watch compounded by the exit of Jensen Huang from Nvidia, who is seen as a critical player in financing the AI buildout, with Nvidia called by some the "central bank of AI."

Apollo Global Management CEO Marc Rowan on Tuesday said that neocloud company AI projects could face an existential crisis if Nvidia funding dries up. "There was a strong incentive for Nvidia as a business matter to get these projects off the ground," Rowan said in an interview with CNBC's "Squawk on the Street." "If that banking function, that guarantor function, gets taken away, access to capital for a large number of neoclouds is going to disappear."

In its earnings filings and conference call on Tuesday, Nvidia shifted the tone in discussing the way it finances the AI ecosystem.

Matthew Tuttle, founder and CEO of Tuttle Capital Management, said it is less likely that these ETFs will find an audience among retail investors than smaller institutional players.

"It may be a stretch for the average retail guy," he said, but he added that having managed leveraged and inverse ETFs, it is clear that many investors "get interested" when a particular asset is seeing outsized moves, as has been the case with AI company-linked CDS in 2026. "The stuff that moves a lot is the stuff that tends to generate the most interest," he said.

Both prospectuses stress that these funds are not designed to move inversely to the reference companies' stocks and can lose money while a company's shares decline. They also note these funds don't short bonds or bet directly against a company's stock, and to buy CDS protection, they pay premiums, which are a unique cost to the fund manager whether or not credit conditions worsen.

The CDS of these companies have been on the move in a major way as part of the AI trade over the past year.

CoreWeave's 9.75% bond due in 2031 is yielding close to 16%, while spreads on its five-year credit default swaps were recently between 1,100-1,200. Spreads over 1,000 are generally considered a sign of distress.

CoreWeave executives have taken their messaging about the debt public, with co-founder and chief strategy officer Brian Venturo posting on X.com on Wednesday that he had purchased $30 million in unsecured company bonds and was going to hold them until maturity in 2031. "I've never been more confident in CoreWeave," he wrote.

Market commentators have noted since Venturo's post that he has sold over $1 billion in shares of the company's stock.

Oracle CDS contracts have moved up over the past year from below 80 to a current range above 300.

The CDS spreads on the biggest tech companies, including Amazon and Meta as examples, rose over 100% within the past year, as their free cash flow turned negative from spending heavily on AI. Even though the market is much less concerned about their balance sheets than that of the neoclouds, major tech company CDS spreads did hit five-year highs earlier this month.

Tuttle said that these ETFs, if approved, would be the "easiest" to use for smaller institutions and family offices that want CDS exposure and "are either too small to trade with the Goldmans of the world or lack the in-house expertise to trade CDS on their own."

"It is a different way to hedge the AI trade," Tuttle said.

ETF investing experts expressed skepticism about this approach and market reception. "I don't see much demand materializing for these types of products," said Nate Geraci, president of NovaDius Wealth Management. But he added it does make sense to target these ETFs "at retail investors who are seeing scary headlines around AI capex spending. Yet another way to speculate (gamble)," he said.

Todd Sohn, chief ETF strategist at Baird Strategas, said he foresees confusion with how these funds will work. "How many non-professionals know what CDS are?" he said.

Tuttle says it won't take billions in assets to make an ETF focused on AI CDS a minor financial success. "My criteria is I want to see a runway to $20 million, which is about our breakeven. I see it here. It doesn't mean it will happen, but I think we have a decent chance."

—CNBC's Michelle Fox contributed to this report

#2997. CEO turned informant: How the FDA built a case against executives promising cheap medications

The federal case against an alleged illegal prescription drug operation and its two top leaders earlier this month features a notable informant: the CEO of a peer company that authorities say was committing the same import violations.

John Thomas Howard, treasurer and director of a Canadian healthcare company called CANARX, was arrested in the tunnel connecting Detroit and Canada on Nov. 8 and charged with smuggling, conspiracy and importing a misbranded drug for patient use in the United States. His brother and the president of the company, Bob Howard, is wanted on similar charges and was still being sought as of last week, according to court records.

The charges mark the first criminal case by U.S. authorities against a company that's part of a cottage industry known as alternative funding programs, or AFPs, that promise to lower or eliminate high prescription drug costs for Americans.

The cheaper medications are typically sourced from international markets, a business model that the Food and Drug Administration and Department of Homeland Security say is illegal and can put patients' health at risk.

At issue is the unregulated nature of drugs intended for foreign markets and, in some cases, the risk of counterfeit products. And while AFPs generally argue that the importation of medication is allowable under U.S. law by individuals for personal use, authorities contend the operations go too far.

CANARX, based in Windsor, Ontario, was established in 2002 and was featured in a CNBC investigation into the booming AFP industry.

Joseph Morris, general counsel for CANARX, told CNBC last year that the business was operating legally and was confident in the safety of the prescriptions it provided. Last week, Morris told CNBC that the Howard brothers "deny all the charges against them, will defend vigorously, and expect to be vindicated in court."

The FDA's case against CANARX started with a tip about a similar healthcare operation called ScriptSourcing. It is detailed in a 35-page affidavit — dated Oct. 29 and unsealed last week — written by Dustin Dobbs, special agent with the FDA's Office of Criminal Investigations.

Based in Bonita Springs, Florida, ScriptSourcing marketed its international prescription program to self-funded employer health plans. Founder and CEO Gary Becker was described on the company's website as a "health insurance and risk management consultant for more than 30 years."

ScriptSourcing had been on the FDA's radar since at least 2022 for potential drug import violations, according to the affidavit. In August 2025, Dobbs, posing as someone interested in the AFP business, emailed ScriptSourcing and asked if the company was FDA-compliant.

A ScriptSourcing employee in responses to Dobbs said the company was FDA-compliant and that it had more than 2,000 name brand specialty medications and worked with more than 3,000 self-funded health plans.

However, that same year, the agency learned of two alleged incidents in which medications from ScriptSourcing's international drug import program had harmed patients, according to Dobbs' affidavit. One patient had been hospitalized due to a small bowel obstruction after she used misbranded Mounjaro sourced by the company via a Canadian pharmacy, and another was allegedly seriously injured after receiving a counterfeit medication.

Facing scrutiny from the FDA, ScriptSourcing CEO Becker turned on the Howard brothers of CANARX.

By May 2026, Becker had become a cooperating witness for the government in its investigation into the Canadian firm, Dobbs wrote. Becker told the FDA that he had done business with CANARX on drug importation and that the company had given him a "road map" on how to operate, according to the affidavit.

ScriptSourcing provided the FDA with a spreadsheet of financial transactions with CANARX between 2021 and 2026. It showed approximately 16,549 prescription drugs have been dispensed, the affidavit said.

Then, in several recorded phone calls between June and July 2026, Becker discussed the foreign importations with the Howard brothers.

The phone calls "show that John Howard was aware of the routes for importation of drugs from Canada into the United States and has tried to mold them to fit [CANARX's] illegal drug importation program," Dobbs wrote in the affidavit. "These phone calls also show that John Howard and Bob Howard were aware that these drugs were not allowed to enter into the United States legally."

Among the shipments en route or being prepared by CANARX at the time the information was provided was a package that contained Neupro, an FDA-regulated drug for the treatment of Parkinson's disease and restless legs syndrome.

The FDA did not respond to a request for comment from CNBC on the details in the affidavit.

Gary Becker's attorney, George Karavetsos, declined to comment when reached by CNBC, but confirmed on Monday that ScriptSourcing was no longer in business.

The AFP business model was at the center of a civil lawsuit originally filed by Gilead Sciences in December 2024 that alleged several companies, including CANARX, illegally imported its HIV drug Biktarvy. CANARX has not responded to Gilead's allegations.

Gilead said in a statement to CNBC last week in response to charges against the Howard brothers that it "welcomed the government's efforts to hold accountable those whose conduct may put patients at risk."

"Pharmaceuticals manufactured for foreign markets are almost always considered unapproved and misbranded drugs under U.S. law, whether counterfeit or even if made by the same manufacturer that produced the version sold in the United States," Dobbs wrote in his affidavit.

— CNBC's Melissa Lee and Paige Tortorelli contributed to this report.
