#2996. As Power Demand Surges, Energy Is Moving Closer To The Edge

Darren H. S. Tan. Chief Executive Officer at UNIGRID.

​Over the summer, Europe suffered a heat wave that strained its power grids, simultaneously increasing electricity demand and forcing some power plants to shut down.​

This isn’t the first time extreme temperatures have impacted the grid’s ability to deliver power. Many places around the world have experienced record-high or record-low temperatures in recent years, and the demand for heating or cooling reached levels that existing energy infrastructure wasn’t designed to handle. ​

The problem is being made worse by the construction of hyperscale AI data centers, which require massive amounts of electricity and further strain the grid. Completely rebuilding the grid to meet modern demands has proven fiscally impractical for many governments, forcing many places to solve the problem on the local level using distributed generation and storage.

Energy infrastructure across the U.S. and Europe was built over a century ago, when energy needs were much different. ​

Even so, studies have shown that we use only about 40% to 55% of the grid on average, and that’s by design, so it can handle spikes when they occur. But recent extreme weather events and data center demand are causing power needs to exceed even that generous buffer.​

For example, the Electric Reliability Council of Texas (ERCOT), which manages electricity in Texas, has said its interconnection queue currently includes over 1,800 projects totaling 474 gigawatts of power, over five times the state’s record peak electricity demand. Ninety percent of those requests come from data centers. ​

The cost to completely rebuild the grid to meet today’s needs is astronomical, with some estimates putting the total replacement cost in the trillions. Because of the potential cost and the fact that the aging grid is a chronic rather than an urgent problem, it receives patchwork upgrades as needed and as government budgets allow. ​

With a full rebuild out of the picture, several strategies are in play to work within the system we already have. When demand approaches available supply, energy companies offer rebates for reducing usage at peak times, implement peak pricing or, in more extreme cases, resort to rolling blackouts, which are planned outages to alleviate the demand during peak periods. ​

Beyond the grid itself, some areas are reducing their dependence on the centralized infrastructure by moving toward local power generation and storage.

Battery energy storage systems (BESS) can store electricity and discharge it when demand exceeds the grid’s capacity. ​

They are already showing promise in several states, such as California, where battery storage capacity has reached over 21,000 MW, equal to about one-third of the state’s peak demand. Eighty percent of that capacity is represented by grid-connected batteries, but smaller batteries are also enabling residents to increase their own energy storage capacity at home.​

As these batteries become cheaper and more reliable, it’s not out of the realm of possibility that these energy storage systems become a standard household appliance in the future, much like a refrigerator or washing machine is today. They can also play a role in energy needs for small businesses.

Being able to store energy and use it in an emergency is only half the equation. The other half is being able to generate electricity independently of the grid. ​

This isn’t a concept that should be unfamiliar to many, as many organizations and homes already have backup generators or solar panels. Although these methods can keep small businesses and homes powered up, they have their disadvantages. Generators are noisy, pollutive and can be dangerous, while solar has significant upfront installation costs. ​

Plug-in solar is emerging as another option for generating electricity locally that has already seen success in Europe and is starting to gain support in the U.S. as well. Instead of being installed on a rooftop, these smaller panels can be mounted on a balcony or a small section of a yard or business property and plugged into a wall outlet to help power homes and small businesses. ​

These systems cost significantly less than traditional solar and are portable, so people can move them around wherever they’re needed. These benefits open up power generation to a whole new segment of the country: commercial and residential renters alike who might never invest in a hardwired battery system or solar installation.

As energy demand continues to increase, expanding traditional grid infrastructure will remain important, but those projects can be expensive and take years to complete. Distributed generation and storage offer another way to increase resilience in the meantime. ​

By generating and storing more electricity closer to where it will be consumed, communities and business leaders can help reduce their reliance on the centralized grid during peak demand.​

We’re unlikely to solve growing power constraints with any single technology, but as local generation and storage become cheaper and more accessible, they can play a larger role in managing peak demand and making better use of the grid infrastructure we already have.​

#2995. Why PDD Holdings Stock Jumped Today

Shares of the Pinduoduo parent gained on earnings even as revenue was flat.

By Jeremy Bowman – Nov 19, 2026 at 2:53PM EST

10 stocks we like better than PDD Holdings ›

Shares of PDD Holdings (PDD +10.61%), the parent of Chinese e-commerce companies Pinduoduo and Temu, were soaring today after it delivered better-than-expected results in its third-quarter earnings report on Thursday.

The stock was up 11.3% as of 2:06 p.m. ET.

Revenue in the quarter was flat at $16.2 billion, but that actually missed estimates at $17.3 billion.

Transaction revenues fell 12% to $7.2 billion, while revenue from online marketing, including search advertising, rose 12% to $8.9 billion, showing it's doing a better job at monetizing its platform, even as sales are falling.

PDD also cut costs in the quarter, with sales and marketing expenses falling 17% to $3.8 billion and general and administrative expenses down 11% to $236 million, helping boost its operating profit by 25% to $4.6 billion.

The company earned less investment income and faced higher taxes, and as a result, adjusted earnings per share were flat at $3.14, though that topped estimates at $2.71.

CEO Jiazhen Zhao said, "During the past quarter, we continued to deepen our support for the merchant ecosystem, helping merchants develop better products and reach new consumers. We see significant potential to build on this progress and will continue investing in the tools and services that help our merchants grow."

Earlier in its history, PDD regularly reported triple-digit growth, driven by PDD's social commerce design and then Temu's rapid international growth.

However, more recently, the company has struggled as Temu's user base has shrunk due in part to weak consumer sentiment at home and tighter regulations abroad.

While the profit growth through cost-cutting was encouraging, the company will need to return to growth for the stock to keep gaining.

#2990. First Nvidia, Now AMD: A Short Seller Just Put Its China Business Under the Microscope

A short report claims AMD’s firmware keeps turning up in updates from a blacklisted Chinese server maker, and the stock is falling.

By Johnny Rice – Nov 19, 2026 at 2:55PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Advanced Micro Devices ›

Advanced Micro Devices (AMD -8.27%) shares were down about 9% as of 1:48 p.m. ET on Nov. 19 after Hunterbrook Media published an investigation alleging that AMD's software keeps turning up in servers made by a blacklisted Chinese company.

The short report lands as the market is still reeling from Jensen Huang's resignation as Nvidia CEO in the wake of reports he was romantically involved with a suspected Chinese intelligence asset, and on the same day new U.S. rules tightening advanced chip exports take effect. So, what should investors take from this?

While the allegations are narrower than some of the headlines make it seem, I think they're another sign that the AI trade has more cracks than bulls would like to admit.

The report centers on a Chinese server maker named IEIT (SZSE: 000977). Hunterbrook says the company was added in March 2025 to the Commerce Department's Entity List -- the list of foreign companies that U.S. firms cannot export to without a special license -- and that since then, IEIT has built at least six newer versions of AMD's firmware -- the base layer software that allows chips to run inside a server -- into its own updates. The report also alleges that three separate server companies that aren't on the Entity List post firmware that is more than 99% identical to IEIT's.

To be clear, Hunterbrook says it doesn't know how IEIT got the code or if anyone held a license and that its records don't directly establish any export violation, and AMD has firmly denied any wrongdoing, telling Bloomberg that the report misrepresents its business and that "AMD complies with applicable US export control laws and regulations."

Software might sound minor next to shipping actual chips, but Hunterbrook argues that updates count as exports. The report points to a settlement last month in which Commerce's Bureau of Industry and Security (BIS) counted each of seven software updates sent after a listing as a violation. And Washington is obviously watching this stuff very closely at the moment.

By Hunterbrook's count, AMD billed $7.75 billion to customers in China and Hong Kong in fiscal 2025 -- about 22% of its $34.6 billion in revenue -- and another $4.28 billion to Singapore, one of five countries where the BIS rule that took effect today requires licenses for advanced chips. Those numbers are pulled directly from AMD's own filings, so they're no secret.

However, Hunterbrook seems to believe some material portion of this could be at risk, and that perhaps the software updates, while potentially violations in and of themselves, suggest AMD chips were still flowing to IEIT even after it was added to Commerce's blacklist.

To be very clear, these are allegations from the media wing of a fund that is short the stock. It stands to gain financially from AMD shares falling. That's a real conflict of interest.

A short report should always be taken with a heavy grain of salt. That said, they shouldn't be dismissed either. A short-seller has an incentive for their research to be taken seriously, and therefore, an incentive to tell the truth.

#2986. Alibaba: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Alibaba is narrowing quick commerce losses on a 2029 profitability target, a timeline that will test how durable its core e-commerce margins really are.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 19, 2026 at 2:56PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Alibaba Group ›

On its second quarter of fiscal year 2027 earnings call, held on Nov. 19, 2026, Alibaba (BABA -0.07%) announced a 10% year-over-year increase in total quarterly revenue. The business is shifting capital into proprietary artificial intelligence hardware to build long-term operational independence. Three things management said stand out.

Alibaba is scaling T-Head, its semiconductor design unit, to power its cloud infrastructure and reduce reliance on overseas graphics processing units. Shifting to domestic hardware protects computing capacity and helps control underlying server costs over time.

Management is trimming losses in quick commerce, its fast-delivery retail service, through higher order sizes and improved operational efficiency. Narrowing these losses strengthens core e-commerce profit margins without sacrificing market presence.

Proprietary processor production lowers the cost per unit of compute across Alibaba Cloud, the company's enterprise digital infrastructure division. Tight market supply for server processing power gives the business strong pricing leverage with corporate clients.

Enterprise computing demand is exceeding industry supply, and Alibaba is accelerating proprietary chip production to capture cloud workloads. Expanding chip output protects server capacity and strengthens margins, while management maintains focus on capital efficiency.

Integrating custom hardware with foundation software models builds a durable competitive moat across domestic and international cloud markets.

#2978. Palo Alto inks $4.5 billion deal for AI startup Applied Compute, shares pop 7% on earnings

Palo Alto Networks shares jumped more than 7% after the cybersecurity firm beat Wall Street's fiscal first-quarter estimates and inked a deal for artificial intelligence startup Applied Compute.

Here's how the company did versus LSEG estimates:

Revenue jumped 39% from $2.47 billion in the year-ago quarter.

Fears of AI-powered cyberattacks, heightened by incidents like the Hugging Face breach, are driving demand for cybersecurity vendors like Palo Alto Networks. Amid this backdrop, the company's shares have jumped 85% this year.

"As AI reshapes the threat landscape, we must continually evolve to protect our customers and enable them to adopt AI securely," CEO Nikesh Arora said in a release.

Palo Alto said annual recurring revenue for its next-generation security business jumped 66% from a year ago to $9.73 billion. Arora said the NGS ARR was one of the highest beats in the company's history.

The cybersecurity company bumped up guidance for fiscal year 2027 as businesses spend more on security in the age of AI.

Palo Alto expects adjusted earnings per share in the range of $4.26 to $4.30 and revenue in the range of $14.52 billion to $14.57 billion. The forecasts topped the earnings of $4.18 per share and $14.16 billion in revenue expected by analysts.

Net income totaled $482 million, or 57 cents per share, up from $334 million, or 47 cents per share, a year ago.

Palo Alto Networks has been steadily acquiring companies to capitalize on demand for cyber tools in the age of AI.

Arora said the company's $4.5 billion all-stock deal for Applied Compute will help businesses secure open-weight models as adoption grows. The startup helps businesses run and customize their own AI models and marks a major investment for Palo Alto beyond cybersecurity.

"If it works, I think this will dwarf the ability that we have in cybersecurity," Arora said on an earnings call. "This could be bigger than what we do."

Outages from Microsoft, banks and air traffic control have heightened concerns around cyberattacks over the last week. U.S. officials have so far ruled out any malicious activity, but experts say the rapid acceleration in patching is straining dated systems.

As businesses discover more vulnerabilities, Arora said the company expects tens of thousands of virtual patches by the end of the fiscal year. "Whatever they do today is going to take three to six months, maybe nine months, depending on how complex and how tired their environment is," Arora said.

#2977. Maximus (MMS) Q4 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Fiscal 2027 guidance assumes zero VA contract performance incentives.

By Motley Fool Transcribing – Nov 19, 2026 at 2:57PM EST

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Maximus (MMS -5.21%) reported fiscal year 2026 results that met revenue targets and exceeded adjusted earnings expectations, driven by technology-enabled productivity and clinical volume surges. Management noted that adjusted earnings per share of $8.24 reflected solid execution and disciplined capital allocation, including $192 million in share repurchases. The company closed the acquisition of Agile Defense to accelerate its presence in the national security market, which management expects to account for approximately 10% of total revenue. Fiscal year 2027 guidance assumes zero contribution from performance incentives on the Veterans Affairs Medical Disability Examination contract while projecting organic growth in state-level services driven by healthcare policy requirements.

Operator: Greetings, and welcome to the Maximus Fiscal 2026 fourth quarter and year-end earnings conference call. At this time, all participants are on a listen-only mode. A brief question-and-answer session will follow the formal presentation. If anyone should require operator assistance during the conference, please press star zero on your telephone keypad. As a reminder, this conference is being recorded. It is now my pleasure to introduce your host, James Francis, VP of Investor Relations. Thank you. Please go ahead.

James Francis: Good morning, and thanks for joining us. With me today is Bruce Caswell, President and CEO, and David Mutryn, CFO. I would like to remind everyone that a number of statements being made today will be forward-looking in nature. Please remember that such statements are only predictions. Actual events and results may differ materially as a result of risks we face, including those discussed in Item 1A of our most recent Forms 10-Q and 10-K. We encourage you to review the information contained in our recent filings with the SEC and our earnings press release. The company does not assume any obligation to revise or update these forward-looking statements to reflect subsequent events or circumstances, except required by law.

Today's presentation also contains non-GAAP financial information. For a reconciliation of the non-GAAP measures presented, please see the company's most recent Forms 10-Q and 10-K. With that, I will hand the call over to Bruce.

Bruce Caswell: Thanks, James, and good morning. Fiscal year 2026 reflected the benefits of disciplined operations, technology-enabled productivity enhancement, and thoughtful capital allocation, all of which constitute meaningful progress against the strategic priorities that we have discussed with investors over the last several years. A couple financial metrics showcase this. Adjusted EBITDA margin expanded to 14.2% this fiscal year, compared to 12.9% in fiscal year 2025. Adjusted earnings per share increased to $8.24 from $7.36 a year ago. These results are driven by investments in automation and AI-enabled solutions, combined with operational discipline that come together to generate value for both our customers and shareholders. We are often asked how to measure or demonstrate the benefit of these investments.

Consider that at the beginning of fiscal year 2026, our margin outlook contemplated approximately 13.7% performance. As the year progressed, the benefits of technology-enabled productivity and disciplined execution supported delivery of the 14.2% adjusted EBITDA margin for the full year. That improvement, achieved within a single fiscal year and despite the pause in VA MDE incentives in the fourth quarter, shows the scalability of our model and the operating leverage that can be created through thoughtful and well-executed technology investments. David will also explain the portion of our fourth quarter results that came from higher clinical volumes, which we expect to moderate.

I will also note that we returned $192 million of capital to shareholders through share repurchases while continuing to invest in capabilities and capacity, both of which we believe position Maximus for future growth. As we have now entered fiscal year 2027, I am excited to share our four main priorities that are already underway. First, accelerating our expansion in defense and national security through our combination with Agile Defense. Second, continuing our strong performance in defending and growing existing customer relationships. Third, executing against the opportunities that have emerged and are still developing from HR-1, also known as the OBBBA, and related policy initiatives.

Fourth, further advancing our AI-enabled solutions and technology innovation, which we will continue to showcase to our government customers. Beginning with Agile Defense, as noted in our earnings press release earlier today, I am pleased to announce that the acquisition has closed and the integration is underway. We have previously shared our belief that Maximus would benefit by establishing a greater position within the defense and national security market. We view this market as attractive due to its size, stability, strategic importance, and long-term growth characteristics. Defense-related spending represents approximately half of the discretionary federal spending, and we believe the demand environment remains favorable as governments increasingly focus on modernization, digital transformation, intelligence capabilities, and national security priorities.

We believe the acquisition of Agile accelerates effective execution of this strategy by at least several years, compared to a solely organic alternative. What attracted us specifically to Agile was not simply its growth profile, but the combination of capabilities, customer access, and culture. We viewed Agile as being the ideal size, meaning large enough to create meaningful strategic value while remaining highly manageable from an integration perspective. Agile brings differentiated customer relationships, strong technical talent, established past performance credentials, and a proven track record of growth within priority national security markets. As evidence, Agile delivered year-to-date book-to-bill performance of approximately 3.1x, reinforcing our confidence in both the quality of the acquisition and the demand environment supporting the business.

We expect that momentum to support double-digit organic growth for Agile in calendar year 2027 on revenue of roughly $450 million. Importantly, Agile helps us progressively grow in new attractive areas and over time will reduce concentration in our portfolio. Prior to the acquisition, our existing defense and national security business represented less than $100 million of revenue. With Agile, defense and national security represents close to 10% of the total company's revenue, providing substantial opportunity for future expansion, which we believe should be enabled by our estimated addressable market for government services within the Department of War, totaling $58 billion by 2029.

As I noted in the announcement call, we believe that Agile Defense is particularly well-positioned within technology services, such as IT and modernization efforts that incorporate AI, automation, cloud, and advanced analytics, which happens to be the fastest-growing portion of the defense and national security space, and comprises about $21 billion of this addressable market. One of the most compelling cases for acquiring Agile is the success of Agile Labs and its ability to engage customers through emerging and non-traditional procurement models. Increasingly, the Department of War is looking to access innovation through approaches such as prototyping, experimentation, and other transaction authorities, or OTAs, rather than relying exclusively on traditional procurement processes.

The high-cadence operating model of Agile Labs enables rapid transition of innovation into working solutions in operational environments. From there, we believe these capabilities complement the investments Maximus has already made in areas such as our corporate venture capital portfolio, AI enablement, technology acceleration, and customer-centered innovation, including our hackathon-as-a-service model. In summary, we strongly believe that combined with Maximus, Agile Defense will be capable of greater growth and scale than as a standalone company. Just as Maximus benefits from the capabilities, customer access, and culture of Agile Defense, so too does Agile benefit from the deep technical bench, commitment to innovation, earned reputation and brand, and resources of Maximus.

As I mentioned earlier, we repurchased $192 million of shares in fiscal year 2026. At the same time, we capitalized on an opportunity that is expected to accelerate our long-term strategy through an acquisition that expands customer access and creates growth opportunities in a priority market. While the mathematics of repurchases remain attractive, we believe the combination of share repurchases and strategic M&A represents the best path forward for long-term shareholder value creation. Even following the transaction, we expect leverage to remain within a disciplined range and supportive of future capital deployment flexibility, including our anticipated $200 million of share repurchases in fiscal year 2027.

Most importantly, we view Agile as a growth story, with the opportunity for further revenue synergies to develop. We believe the combination positions Maximus to pursue a broader set of opportunities, participate more effectively in evolving procurement models, and strengthen our relevance in one of the most important government markets. Our second priority is successfully defending our existing portfolio and driving on-contract growth by delivering exceptional outcomes for our customers. We believe that our portfolio is notable for its multi-year contracts with customers, where our relationships often last decades. Our recompete performance continues to reflect the strength of our customer relationships, operational execution, and the deployment of effective technology.

During fiscal year 2026, our recompete win rate was approximately 89%, consistent with our multi-year historical performance. Let me highlight some notable wins. In the fourth quarter, Maximus re-won a significant healthcare-related state contract representing more than $800 million of total contract value over 10 years. Not only were we successful in retaining the work, but the renewal pricing increased compared to the previous contract, demonstrating that this customer recognizes the value, quality, and outcomes we deliver to them. We also recently secured two additional important state contract renewals totaling $528 million that were included in our unsigned award balance at fiscal year end. Those two wins reinforce what we believe is a consistent theme throughout the portfolio.

Customers continue to value experienced partners that can combine operational execution with technology-enabled service delivery. Turning to the VA Medical Disability Examination, or MDE program, we previewed our views during the Agile Defense transaction announcement when we had a draft solicitation. Since then, a formal solicitation has been released with a submission due date in mid-December, showing a purposeful pace by the VA. We expect a six-month extension of our current work, with the new contract beginning July 1st, 2027. The solicitation covers a five-year period rather than the 10 years we had assumed, which I'll come back to in our pipeline discussion.

The elements of the draft solicitation we disclosed have carried over, and we continue to view the requirements favorably. Importantly, there's a continued emphasis on performance-based contracting. The structure appears designed to reward contractors that consistently deliver high-quality services, meet performance standards, and invest in the capacity needed to support veterans effectively. Over time, we believe this should provide an opportunity for higher-performing vendors to earn a larger share of volumes within their service groups. We believe that approach aligns well with the Maximus operating model, our track record of performance-based delivery, and our longstanding commitment to quality and customer outcomes.

Ultimately, our strategy for ongoing recompete success is straightforward: deliver exceptional outcomes for customers, earn fair returns for the value of services we provide, and look for opportunities for our customers to utilize our scaled platforms. Our third priority is executing against the opportunities associated with the OBBBA and the growing emphasis on program integrity across government. As we enter fiscal year 2027, we continue to view our progress favorably and recognize there is more opportunity if the right factors come together. I'll preview David's remarks by noting the U.S.

Services segment assumes mid-single-digit organic growth for fiscal year 2027, supported by work related to the legislation. Diving into Medicaid, five states thus far have executed contractual arrangements with us intended to support beneficiaries with community engagement, also known as work requirements, and semi-annual eligibility redetermination. Numerous additional states remain actively engaged in planning and evaluation efforts. As we've discussed previously, implementation decisions vary meaningfully by state. Factors such as approaches to medically frail populations, operational design decisions, and legislative considerations continue to influence timing and scope.

Most recently, on November 13th, CMS announced that states will have the option to suppress for six months the adverse actions, meaning denials and dis-enrollments, that may result from their application of work requirements to their Medicaid population beginning January 1st, 2027. The increased activity we forecast under our contracts remains unchanged, as it relates to OBBBA requirements that are not suspended. Specifically, states must continue to process new applications and renewals, determine eligibility, assess compliance with work requirements, and handle exemption requests. States have until December 11th to opt in, and we'll support our customers as they consider this new option. In short, we believe the underlying demand drivers remain intact.

States continue to require solutions supporting beneficiary engagement, exemption determination, compliance monitoring, eligibility administration, and program integrity. Regardless of individual implementation approaches, these requirements create operational needs that we believe align well with Maximus' experience and capabilities. In SNAP, the environment continues to be highly active with system and operational planning by states moving progressively toward implementation. Greater urgency is being felt now that states are responsible for 75% of the administrative costs of SNAP. Our sales teams continue participating in discussions and demos intended to help current and hopefully future customers as they evaluate strategies to reduce error rates, improve program integrity, address fraud concerns, and strengthen overall program administration.

Increasingly, our discussions with customers are extending beyond individual program solutions and toward integrated approaches that support multiple benefit programs simultaneously. This is an area where Maximus possesses significant operational experience and proven capabilities through our existing state contracts. More broadly, we continue to believe that program integrity will remain an enduring bipartisan priority. Whether the focus is fraud prevention, payment accuracy, eligibility verification, or operational efficiency, governments increasingly seek technology-enabled solutions that promote program integrity while maintaining positive beneficiary experiences. Our fourth priority is accelerating the adoption of AI-enabled solutions while continuing to expand the innovation ecosystem that supports our long-term growth strategy.

Over the last several years, we've consistently discussed AI as an enabler of better outcomes, stronger program performance, and improved experiences for both customers and beneficiaries. Fiscal year 2026 provided further evidence supporting that thesis. What we've learned is that government customers remain appropriately cautious regarding AI adoption. However, that caution tends to diminish considerably when solutions can demonstrate measurable improvements to quality, program integrity, customer experience, speed, or cost-effectiveness. Increasingly, the conversation is no longer about whether AI should be deployed, but how it can be deployed responsibly and effectively. Within Maximus, adoption continues to expand across a broad range of use cases.

We're applying AI to customer interactions, training, quality assurance, workforce enablement, automation, software development, and operational support functions. These deployments are generating valuable experience that helps us better support our customers pursuing similar objectives. We refer to this as being customer zero for our AI solutions. An important complement to this strategy is Maximus Ventures, our corporate venture capital platform. As we've discussed previously, the objective of Maximus Ventures is to identify emerging technologies that can help create differentiation in our bids, enhance existing contracts, and improve customer outcomes. We are increasingly seeing those investments move beyond evaluation and into operational deployment.

Today, one portfolio company solution is already in production, and we've approximately a dozen pilot initiatives underway across the enterprise. These include applications designed to improve onboarding, workforce training, and customer service representative performance through AI-enabled tools. We're also leveraging investments such as Spectro Cloud to strengthen our capabilities related to AI infrastructure governance, operational control, and technology management. As government customers move from experimentation to scale deployment, we believe governance, security, transparency, and responsible adoption will become increasingly important differentiators. Taken together, our combination of operational expertise, proprietary data, technology investments, venture partnerships, and real-world deployment experience positions Maximus well as customers increasingly seek trusted partners for AI-enabled transformation.

Put simply, we continue to lead our own transformation and partner with our customers to demonstrate the evolving potential of AI. Healthcare costs was obviously a major issue in the election, and many recognize that significant changes, however, would take historic legislation. Something like Medicare for All would require historic legislation.

We are monitoring to see how Congress looks at this and whether they might seek to kind of take a very pragmatic approach that leverages existing programs to address the challenging topic of healthcare access and cost, that was clearly part of the election narrative, but do so in a way that builds on existing programs. So we feel like we are very well-positioned to support those types of initiatives. At the state level, as I commented in my prepared remarks, the OBBBA does remain law, and states are progressing with the implementation. This is happening in an environment where final guidelines and rules are still being clarified.

An example that we point to is the medical frailty exemption, which there was a court ruling on just recently, and that applies to work requirements. There may be further adjustments in terms of the law's provisions in the coming years, but the underlying requirements very much remain. The question is, where are the growth vectors at the state level, and how might the midterms affect those? From an opportunity standpoint, I do think that there is a real focus on program integrity going forward, and it is hard to argue about the importance of program integrity.

It is worth noting that in addition to the SNAP payment error rate measure, which we have discussed it in some detail, that where states have to have their payment error rates above 6% or they face consequences in terms of SNAP funding beginning in fiscal year 2028. It is important to note that just down the line, beginning in fiscal 2030, that would be October of 2029, CMS will no longer be allowed to waive disallowances to Medicaid for states that have error rates in Medicaid that are above 3%. That is way out on the horizon. We really have not talked about it much before.

The point being, this issue of program integrity, it spans multiple program areas, whether it is SNAP or Medicaid or other types of benefit programs. We think that is something that is bipartisan in nature and something that can be focused on. Certainly, it is an area where there is no question that the evolving technology like AI can make much better decision support systems possible for states as they work through these things. Another opportunity area at the state level, and we will see what happens with this, is that there are four states, Georgia, Kansas, Texas, and Wisconsin, that are now looking at potential healthcare legislation.

Could that lead to Medicaid expansion in one or more of those states? Potentially. We will stand by and watch how that goes. There are states that have expanded Medicaid, and we support many of them in that regard. Newly elected governors are looking to modernize agency operations. That has been a theme. That does fit into the program integrity area, but also, we have seen a lot of emphasis this past week, obviously, where there have been various outages of systems, whether they are commercial or government in nature, that reflect antiquated technology, technical debt that is built up. That is going to need to be addressed.

We think we are very well positioned to support those efforts of the new governors out there as we go forward. Long answer to your question, but like I said, I would summarize it by saying we think the tailwinds likely outweigh the possible headwinds here.

Charlie Strauzer: That is great. Thanks, Bruce, and thanks for taking my questions.

#2974. Can $300 a Month in VOO Really Build a $1 Million Portfolio? Yes, But There's 1 Factor to Consider.

Even small investments can build substantial wealth over time.

By David Dierking – Nov 19, 2026 at 3:00PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Vanguard S&P 500 ETF ›

A $1 million portfolio sounds like something that only high earners have. The good news is that it's something anyone can have. You just need consistent investing and the necessary amount of time to get there.

Time is the most important factor. It's even more important than the rate of return you see on your investment (although that's important too). The more years you have to invest, the greater the likelihood that you'll hit the million-dollar mark.

What does the path look like? Can most people stay on it?

The answer to this is really just a math problem. If you know you're investing $300 a month and your end goal is $1 million, you need to figure out an estimated average annual return over the life of the investment. With that, you can figure out how many years it takes to get there. Let's use the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO -1.59%), because it's the largest and one of the cheapest exchange-traded funds offering exposure to the index.

The long-term average annual return for the S&P 500 is 10%. We have no way of knowing for sure, but let's say the index keeps returning that. At that rate, the combination of contributions and compounded growth gets you to $1 million in just under 34 years.

Here's a larger table of the path to $1 million:

As you can see from the table, not much growth happens in the first 10 years or so. It's not until around year 25 that your account balance really starts to take off.

That can be the frustrating part of investing when you're just starting out. Most of your balance is your own contributions. There's very little growth to be had. It's not until you've been consistently doing it for decades that you start to really see results.

That's why it's so important to stick with it. It takes discipline, and only those who invest consistently throughout their entire lives really get to see the long-term results. But once you get there, you'll see quickly how even small, inconsequential investments can grow into substantial wealth over time.

#2973. Data center electricity use could double by 2030, IEA says

Global electricity use by data centers could double by 2030 and stretch power grids, potentially delaying new projects, the International Energy Agency warned.

Data centers could consume around 1,000 terawatt-hours of electricity a year by 2030, according to the IEA, which added that tight supply chains, local opposition and limited grid capacity could slow construction.

The warning is part of the IEA's Stated Policies Scenario, in which the agency forecasts are based on measures that have been put forward but have not yet been adopted.

The IEA cites large data center project pipelines in Texas, Virginia, Finland and the U.K. In several regions, the combined capacity of data centers waiting to connect exceeds current peak demand.

Without grid upgrades, power networks could become more congested and less reliable, the IEA said.

New projects could face longer waits to connect, while generators could be forced to cut output when the grid cannot meet demand.

Peak electricity demand is projected to rise 40% to 50% by 2035, according to the IEA. Under the Stated Policies Scenario, grids should be expanded by 26 million kilometers of new lines by 2035, while about 16 million kilometers of existing lines need to be replaced or refurbished. Supplies of copper, cables and transformers are already tight, the IEA said.

Under the Current Policies Scenario, which is based on energy policies and regulations already in place, grid spending is projected to rise 75% over the next decade. The agency says power networks are "one of the biggest energy investment stories of the next decade."

The scenario projects that renewable power generation will grow 85% more in the next decade than in the previous one. Solar photovoltaics would become the world's largest source of electricity from the late 2030s

Electricity would account for nearly 30% of global final energy consumption by 2035, up from 23% in the IEA's main scenarios.

The IEA said the 2026 energy crisis could accelerate electrification as countries seek to reduce their reliance on imported oil and gas. Electrification can reduce exposure to imported fuels, but it also makes a reliable power grid more important.

Before the Iran war, an average of 20 million barrels of crude oil and oil products a day transited the Strait of Hormuz in 2025 — about 25% of global seaborne oil trade. The strait also carried nearly 20% of global LNG trade.

"The pressures of the current crisis must not obscure the choices that will shape energy security, sustainability and prosperity for the decades to come," IEA Executive Director Fatih Birol said.

#2969. Novartis raises blockbuster drug forecasts to $18 billion as it seeks to prove growth can last

Pharma major Novartis is becoming more optimistic about some of its biggest growth drugs, even as investors question the company's ability to sustain growth beyond 2031 and offset the financial impact of older drugs' patent expiries.

The Swiss drugmaker now expects multiple sclerosis drug Kesimpta, leukaemia treatment Scemblix, and cholesterol medicine Leqvio to generate $18 billion in peak sales, up from a previous forecast of $14 billion combined.

CEO Vas Narasimhan said the upgrades were driven primarily by strong commercial performance, pointing to growing market share for Kesimpta and Scemblix and robust demand for Leqvio in the U.S. and Asia.

"It's really that in-market commercial performance that's driving these upgrades," Narasimhan told reporters on Thursday.

Shares fell 2.4% in mid-morning trading in Switzerland. For investors, the question will be whether those drugs can grow fast enough to offset lost sales as other drugs face competition from cheaper rivals entering the market.

Even so, the overall growth outlook looked slightly more cautious despite stronger expectations for some key growth drugs.

Novartis projected annual sales growth of 5% through 2031, slightly less ambitious than its previous guidance of between 5% and 6% through 2030, although it covers a different five-year period.

CEO Vas Narasimhan said the revised outlook reflected the expected entry of U.S. generic competition for Kisqali, a breast cancer treatment, in the second half of 2031.

Citi analysts noted the 2031 sales guidance was about 3% above consensus, adding they expect the market to remain skeptical of post-2031 growth, pending further pipeline data.

Novartis has faced several recent clinical setbacks. In September, its closely watched cardiovascular pelacarsen trial failed to show improved cardiovascular outcomes. The following day, its del-desiran drug for muscle-wasting disorder failed in a late-stage trial, sending shares down nearly 11% in its worst single-day drop since 1987.

Narasimhan defended the drugmaker's capital allocation strategy after the failure of its experimental drug del-desiran, which it acquired via a $12 billion deal for Avidity Biosciences earlier this year.

Shareholders have differing views on acquisitions, with some favouring more aggressive dealmaking and others preferring a different approach, Narasimhan said. However, he said Novartis remained confident in its strategy, pointing to a 275% total shareholder return over the past decade, which he said placed the company among the sector's top performers.

The Nets took a 137-107 loss to the Bulls and embarrassed themselves in front of the greatest player to ever wear their uniform, with Julius Erving one of 10 living members of their 1974 and '76 championship squads being honored at halftime.

"We didn't really talk to the players, the coaches doing all the talking and they were getting their preparation in, doing all their drills … I fully anticipated the Nets to be so fired up having us here, they would blow Chicago out. "Two years in Virginia and three years in New York, I'd say those five years accumulated became probably the best years in terms of really enjoying the sport and learning and going through the process of developing," said Erving, who was raised in Hempstead and then Roosevelt.

When you see a 5-on-5 basketball game played well, it's poetry in motion. And the teams that were successful are the ones who recognized how to do it the right way."

#2960. Trump Just Disclosed Tesla and SpaceX Stock Purchases. Here's What His Portfolio Moves Reveal

The president's latest financial disclosure includes purchases of Tesla and SpaceX, but the stocks he sold tell a more interesting story.

By Jonathan Ponciano – Nov 19, 2026 at 3:09PM EST

President Donald Trump's latest stock transactions are out, and yes, Tesla and SpaceX made the list.

On Thursday, the Office of Government Ethics made public a 25-page transaction report covering hundreds of transactions from September. It shows purchases of Tesla (TSLA -1.75%) and SpaceX (SPCX -4.19%) stock, along with sales of household names like McDonald's (MCD -0.88%) and Home Depot (HD -0.18%).

So how have the moves panned out, and what do they say about the billionaire president's portfolio? So far, the results are mixed: Several of the stocks Trump sold have outperformed the Tesla and SpaceX shares he bought, and the filing seems to indicate a shift toward dividends and bonds rather than a big bet on Elon Musk.

Reports like this one list each trade as a dollar range rather than an exact amount. Trump's four Tesla purchases, made between Sept. 1 and Sept. 10, total between $117,004 and $330,000. His two SpaceX stock purchases add up to $16,002 to $65,000. He also bought $100,001 to $250,000 in SpaceX bonds that pay 5.35% interest and mature in 2031.

The president also had much bigger moves in the filing. On Sept. 23 alone, Trump sold shares of McDonald's, Home Depot, Comcast (CMCSA -1.90%) and Fidelity National Information Services (FIS -3.61%) worth $1,000,001 to $5 million apiece. He also sold Nike (NKE -5.76%) and Constellation Brands (STZ -1.47%) stock worth $500,001 to $1 million each. The single largest transaction in the report, made the same day, was a purchase of $5,000,001 to $25 million of the Vanguard Dividend Appreciation ETF (VIG -1.50%).

Here's how each has done since the day of the transaction, based on that day's closing price and Thursday afternoon trading, according to Nasdaq data. The filing doesn't give the exact prices Trump paid or received.

Four of the six big stocks Trump sold have climbed 10% or more since then. But the FIS sale dodged a 25% drop, marking the best call in the batch. In other words, it's a mixed record, like most investors', and of course, these are just short-term movements. Long-term the picture can look vastly different.

There are three big problems with treating this filing as a stock-picking guide.

The first is timing. These transactions happened in September and became public about eight weeks later. Anyone who copied the McDonald's and Home Depot sales on Thursday would be selling after those stocks had already rallied.

The second is size. Because transactions are reported in ranges, a $1,001 Tesla purchase and a $15,000 one look identical. It's hard to tell a big bet from routine housekeeping.

The third is motive. The filing doesn't say who chose these transactions or why. Many of them involve funds and municipal bonds, which make the account look more like a rebalancing portfolio than a series of handpicked bets.

Tesla and SpaceX are two of the most talked-about tech stocks on the market, and a presidential purchase makes a fun headline. But the biggest message in Trump's filing is a lesson in diversification: a large move into a dividend fund, trims to individual stocks, and lots of bonds.

If you like Tesla or SpaceX, buy them because you believe in the businesses and can hold through the swings, not because they showed up on a government disclosure form.

They just revealed what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy right now, available when you join Stock Advisor.

#2959. Diving deeper into Cavs last-second loss to Grizzlies: Progression isn’t always measured in final result

CLEVELAND — The Cleveland Cavaliers were two free-throw misses away from pulling out a victory over the Memphis Grizzlies. Instead, they fell 124-121 on a night they simply couldn't knock down a shot when they needed to most.

26 points on 9-of-11 shooting, 7 assists, 1 rebound, 1 block

Spencer couldn't miss from three, going 5-for-6 beyond the arc. The Cavs didn't have an answer for his ability to create space for pull-up threes. And when the defense overplayed him to prevent the three, he got around them to score in the paint himself or find a teammate. That's exactly what you want to see from someone with a lethal three-point shot.

On a night when seemingly everyone had a good offensive night, Spencer had the best.

34 points on 14-of-27 shooting, 3 rebounds, 3 assists, 1 steal

Mitchell has had strong scoring nights this season, but this might've been his most explosive outing yet. The perennial All-NBA guard was able to create so much separation between him and his defender with his first step. This allowed him to get into the paint at will and go 9-for-12 on shots in the restricted area.

Two things kept this from being an outstanding night for Mitchell.

First, the three-ball wasn't falling as he went 2-for-9 from beyond the arc. Creating space off the dribble helped him generate clean looks. They just didn't fall.

Second, Mitchell wasn't able to get a big shot to go down when he needed one most. The Cavs had several opportunities to make this a game that wasn't decided at the free-throw line, but they seemingly missed every one of them. That included a couple of errant shots from Mitchell in the fourth as he went 2-for-7 in the final quarter.

18 points on 5-of-9 shooting, 8 rebounds, 5 assists, 4 steals, 1 block

Boozer is incredibly polished for a rookie. He affected seemingly every aspect of the game, which showed through in how he packed the box score.

Anything bought from the links helps support Fear the Sword. You can buy the Browns throwback shirt HERE. You can also shop all of Homage's Cleveland gear HERE.

Merrill made a nice drive to the paint in the closing seconds of the game to force a trip to the line with two seconds left in a game the Cavs were trailing by one. Two free throws would've likely given the Cavs the win. One would've at least ensured overtime. Instead, the 89% career free-throw shooter missed both.

Rather than focusing on the missed free throws, Atkinson praised Merrill for getting t to the line afterward.

"I love the process," Atkinson said. " I love that he drove. He was so smart to drive the ball. That's what I look at. That's a great play. This is sports."

Overall, this was a rough game for Merrill, who made his return to the lineup after missing several games with an elbow injury. He went 1-for-4 on outside attempts and fouled out.

16 points, 7 rebounds, 5 assists, 1 steal

Hezonja made the most of his two starts so far this season. Against the Dallas Mavericks, he scored 22 points. He backed that up here with another all-around impressive performance.

Hezonja can create mismatches in a variety of ways, making him such a unique offensive weapon. Being able to stretch the floor, take someone in the post, and recognize when teammates have advantages allows him to pair well with a wide variety of lineups. He's someone who should get more minutes when it's a favorable matchup like this.

You seem to always get the best version of Allen when you make him a point of emphasis in the game plan.

On Wednesday, the Cavs made a concerted effort to attack opposing center Zach Edey. One of the ways they did that was to try to get Edey to guard out in space, which meant they used Allen even more than normal as an on-ball screen and roller, and as a playmaking hub at the top of the key. Allen excelled in both areas.

Allen is a much more versatile player than he gets credit for being. He's a solid passer, can attack off the dribble with his speed, and isn't a bad ball handler. These were all on display in a game where the Cavs made it a point to feature him.

The second block was incredible. I'm not sure if I've seen anything exactly like that.

"We all trust him, you know, and I would do it again tomorrow. I'd do it again the next night. You're not defined by two free throws at the end of the game."

– Donovan Mitchell on Sam Merrill's two free-throw misses

The Cavs were in the 99th percentile for offensive rebounding (20) and rebounding overall.

The Grizzlies came into this game sixth in both offensive rebounding and second-chance points. The Cavs made sure they weren't able to use that against them this evening.

Even though the Cavs didn't get the result they wanted, this was an impressive performance on the glass for a team that has struggled with rebounding.

Results matter — even in the regular season. From that perspective, this was a failure. Cleveland had a chance to win, but didn't.

At the same time, this Cavs team won't be defined by November results. Postseason success or failure will do so. The process is far and away more important this time of year.

There were some good things to take away from this game from a process perspective. Cleveland put an emphasis on rebounding against one of the best rebounding teams in the league, and they succeeded. Their offense flowed better than it has in recent games as well. Part of that could be attributed to James Harden slowly shifting to a less ball-dominant approach. Role players like Hezonja and Meleek Thomas having strong outings once again is another positive sign. You could also point to the defense in the second half as a positive, as they held Memphis to just 53 points and a much more manageable 112.8 offensive rating then. Those are all good things they can build off of.

"I told everyone in the locker room, we want to learn through wins," Mitchell said. "I've told them that a thousand times. But this loss to me — I don't say this often — it was a good loss for us. There's things we can really take away from watching film. Not even just a schematic standpoint, just the effort, resilience."

It's easy to get caught up in wins and losses, especially after defeats like this. However, there's reason to believe this team is heading in the right direction.

"I'm more so proud of everybody in the locker room than anything else tonight because this is what we need to do," Mitchell said. "We'll grow into that. We're getting the feel for each other, but this is something that we got to continue to carry [forward]."

#2954. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

#2951. Celtics star Jayson Tatum called for technical after throwing ball toward official late in loss to Nuggets

Jayson Tatum got called for a technical foul at the worst time on Wednesday night, and it cost the Boston Celtics a chance at extending the game.

Tatum was called for a technical foul after he threw a ball toward an official in the final seconds of the Celtics' 105-101 loss to the Denver Nuggets at Ball Arena on Wednesday. While the act itself didn't cost the Celtics the game, it made sure they didn't have a legitimate chance to send the contest into overtime.

Denver star Jamal Murray drained a jumper with just seven seconds left in the game, which put the Nuggets up by three points at the time. Tatum caught the ball after it went through the net, and started to make a move to pass it in as the Celtics called a timeout.

But, instead of just turning to hand the ball to the official standing right next to him, Tatum turned and tossed the ball toward the official standing near the scorer's table at midcourt. The ball went behind that official, who seemed a bit confused. The official standing right next to Tatum then called him for a technical foul.

That sent Murray back to the free-throw line. He sank the extra shot, which put the Nuggets up by four and ruined any hopes the Celtics had of forcing overtime.

The throw that Tatum made wasn't egregious by any means. He didn't drill the official with the ball or seem to throw it with any malicious intent. And it even looked like the referee was looking in his direction briefly before turning his back to Tatum once the ball was in the air. Tatum tried to plead his case during the chaotic moments that followed, but the officials weren't having it.

Regardless of intent, throwing a ball at or toward an official like that is often going to result in a technical.

Tatum said after the game that he thought the official, John Goble, was looking toward the Celtics and he didn't intend to throw the ball without him seeing it.

"I should have probably just given the ball to the ref closest to me," Tatum said, via The Athletic. "…I thought that [official] John [Goble] was looking at our bench. I should have just found the guy closest to me — the ref closest to me — and just gave him the ball. So that's on me."

Tatum also claimed he wasn't mad in the moment and wasn't throwing the ball out of frustration.

"John Goble called timeout and pointed at our bench," Tatum said. "I thought he was looking at this way, but, yeah … they called a tech."

Murray and the Nuggets rallied from an early 15-point hole to beat the Celtics on Wednesday night. Murray had 21 points and seven rebounds in the win, which pushed the Nuggets to 8-5 on the year. Nikola Jokić finished just shy of a triple-double, with 24 points, 9 assists and 8 rebounds. The Nuggets had only 13 fouls called on them all night, compared to the 30 that Boston was hit with.

Tatum led the Celtics with 24 points and eight rebounds in the loss, though he scored only four points in the second half. Neemias Queta finished with 17 points and 10 rebounds. The loss marked the second straight for the Celtics and dropped them to 11-4 on the season.

#2945. Warriors vs. Mavericks player grades: Will Richard stars, Yaxel Lendeborg struggles

The Golden State Warriors continued inability to play basketball without Steph Curry took another step forward on Wednesday, when they lost 116-94 to the Dallas Mavericks. It was not quite as ugly as their previous two blowouts, but it was still a one-sided affair, and it gave them four consecutive losses, as well as a strong splash of cold water that followed their eight-game winning streak.

So let's dive into some more disappointing grades. I'll try to be concise this time, because … well, you know. You're not exactly dying to read more about this game are you? This is a judgement free zone, but…

As always, grades are based on my expectations for each individual player, with a "B" grade representing the average performance for that person.

Note: True-shooting percentage (TS) is a scoring efficiency metric that accounts for threes and free throws. Entering Wednesday's games, league-average TS was 57.4%.

30 minutes, 13 points, 5 rebounds, 4 assists, 2 steals, 1 foul, 6-for-18 shooting, 0-for-3 threes, 1-for-2 free throws, 34.4% TS, -25

Lendeborg has spent much of his rookie year looking like he can be a co-star next to Curry, and perhaps even the face of the franchise when Curry decides to hang up his sneakers. But these three games since Curry has been sidelined have been a rude awakening for him that there's still a lot of work to be done before he gets there … which is as it should be for a player who has been in the NBA for less than a month.

Being the focal point of the offense has been difficult for Yaxel, who has shot 16-for-54 over those three games. His jump shot has looked really rough lately — perhaps a little rushed? — and after opening the season 27-for-61 from deep, he's gone just 4-for-21 over his last four contests, with some ugly misses. He did a good job attacking the rim in this game when his shot wasn't falling, but he only shot 6-for-15 inside the arc, and only earned one trip to the free throw line.

It wasn't all bad, though. Despite being the first option, he played more than 30 minutes without a turnover, which is extremely impressive. He had a great stretch at the end of the first quarter and beginning of the second where he was the best player on the court; it was far and away his best run since Curry got injured.

But ultimately, it was a game that highlighted what Lendeborg needs to improve on more than what he currently excels at, and while he worked hard on defense, the results weren't very good there, either.

23 minutes, 3 points, 1 rebound, 2 assists, 1 steal, 2 blocks, 1 turnover, 1 foul, 1-for-1 shooting, 1-for-1 threes, 150.0% TS, -28

22 minutes, 10 points, 8 rebounds, 1 steal, 1 block, 4 turnovers, 3 fouls, 5-for-8 shooting, 0-for-2 free throws, 56.3% TS, -27

He was, quite simply, a defensive menace. Richard ended the game with four steals, but it felt like a lot more, partially because of the amount of plays he disrupted and passes he deflected, and partly because of how many times one of his steals led directly to an uncontested bucket. He's on the court for defense, and he did it better than anyone on either team tonight … that he dropped in a team-leading 20 points, on great efficiency, was just an added bonus. But one hell of an added bonus, I should specify.

That Richard is out there at all given the torn UCL in his thumb is noteworthy. That he's playing this well? It's absurd.

Grade: A+Post-game bonus: Led the team in points.

7 minutes, 2 points, 1 rebound, 2-for-2 free throws, 113.6% TS, +4

Some extended garbage time for Leons, who has been a beneficiary of all these blowout losses. He didn't really make an imprint in this game, for better and for worse.

#2944. Trump praises ‘great relationship’ with Putin after meeting, as Ukraine war takes center stage

SHENZHEN, China — U.S. President Donald Trump met with Vladimir Putin and Xi Jinping individually on Thursday, ahead of a scheduled trilateral with his Russian and Chinese counterparts, according to pool notes and Chinese state media.

On greeting the Russian president on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation forum, Trump said he had a "great relationship" with Putin, adding they had known each other for a long time and expressed hope that "a lot of wonderful things can come out of this, or at least start."

The two presidents were meeting for the first time since a summit in Alaska in August last year.

Russian presidential aide Yuri Ushakov said the talks lasted for approximately 40 minutes, and Putin left the meeting place in "an excellent mood," according to Russian state news outlet Ria Novosti.

Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine was a key topic for the talks, Ushakov said, alongside preparations for the trilateral summit with China's Xi.

In a separate bilateral meeting, Trump said he had discussed the Ukraine war with Xi, saying they should work together to bring an end to it.

"We're looking to both help with respect to the war with Russia and Ukraine," Trump said, according to Reuters. "I'd like to see it ended," he added.

Xi, meanwhile, said the two countries should cooperate to prevent artificial intelligence safety risks and strengthen communication on trade, according to Chinese state media.

Earlier on Thursday, the leaders and other members of APEC gathered for a summit. Trump and Xi entered the room side by side.

Xi said China would provide $20 million to support APEC cooperation, and establish a "Center for Asia-Pacific Community Studies in Shenzhen to conduct policy research and exchanges and provide intellectual support."

Later in the morning session, Trump said that he opposed regulating artificial intelligence as it would "stifle" the industry.

"The United States has been a Pacific power for hundreds of years, and we continue to work to promote growth and stability, sovereignty, prosperity, and peace throughout this region," Trump said.

U.S. officials said last week that Trump did not plan to raise Nvidia's Jensen Huang and allegations of an affair with a Chinese spy at his meetings.

#2941. Musk accuses Zelenskyy of lying about Starshield restrictions in Ukraine

SpaceX founder Elon Musk accused Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy of being untruthful about restrictions around Ukraine's Starshield network and said Kyiv wanted to use Starlink for offensive operations.

Asked at The New York Times' Dealbook Summit about Zelenskyy's claims that Starshield had been slowed down, Musk said, "That's false ... I don't want to blow your mind, but Zelenskyy is not always truthful."

When pressed on whether he meant Ukraine was not always truthful, Musk doubled down with a reply: "No, it's not always truthful."

His comments came after Zelenskyy on Wednesday said speed restrictions remained in place on Starshield, hampering Ukraine's ability to intercept Russian missile and drone strikes. "We are discussing this with the American side," he said.

Russia invaded Ukraine in February 2022, stoking a conflict that has resulted in a refugee crisis and hundreds of thousands of deaths. Ukraine has been able to beat back Russian forces more effectively than many observers thought possible, despite having a much smaller land area and population.

However, it relies heavily on SpaceX's Starlink and Starshield for capabilities like battlefield communications and drone operations. Musk has previously said that "if we were to turn off Starlink, the entire Ukrainian front would collapse immediately."

The SpaceX CEO sought to distinguish between Starlink, a commercial satellite network, and Starshield, a military system developed by SpaceX and operated by the U.S. government.

Musk said that Starlink is subject to speed limits because it is not intended for offensive military operations, adding that such missions should instead use the Starshield network.

"Any action taken by Starshield is up to the War Department. Starlink is commercial and is not intended as a weapon of war," Musk said, referring to the U.S. Department of Defense.

In October, Musk said the U.S. government and President Donald Trump were responsible for any restrictions on Ukraine's Starshield network after slowdowns to the service were reported.

"Starshield systems are owned and controlled by the U.S. government. Any limits on speed or usage are decided by the US military and ultimately POTUS," Musk wrote on X.

The Wall Street Journal later reported on Oct. 20 that Musk had ordered the slowdown, citing U.S. officials and people familiar with the matter.

"What I'm trying to tell you is Zelenskyy is actually asking to use the Starlink commercial satellites for events of military actions, and we have declined to do so, as we have declined to do so from the beginning of this conflict," Musk added in the interview.

On Oct. 24, Musk said SpaceX had restricted Ukraine's access to Starlink because Kyiv had violated the service's terms by using it for offensive drone strikes, a claim Ukrainian officials disputed.

"Ukraine military violated Starlink terms of service and used it for offensive drone strikes to kill people. That loophole was closed last week," Musk wrote on X one day after.

CNBC has reached out to Ukraine's ministry of foreign affairs for comment but did not receive a response. The president's office could not immediately be reached.

#2935. Federal Reserve Board releases results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 10:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

Key findings from the 2025 survey include:

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2930. Lakers win streak snapped as offense goes ice cold against Jazz

The Lakers' winning streak was finally snapped on Wednesday in a rather unceremonious way as the Jazz won going away in the fourth quarter, 109-97.

LA struggled all night offensively, finishing the game shooting just 38.2% from the field and 27.3% from three. They turned the ball over 18 times, leading to 26 points.

Their defense kept them in the game as they forced the Jazz into 23 turnovers for 29 points of their own. However, Utah eventually took control in the fourth to put the game to rest.

Luka Dončić started the game with a layup. Utah, though, had a slight upper hand, with Keyonte George scoring five. Quentin Grimes continued his hot shooting from the previous game by draining a 3-pointer.

On the flip side, Ziaire Williams picked up three quick fouls and had to be subbed out.

Still, the Lakers went on a 7-0 run to give themselves a three-point lead.

Utah answered with a run of their own, scoring six in a row to retake the lead. George and Ace Bailey combined for 10 points. Luka had six, and Williams had five for Los Angeles.

The purple and gold ended the quarter on a 14-4 run, led by Luka, who now had 15 points, giving them a five-point lead.

Collin Sexton was the first to score for Los Angeles in the second period. He had four points. The team, though, was 1-7 from the field. Grimes finally scored for LA on a midrange jumper.

Svi Mykhailiuk started cooking for Utah, draining four triples. He had 12 points in nine minutes. The Lakers were a rough 3-10 from the field in the period.

The half stayed back-and-forth, with the teams trading buckets and the lead. George tallied 11 points for Utah. Luka took over most of the scoring as the quarter wound down, pushing his point total to 22.

LA shot 31% from the field and shot 26% from behind the arc. They were down by two at halftime.

The third period began with the Jazz in control. Josh Green was fouled from behind the arc and converted a four-point play. Luckily for LA, Grimes was still scorching hot, draining two triples and slamming home a dunk to take the lead.

Grimes now had 13 points. He was also 3-4 from behind the arc.

Luka and Grimes kept the Lakers in the lead with their firepower as nobody else on the team was in rhythm offensively. Los Angeles had 21 points off Utah's 16 turnovers, a major reason why they were up.

With 4:25 left, the purple and gold were up three.

The rest of the quarter was a drag for both teams with heavy legs and rough scoring for both sides. Los Angeles did get a small boost from Sandro Mamukelashvili with four points. However, Utah was up by two at the end of the third.

Both teams had a slow start to the final frame. Walker Kessler started to find some offense, scoring five points. George put the Jazz up by three with a triple. He had 26 points.

With 7:50 left in the quarter, the purple and gold once again had to claw their way to a win.

George and Bailey combined for five points, putting Utah up by eight. Things kept getting worse for the Lakers as the Jazz extended their scoring run to 15-0, thanks in part to George and Bailey lighting it up. They had 30 and 15, respectively.

A sudden momentum shift happened when Sexton was subbed in, bringing some energy to the team. LA went on a 7-0 run to make it an eight-point game with 4:15 left, forcing a Utah timeout.

Everything went bad once again out of the break as the Jazz just turned the jets on and made it an 18-point game. LA emptied the bench with 1:51 left.

Luka finished with 29 points, five rebounds and seven assists. Kessler had a slow game with 10 points and 11 rebounds. Grimes ended with 20 points, seven rebounds and four assists.

Williams logged seven points. Sexton scored 12 points off the bench. Matisse Thybulle dished three dimes and had four steals. Jarred Vanderbilt and LaRavia combined for 11 rebounds, five assists and four steals.

The Lakers' next game will be against the Sacramento Kings on Friday at 7:00 PM PT.
