#2929. Family offices move more money into stocks, hedge AI risk, according to CNBC Family Office Portfolio Tracker

A version of this article appeared in CNBC's Inside Wealth newsletter, a weekly guide to the high-net-worth investor and consumer. Sign up to receive future editions, straight to your inbox.

Family offices continued to boost their stock holdings in the third quarter, although they hedged the artificial intelligence trade with a larger bet on financials, according to the CNBC Family Office Portfolio Tracker.

Family offices increased their allocation to public stocks to 36.4% of their portfolios in the third quarter, up from 35.6% in the second quarter, according to the Tracker.  Their stock holdings have jumped from 33.6% in 2024, a substantial increase for family offices, which rarely deviate from their long-term financial allocations.

The CNBC Family Office Portfolio Tracker is powered by data from Addepar, the foundational data and artificial intelligence platform used by financial professionals globally that now has over $10 trillion on its platform. Unlike surveys, Addepar's data provides a real-time look into the portfolios of hundreds of single-family offices — aggregated and anonymized —representing a total of more than $1.4 trillion in family office assets.

The increased allocation to stocks, whether from passive market gains or active buying, shows that family offices aren't pulling back from the market despite growing fears of an AI bubble and weakness in tech stocks.

"As we've studied family offices for more than 15 years, they've demonstrated time and again that they take the long view," said Eric Poirier, CEO of Addepar. "But they do like staying invested. So with the run-up in AI thematically, they're staying exposed, they're risk on."

At the same time, family offices are hedging their tech bets. According to Addepar, family offices have lower exposure to AI stocks when compared to the broader S&P 500. While tech stocks account for 40% of the S&P market cap, family offices have an average allocation of 28% of their equity portfolios in tech stocks, according to the Tracker.

They are offsetting the tech risk in part with financials. Financial services account for 11% of the S&P, but the average allocation for family offices is 18%. Poirier said the lower tech weighting and higher financials exposure suggests that family offices see financials as a potential offset to AI risk.

"They're fading the AI trade to some extent, but still staying quite exposed," he said.

Family offices are also continuing to dial back their exposure to alternative investments. Alts account for 47% of their portfolios, down from 48.5% a year ago. The biggest decliner was real estate, down to 7.2% from 9.4% in the third quarter of 2025. Higher interest rates have driven down valuations in many real-estate portfolios and funds, likely accounting for much of the drop.

Hedge funds also saw a decline, from 8.9% a year ago to 7.5%, as many funds grapple with more volatile stock markets and declining tech stocks. Their investments in private credit declined slightly, to 1% of their portfolios. Their private equity holdings fell for the second straight quarter, to 6.4% of their portfolios.

They also saw a decline in their allocation to private companies — companies held directly rather than through a private equity fund. Family office holdings of private companies fell 0.3% to 15.7%, likely due to slight declines in valuations. Still, private companies remain by far their largest segment among alts, as family offices continue to focus on direct investing.

Cash piles grew slightly for family offices — to 8.6% — suggesting they want to keep adequate dry powder on hand in case of a market correction or crisis.

The big question for family offices this year and next is how they manage rising yields and inflation fears. Some were positioning for inflation at the start of the year with bets on infrastructure and real estate, yet the surge in the yields driven by oil prices was faster than many expected.

Family office holdings of fixed income have steadily increased for three straight quarters, to 8.1% in the third quarter.

"Family offices tend to be able to withstand market volatility more so than other investors," Poirier said. "That said, their response to the historic rise in fixed income and rates has been more muted relative to even five-year averages."

The Inside Wealth newsletter by Robert Frank is your weekly guide to high-net-worth investors and the industries that serve them.

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

#2924. Week 11 Fantasy Football Rankings: WR

Luther Burden III comes off bye against the Saints, Jordyn Tyson tries to get comfortable in the WR2 ranks, and Carnell Tate (shoulder) hopes to suit up for a dream matchup.

Other positions: Quarterback | Running Back | Tight End/Kickers/Defense

WR Notes: I touched the Parker Washington rankings stove. I am now at urgent care. That ended quite poorly for me. I had failed to accept/appreciate this is just "one of those offenses." Multi-dimensional and deep, not everyone is going to get theirs every week, but it will all be there by the end. … Cade Klubnik was reasonably adept at getting the ball to his primary pass catchers during Geno Smith's injury-abbreviated Week 10. It's fair to wonder if that could really become a trend, but with Omar Cooper Jr. (ankle) on the shelf, Garrett Wilson has the compiling lane all to himself, with Adonai Mitchell certain to receive down-field opportunities. You aren't benching Wilson and you are probably thankful to have Mitchell as a WR3/4 option. … DJ Moore has eclipsed 4/80/1 twice in three games since returning. Good, though he has also yet to catch more than four passes during that timespan. Will the Bills really be throwing enough against the Dolphins for Moore to spike a week? Perhaps not, but the WR2 options are unsurprisingly scarce with six teams on bye.

The Saints took Jordyn Tyson for a Week 9 test drive before getting him up to 120 on the highway in Week 10. The No. 8 overall pick is now a fully-operational member of the Saints' offense. We would have to imagine Chris Olave is given some tender loving care after his four-target Week 10, but Tyson is obviously now a WR2 until further notice — or his hamstring gives out. … DeVonta Smith (hamstring) returns to a post-apocalyptic Eagles offense. Although it includes just one appearance since September — and only one without Jalen Hurts — Smith has cleared 6/60 in each of his past three games. Joe Milton is a potential fantasy anchor, but bye-addled lineups have to take WR2 work where they can find it. … Carnell Tate's two-game absence weirdly coincided with the best two-game stretch of Cam Ward's career. We are going to assume there's not some bizarre "Ewing theory" thing going on and that Tate's return will only enhance Ward's improved play. Already trending toward every-week WR2 status before his interregnum, Tate is certainly a must play vs. the broken Cowboys. … Myles Price is who "we" (I don't mean to implicate you in this) thought Reggie Virgil was? I don't know, workshopping that one.

Things are dire enough at WR2 that Caleb Douglas is now legitimately in the mix. Amongst wideouts to play at least three games over the past five weeks, Douglas is the WR22 by average PPR points. "WR22" is a reasonable "no more, no less" guess for his Week 11 production. … Josh Downs played 10 snaps in his Chiefs debut and finished as the WR19. Now hopefully he makes like Jordyn Tyson and triples that number for his follow-up. You feel good about point chasing Downs despite his limited Week 10 work. … Keenan Allen played under 50 percent of the snaps in his 49ers debut and finished as the WR35. Not too shabby, but ... admittedly kind of shabby. I'm having a surprisingly difficult time finding a Week 11 rank I'm comfortable with, though the general takeaway is: I won't be benching Allen in any format. … Deebo Samuel's nuclear spiked week against the Cowboys still featured all of three catches and three targets. Coach Kyle Shanahan has an even more particular vision for Samuel's usage this time around than he did during Deebo's first Niners go-round. He should be a healthy part of the Week 11 game plan to counteract the Vikings' pressure, but Samuel's lack of floor makes him more of a WR3 than WR2.

DK Metcalf is on the WR3/4 borderline by average PPR points and must now deal with the healthy returns of Roman Wilson (finger) and Michael Pittman Jr. (foot). He has never really established a ceiling in Pittsburgh, and his floor is not what you would expect. He's frankly a starting lineup space filler. At least that's something that is needed for Week 11. … Emeka Egbuka has struggled to produce regardless of who is under center this season, so it's difficult to parse how big of a deal Baker Mayfield's return to the inactives list is going to be. Alas, everyone has struggled not to produce when facing the Lions' pass defense this season, one now missing linchpin pass rusher Aidan Hutchinson. You aren't working overtime to get Egbuka into your WR2 spot, but it would take some genuine effort for him to end up on Week 11 benches. ... Sunk cost Jaylen Waddle rankings will continue until morale improves. This might for real be his genuine, actual, last chance in the top 30 at wideout. He should be as healthy as he's been in months coming off the Broncos' bye, and the Raiders are a smash spot matchup.

If Waddle isn't ready to operate as the Broncos' WR1, perhaps Pat Bryant is. As is the case with Jonah Coleman, Bryant seems to pass every Sean Payton audition. He arguably has the best overall game in this strangely inert receiver corps. If you have been stashing Bryant on your bench, byemageddon is the perfect time for a pre-Thanksgiving unboxing. … You "have" to bench Jalen Coker, except, of course, you cannot actually do so this week. He has a reasonable enough "last chance" matchup in the Ravens' increasingly broken defense. If Coker does not return to our WR2/3 graces this week, he will indeed find himself on the pine for next week's 32-team extravaganza. … If Mark Andrews is the biggest winner of Jacoby Brissett's insertion under center, Ja'Kobi Lane is a close second. Lane has spent most of 2026 percolating but been derailed by injuries to both himself and his quarterbacks. Now he can offer reasonable hope of being a big-play based WR4. … Malachi Fields, Elic Ayomanor and Tre' Harris are the kind of WR4s you typically end up tiebreaking against that might actually come in handy this short-handed week. Chimere Dike also gets a second chance as a WR4/second FLEX after being handily out-produced by Ayomanor in Week 10.

#2922. Takaichi gets chilly reception in China on first Japan leader visit since 2019

Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi received a subdued welcome on her first trip to China as premier, signaling Beijing's limited appetite for repairing ties strained by a simmering row over Taiwan.

At gatherings such as this week's Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit in Shenzhen, protocol details — including who greets a visiting leader at the airport and where they are positioned in official photographs — often convey political messages.

Takaichi was met on the tarmac Wednesday by a retired Chinese diplomat, rather than a serving official, and Japan's ambassador to China, Kenji Kanasugi.

The low-key reception contrasted sharply with the warmer welcomes given to other visiting leaders, including South Korean President Lee Jae Myung, while U.S. President Donald Trump was greeted personally by Chinese President Xi Jinping.

In the leaders' group photo, Takaichi was placed in the back row, off to the side and several places from Xi, who stood alongside Trump and Russian President Vladimir Putin.

"Beijing sought to downgrade her official status to that of a head of government rather than a head of state," said Jeremy Chan, senior analyst at Eurasia Group.

During Thursday morning's greeting line, Takaichi spent several minutes trying to talk to the Chinese leader without an interpreter present, while most leaders exchanged handshakes and kept walking.

"It has become increasingly difficult for her to engage in formal or even informal conversations with Xi," said Kei Koga, an associate professor at Nanyang Technological University in Singapore, who sees Takaichi's intention as to still "project the image that she could still communicate with him despite the ongoing tensions."

At a press briefing Thursday afternoon, Takaichi said Japan's position on Taiwan remained unchanged as stated in the 1972 Japan-China Communique, adding that her leadership has been communicating with Chinese counterparts on rare earth export controls.

Takaichi's subdued reception signaled Beijing's lack of enthusiasm to repair ties with Tokyo, analysts said, potentially prolonging China's curbs on critical mineral exports that have gripped Japanese industries for the past year.

"China's frosty reception of Takaichi shows quite clearly that Beijing is not yet ready to forgive her over her remarks about Taiwan," Chan said, expecting the bilateral relationship to remain strained until Takaichi leaves office.

That could leave Japan grappling with continued restrictions on rare earth supplies from China next year, said Tianchen Xu, senior economist at the Economist Intelligence Unit.

Relations deteriorated sharply after Takaichi said in November 2025 that a Taiwan Strait crisis could constitute a situation threatening Japan's survival and warrant a self-defense response.

While the substance broadly aligned with Japan's existing security policy, previous prime ministers had generally avoided stating it so explicitly in a Taiwan scenario.

China reacted angrily and demanded that Takaichi retract the remarks. She has not done so, while stating that the door to dialogue with Beijing remains open, with minimal high-level contact over the past year.

China has since tightened restrictions affecting Japan, including controls on dual-use and rare earth exports with potential military uses. It also discouraged its citizens from traveling to Japan and halted Japanese seafood imports.

Takaichi's visit marked the first trip to China by a Japanese prime minister since Shinzo Abe traveled to Chengdu in December 2019 for a trilateral summit with Chinese and South Korean leaders.

No formal Takaichi-Xi meeting has been publicly scheduled during her visit. Meanwhile, Xi is expected to join Trump and Putin for a historic trilateral gathering on Thursday, alongside a bilateral meeting with Trump.

Takaichi needs to show a "reflection of what she said about Taiwan a year ago," said Huiyao Wang, founder of the Beijing-based Center for China and Globalization, which is often seen as close to Beijing's thinking.

Wang said Tokyo should show greater flexibility, pointing to Trump's comments that the U.S. would avoid getting involved in the Taiwan issue. He argued that Japan holding firm on the issue would not help relations with Beijing.

Such reflection, he said, "would really help to clarify some of those misunderstandings between the two countries."

— CNBC's Evelyn Cheng contributed to this report.

#2921. Timothée Chalamet and Kylie Jenner witness Lakers shocking loss to the Jazz

Crypto.com Arena was once again the home to the biggest stars in Hollywood during the Lakers' 109-97 loss to the Utah Jazz on Wednesday night, snapping Los Angeles' nine-game winning streak.

In a turn of events, Kylie Jenner was sitting courtside with her boyfriend, Timothée Chalamet.

The two were spotted on the Spectrum Sports broadcast during the second half, when the Lakers were trailing 56-54.

Timothée Chalamet and Kylie Jenner spotted sitting courtside at the Lakers game on Wednesday night. NBAE via Getty Images

"There's a power couple. Timothée and Kylie," Lakers color commentator Stu Lantz said when the two were spotted.

It's well known that Chalamet is a die-hard New York Knicks fan, as he's been spotted as a regular sitting courtside at Madison Square Garden.

During the Knicks' NBA title run last year, Chalamet was spotted cheering on his beloved Knicks when they were home and when they were on the road in San Antonio.

Chalamet's appearance in Los Angeles likely happened because New York had the day off and the next time they play will be Friday on the road against the Minnesota Timberwolves.

Timothée Chalamet and Kylie Jenner sitting courtside at the Lakers game on Wednesday night. Getty Images

Though Chalamet's allegiance lies with the Knicks, Jenner is a Los Angeles native, but it's unknown if she grew up a Lakers fan.

Last August, she appeared on an episode of the "Better Half" podcast and revealed she's developed an allegiance to the Knicks.

"There's something about the energy in the stadium and [in Madison Square Garden] and being there," Jenner said. "It was so much fun, and then I followed them for those two seasons."

Luka Dončić running down the court during the Lakers' 109-97 loss to the Jazz on Nov. 18, 2026 in Los Angeles. NBAE via Getty Images

Her boyfriend, who is well known for his roles in Marty Supreme and Dune, has always made it known he's a big-time sports fan and even showed off his knowledge when he appeared on the ESPN show, "College GameDay," in 2024.

One reason that made Chalamet show up was likely because he came to watch early MVP favorite Luka Dončić, who's had two 50-plus-point performances this season.

Dončić came into Wednesday's game against the Jazz averaging 38.8 points with 7.2 rebounds and 7.7 assists.

Though the Lakers lost, Chalamet and Jenner also watched Dončić score less than 30 points in a game for the first time since Oct. 23, against the Clippers.

Dončić still managed to finish as the Lakers' leading scorer, having 29 points with five rebounds and seven assists.

#2914. Tired-looking Lakers can't make their shots in loss to Jazz

The tank appeared to be empty, and there was nothing Luka Doncic or Quentin Grimes or Walker Kessler could do to help the Lakers this time.

Doncic and Grimes especially had been driving the Lakers during their nine-game winning streak, but their struggles Wednesday were a microcosm of what ailed the Lakers during a 109-97 loss to the Utah Jazz at Crypto.com Arena.

Doncic and Grimes combined to shoot 18 for 48 from the field and six for 22 from three-point range.

As a team, the Lakers shot 38.2% from the field and 27.3% from three-point range as their nine-game winning streak ended.

After the Jazz opened an 18-point lead with 1:51 left, Lakers coach JJ Redick waved the white flag, pulling his starters after a rough night.

The Lakers were playing a back-to-back game and third game this week, and they looked fatigued from it all.

Utah (3-13) also was playing on the second night of back-to-back games, but won their second straight despite turning the ball over 23 times.

"I asked the guys, 'Who in here feels like they played well tonight?' Nobody raised their hand," Redick said. "So, like everybody can be better. We can sit here and nitpick every single guy. Nitpick me. It's like whatever. Could my rotation's been different? Maybe gotten a little more offense. Maybe played a little more size at the five. … Yeah, we'll do all that.

Read more: How three emergency centers helped Lakers survive the Hornets without Walker Kessler

"But we didn't play well. Nobody played well. So, it's not Walker. It's not Luka. It's not Q. It's not Sandro (Mamukelashvili). Nobody had a great game."

Doncic had 29 points, but he shot 10 for 28 from the field and two for 12 from three-point range, never able to get on track because of the constant double teams the Jazz sent his way.

"Honestly I've just got to do way better than this," said Doncic, who was assessed a technical foul in the second quarter,. "This is unacceptable for my part. I got to lead the team in better ways. So just, like I said, got to look at this game, got to move on and learn from this."

Doncic had to do a lot with his backcourt running mate, Austin Reaves (left heel bruise), out.

Doncic has been carrying the team, but he still took responsibility for the loss and explained what he has to do to be a better leader for the Lakers.

Lakers forward Sandro Mamukelashvili goes to the basket against Utah's Brice Sensabaugh and Svi Mykhailiuk. (Eric Thayer / Los Angeles Times)

"Well, just a little bit of everything," he said. "Obviously the shots, the turnovers. Those [are] two [of the] specifics. I think defensively, we're in a good space. Just offensively, I've got to lead the team better than this."

Grimes had 20 points, but he shot eight for 20 from the field and four for 10 from three-point range.

He had just come off a career-high 47-point game Tuesday night against the Hornets.

"We had a lot of emotion in last night's game coming back as a team, as a whole, and it was a quick less-than-24-hour turnaround against a team that's better than their record (indicates)," Grimes said. "We tried to get as much energy as we could into that third, but it just wasn't enough. Not enough legs. We felt like none of us played well tonight and we were still in the game. So that's a positive takeaway, for sure."

Kessler spent his first four years in the NBA playing for the Jazz, making relationships and friendships that remain even though he now plays for the Lakers. But when the Lakers and Kessler played the Jazz last month in Utah, those same friends did all they could to make life difficult for the big man.

Lakers center Walker Kessler dunks over Jazz guard Ace Bailey in the second half on Wednesday. (Eric Thayer / Los Angeles Times)

Kessler had only two points on just two shots, to go along with seven rebounds. The Lakers won the game, so it was all good for Kessler.

He missed the game against the Charlotte Hornets on Tuesday night because of right lower leg soreness but was back starting and looking forward to having a better outing Wednesday against the Jazz.

He finished the game with a double-double of 10 points and 11 rebounds, but shot four for 10 from the free-throw line and three for seven from the field.

"It was a tough night for me," Kessler said. "Tried to get it back with the rebounds on the defensive side. My touch was not what it should be tonight, from the free-throw line obviously and from the field. But, you got to move on, figure it out and respond. That's why I love this game so much because you're only as good as your last game. So, this one stings a little bit, but wake up tomorrow and go back to work and get another one Friday."

#2906. Intel signs first external customer for advanced 18A chips

Intel CEO Lip-Bu Tan said on Wednesday that the company has signed the first outside customer for its 18A chips.

"Resilient supply on the foundry is very important," Tan said at the TomorrowX Summit in Austin, Texas. "And then 18A, I'm involved in production and we just signed the first customer," Tan added, without naming the debut client.

It's a milestone moment for Intel in its five-year effort to take on chip manufacturing leader Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co. After years of missteps and low yields, Intel touted 18A as key to a turnaround that would finally convert the company into a competitive chip manufacturer for non-Intel products.

"It's a huge win for foundry," Creative Strategies analyst Ben Bajarin told CNBC in an interview.

Pushkar Ranade, Intel's technology chief, clarified on X that the external customer had signed up for Intel's 18A-P manufacturing process node, which went into risk production in June. In May, reports indicated that Apple and Intel were closing in on a deal to use Intel's foundry to make some of the chips for Apple devices.

Intel's stock has lost more than half its value since the end of September, with most of that plunge coming late last month after the company said revenue this quarter will be at least $1 billion shy of analysts' estimates due to a PC sales slump and supply chain challenges.

In considering who the customer may be, Bajarin pointed to Nvidia, Apple or the major hyperscalers that make their own custom processors.

"Amazon, Microsoft and Google are struggling to get capacity for their CPU ambitions at TSMC," he said. "So it makes sense that you go somewhere for at least some capacity."

Tan said in late October that he sees the global market for server processors increasing as much as five times over the next five years to about $200 billion, but that Intel can't currently meet customer demand.

Wall Street has turned on Intel of late after the chipmaker had become a top trade starting in the middle of last year. The U.S. government took a 10% stake in Intel in August 2025, and Nvidia followed with a $5 billion investment, as optimism grew around the prospect of U.S.-made chips that could replace an almost complete reliance on Taiwan.

Until now, Intel has had no marquee customer for its foundry business, primarily manufacturing chips for its own products at its factories, including a huge new campus in Arizona.

Intel is a leader in another step of the chipmaking process known as advanced packaging, where individual chips are packaged together and connected to the larger system. Tan also announced on Wednesday that Microsoft is its first publicly named customer for its most advanced packaging technology, known as EMIB.

—CNBC's Kif Leswing contributed to this report.

#2903. Lakers’ nine-game winning streak ends with loss to Jazz

The mark of a truly good team isn't just how dominant they look when they're firing on all cylinders.

It's also how they're able to hang in, or even win, games when things aren't going their way.

The Lakers hung in for most of Wednesday night against the Jazz despite playing well below the standard they've established for themselves over the last month, but they fell to the Jazz 109-97 at Crypto.com Arena to end their winning streak.

Luka Dončić struggled like most of his teammates during the Lakers' 109-97 streak-snapping loss to the Jazz on Nov. 18, 2026, in Los Angeles. NBAE via Getty Images

The Lakers' scoring total marked just the second time this season they were held below 100 points, just above their season-low 95 points they scored in their Oct. 30 road loss to the Warriors.

They shot a season-worst 38.2% from the field (34 for 89) and 27.3% (9 for 33) on 3-pointers.

Keyonte George, going up for a shot, had 30 points and 10 assists to help lead the Jazz past the Lakers. NBAE via Getty Images

The Lakers fell to 13-3 on the season following the loss to the Jazz after winning their previous nine consecutive games, which included a comeback overtime victory over the Hornets on Tuesday night.

Luka Dončić had another poor shooting performance (10 for 28), scoring 29 points to go with seven assists and five rebounds, but the Slovenian superstar also recorded nine of his team's 18 turnovers.

Wednesday ended Dončić's streak of scoring at least 30 points (11 games).

Kyle Filipowski (2) and Collin Sexton (10) battle for the ball during the Lakers' loss to the Jazz. AP Photo/Caroline Brehman

Quentin Grimes added 20 points, seven rebounds and four assists, Collin Sexton chipped in 12 points and Walker Kessler had a 10-point, 11-rebound double-double against his former team.

The Lakers hung in for most of the game despite struggling to get shots to fall, tying the game at 83 apiece when Grimes made a layup at the 8:19 mark of the fourth.

But the Jazz, who are now 3-13 on the season after back-to-back wins over the Clippers and Lakers, went on a 15-0 run to create a gap the Lakers couldn't close.

#2900. Federal Reserve Board announces pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, and changes to streamline and adjust their check services

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 1:30 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday announced pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, such as the clearing of checks, automated clearing house (ACH) transactions, instant payments, and wholesale payment and settlement services.

By law, the Federal Reserve must establish fees to recover the costs, including imputed costs, of providing payment services over the long run. The Federal Reserve expects to recover 101.5 percent of actual and imputed expenses in 2027, including the return on equity that would have been earned if a private-sector firm provided the services. Overall, price changes for 2027 will result in an estimated 1.4 percent average price increase for established, mature services.

In addition, the Federal Reserve Banks announced changes to streamline and adjust their check services. As described in the request for information on check services issued last year, the volume of checks processed by the Federal Reserve Banks is declining, but checks remain an important payment instrument for many consumers and businesses. The changes were informed by public feedback and ensure that the Federal Reserve Banks continue to provide check services efficiently. The adjustments include optimizing check processing windows during low-volume periods, increasing fees that banks and credit unions pay to process checks, and other operational updates. These changes do not impact consumers' and businesses' ability to pay with or receive checks and only impact how banks and credit unions process checks with Federal Reserve Banks.

The entire 2027 fee schedule for all of the Federal Reserve's payment services is published on FRBservices.org®. Additional information on the Federal Reserve's payments services, including service usage statistics, is available on the Board's website.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.â¯â¯

#2897. Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella calls Outlook outage a 'perfect storm.' Here's what went wrong

Microsoft could have done more to prevent the Outlook outage last week that brought swaths of corporate America to a standstill, CEO Satya Nadella acknowledged in an interview with CNBC's Jim Cramer.

"It was one of those crazy perfect storms where there was a latent bug that got sort of found only during a particular traffic pattern," Nadella said on "Mad Money" on Wednesday evening. "Of course, we should have done the testing before. We should have figured out how to have the bulkheads such that you didn't have a global outage."

The Outlook disruption last Thursday was the first domino to fall in a rough week for the technology sector, as a string of major outages disrupted everything from corporate communications to airline operations and banking transactions. The incidents underscored just how dependent everyday life has become on technology — and how indispensable Microsoft is to corporate America. That role is why its stock has long been a cornerstone of investors' technology exposure.

Nadella said the incident highlighted a broader challenge facing corporate America: as AI helps companies uncover software bugs faster, the growing number of updates needed to fix them can introduce instability into older systems.

"The thing that AI has done is it allowed us to find these bugs and fix these bugs. But that means you've got to then patch them, and that creates instability," he said, explaining that there's been an increase in how quickly companies develop, test and deploy fixes, known as "patch velocity." He added, "And I think a lot of what you're seeing is a little bit of, maybe, old infrastructure with new patches and instability."

Despite Microsoft's critical role in the business world, its stock had a rough start to the year as investors worried that advancements in artificial intelligence could upend seat-based software businesses. But the narrative started to shift over the summer, supported by Microsoft's strong cloud growth and relative spending discipline versus its hyperscaler peers.

Shares bottomed out for the year in late June, and since the company's late-July earnings report, the stock has climbed 30%. The momentum has stalled a bit of late, though. The stock has pulled back nearly 9% over the past two weeks since its all-time closing high on Nov. 4, the day after the midterm elections.

Nevertheless, Microsoft's latest earnings on Oct. 28 reinforced its business momentum. Azure cloud revenue surged 48% to $33.2 billion, highlighting robust demand for AI computing power. At its Ignite conference Tuesday, Microsoft also laid out its vision for Copilot as the central hub for workplace productivity, making the case that AI could strengthen its dominance in enterprise software rather than undermine it.

Copilot has faced criticism for lagging behind the AI offerings from Anthropic and OpenAI, in which Microsoft was an early investor. Nadella highlighted Microsoft's September overhaul of Copilot, which brought its AI tools into a single app and introduced consumption-based pricing for more advanced features. By charging customers for usage, Microsoft is helping to defend against a decline in the number of paid seat licenses, which had been at the heart of the AI disruption thesis earlier this year.

"So the consumption will go up, and people will have to have new business models. So, for example, in Dynamics now, a lot of our business is not about seats; it's usage," Nadella said. Dynamics is Microsoft's customer relationship management and enterprise resource planning application.

The Copilot update also reflected Microsoft's push to become less reliant on OpenAI's models in its own applications as their partnership evolves.

"You want Claude, you want OpenAI, or our own models? You can route across all of them," Nadella said.

Another one of the debates surrounding Microsoft and other cloud operators is whether their AI buildout plans will be derailed by growing opposition to data center construction. Some on Wall Street, including CNBC's Jim Cramer, argue that established tech giants like Microsoft stand to benefit, at the expense of smaller players such as the neoclouds.

Nadella acknowledged the challenge, emphasizing that companies must demonstrate the tangible benefits of their investments to the communities hosting their facilities.

"We have to win the hearts and minds and the votes one community at a time," Nadella said. "It's not about even a narrative that I can tell you, but it is about the reality that communities feel."

He pointed to Quincy, Washington, where Microsoft has operated a data center for roughly two decades, as an example of how such investments can benefit local economies through jobs, tax revenue and infrastructure improvements.

"The overall tax benefits that have come in have all gone to building a new school, a new city center, lots of the facilities that the people in the community love, and that and oh by the way the wages, the average wages of the people who are employed there is twice the average wage before," he said. "So we have created long term employment."

Nadella also addressed Jensen Huang's departure from Nvidia, which has dominated headlines from Silicon Valley to Wall Street and Washington. Microsoft is a big buyer of Nvidia chips for its Azure cloud.

"Jensen, let's face it, was a singular founder," he said. "He did a fantastic job building a great company. And by the way, I know [interim CEO Colette Kress]. She worked at Microsoft for a long time. She's a terrific person. Ian Buck, Nick Parker, who worked with us, who's there. So [these] are all fantastic people at Nvidia, and we are very thrilled to be partnering with the company Jensen built with the people that are at Nvidia. And then, of course, it's the Nvidia board will decide what happens next."

#2896. Agencies extend comment period for proposed third-party risk management guidance

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, the Federal Reserve Board, the National Credit Union Administration, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency today announced that they will extend, until December 16, the comment period for proposed guidance on managing risks associated with third-party relationships. Comments on the proposal were originally due November 16.

The proposed guidance reflects the agencies' supervisory experience and lessons learned from examining financial institutions' third-party risk management practices. It is intended to help banks and credit unions tailor their risk management practices to the risks associated with individual third-party relationships. The proposed guidance takes a principles-based approach and, like all supervisory guidance, is non-binding.

When finalized, the federal bank regulatory agencies plan to rescind existing third-party risk management guidance and replace it with the finalized guidance to promote consistency and prudent innovation in the banking industry. NCUA also plans to rescind its existing third-party risk management guidance and will conduct its own reviews to update related guidance as needed.

Also today, the Federal Reserve Board separately announced that it will extend, until December 16, the comment period on a proposed third-party risk management guide specifically for Federal Reserve-supervised traditional community banks.

Federal Register notice: Proposed Third-Party Risk Management Guidance; Extension of Comment Period

Federal Register notice: Proposed Third-Party Risk Management Guide for Traditional Community Banking Organizations

Agencies seek comment on proposed third-party risk management guidance and issue statement on community bank engagement with core service providers

#2894. The Suns beat the Kings 128-107 to get to .500 for the first time this season

The Phoenix Suns are now .500 for the first time this season as they won convincingly against the Sacramento Kings, 128-107. The Suns were firing on all cylinders offensively with both Devin Booker and Dillon Brooks scoring 29 points a piece to lead the Suns. The Kings were led by rookie sensation Darius Acuff Jr., who scored 36 points on 11-of-22 shooting, including 6-for-11 from 3, while Zach LaVine scored 18 as well on 8-of-12 shooting.

The Suns were not sharp defensively in the first half but had long stretches in the third and fourth quarters where they were active in creating Kings turnovers and deflections. Even when they were sharper on defense, the shot-making from LaVine and Acuff Jr. kept this game closer than what it should have been through three quarters.

The Suns' game plan against the Kings was to make Acuff Jr. defend, and he simply did not hold up. Many of Brooks 29 points came against Acuff Jr. with his back to the basket in the mid-post, and Miles Bridges scored 13 points on 6-of-8 shooting, taking advantage of smaller mismatches in the post as well. The Suns' ball movement was exquisite all night long because of the Kings' inability to stop the Suns' scorers, and the Suns rarely ended possessions without generating a quality look. The Suns shot 57% from the field and 44% from 3 on 15-of-34 shooting.

It was the same story again for Jalen Green as it was against the Nuggets. He struggled to get going in the first half, with missed shots and turnovers from his loose handle that gave the Kings momentum and stalled out Suns runs. But he stayed in the fight, and in the second half, he looked more effective. He finished with a team-high seven rebounds and a team-high seven assists. He was doing the little things well and also scored 18 points on 4-of-7 shooting from long distance.

The Suns bench scored an adequate 33 points tonight, and their scoring may continue to trend down slightly as Jordan Ott once again had Brooks in with the second unit to start the second and fourth quarters. By far the biggest takeaway from this game was how much better the starters are playing together on both ends of the court. Albeit against a now 1-13 Kings team, the main four Suns players are starting to look more confident and more connected as a group that has shaken off its slow 1-5 start to the season.

This game started off with a lot of tough shot-making. Dillon Brooks got the Suns going with a backdown layup and a 3-point jumper. NBA rookie leading scorer Darius Acuff Jr. came out firing, scoring 10 early points for the Kings. The Suns have a professional bucket getter of their own with Booker, and he scored 12 points in the first period. Six of his 12 points came on pull-up 3-point shots; his ability to knock those shots down has opened his game in a big way, and that continued to be the case against the Kings.

The Suns again subbed out Brooks first to give him minutes to start the second quarter with the bench, and Jalen Green showed some aggressiveness and energy but only went 1-of-5 from the field, and his loose handle continues to get in his way of being a solid creator for this offense. Outside of Green, both teams showed up offensively in this game and shot over 50% from the floor as the Suns led 36-31 after one, setting up the bench to take the game over.

The Suns started the second quarter strong as Gillespie knocked down a 3-point jumper and Brooks scored in the midrange. Brooks got the ball again isolated in the mid-post and missed, but Goodwin skied in for the offensive rebound tip-in to give the Suns a 43-35 lead. The Suns still could not stop the Kings as LaVine knocked in a long-range triple that Bridges matched on the other end. After a Leaky Black layup at the basket, Green found himself all alone in the corner for a wide-open 3-point shot that missed, and Monk hit a wide-open shot from the corner to get the Kings back within three.

After the timeout, the Suns got a thunderous dunk from Bridges on a nice feed from Green driving baseline. The starters continue to look more comfortable creating offense for themselves and each other the past few games. Then it was the Dillon Brooks show; he made a turnaround over LaVine, hit a 3, then scored at the rim in transition in almost consecutive possessions. He continues to look more and more like the Brooks we saw last season. The Suns were up 55-48 and had the chance to go up even more, but Luke Kennard missed back-to-back wide 3s. The Kings capitalized with Acuff Jr. and Sabonis's baskets to make it 55-52 with 4:30 left to play in the half.

They came out of the timeout with the starters plus Maluach on the floor and started what looked to be a commanding 10-0 run to finish the half. Green got to the free-throw line out of the timeout, and the Suns forced a Kings miss. What happened next was the best possession of the night: Brooks drove to the rim in transition, kicked it out to the corner, and the ball swung all the way back from one corner to the other for a wide-open Brooks 3 that he, of course, knocked down. The Suns then capped off the run with a wide-open Green 3.

But then the Suns got in their own way and allowed Acuff Jr. to hit back-to-back 3s, and a Green turnover led to a wide-open Hunter layup on the other end to close the half on an 8-0 run. Suns led 65-60 at halftime. Both teams shot over 50% in the first half in a fun-to-watch, up-and-down game. Acuff Jr. scored 21 points in the first half to lead the Kings, and Brooks led the Suns with 17, scoring 12 points in the second quarter.

The Suns did not let off the gas in the third quarter as Booker found Ighodaro for the alley-oop on their first possession. Then Green got right to the rim for an open layup, and a Booker made midrange floater went in as he was attempting to foul bait, but no call came. Booker picked up a technical foul after he was stripped by Acuff Jr. at the rim after not getting the calls he is typically accustomed to getting. After a missed pull-up jump shot by Hunter, the Suns starters executed another flawless possession as Bridges was doubled in the post, and the ball swung around the perimeter, and Brooks knocked down another 3 to add to his total. Timeout Kings as the Suns led 78-69 with eight minutes left to play.

The Suns continued the onslaught on both ends, forcing back-to-back deflections that went out of bounds. However, LaVine and Acuff Jr. each made extremely difficult 3-point shots on each of those possessions. Undeterred, the Suns continued to play excellent defense that resulted in a Booker-to-Maluach alley-oop, a wide-open Booker layup in transition, and after forcing another stop, Brooks nailed another 3 to give the Suns an 87-74 lead.

The Suns starters did exactly what they needed to start the third quarter, and the bench unit plus Booker finished it off. The Suns found Maluach for a layup right out of the timeout as he continues to show his soft touch around the rim. The Suns continued to attack the rim and draw fouls as Gillespie and Booker each cashed in on free throws. The straw that finally broke the camel's back was a Ryan Dunn steal and aggressive dunk in transition to give the Suns a 20-point lead, 96-76, Kings timeout.

The Kings closed the quarter strong as Acuff Jr. continued to show his scoring prowess, getting to the line and knocking down a 3. The Suns led 99-84 after three quarters of play.

The Suns bench did not keep it going to start the fourth quarter as Kennard missed another wide-open 3. The Kings scored 6 quick points attacking the rim in transition while the Suns offense went cold for the first time all night. With the Kings down nine, Jordan Ott quickly called a timeout two minutes into the quarter to try and stop the Kings' run.

Out of the timeout, Green slipped a screen for Bridges and cut to the rim for an easy dunk for the Suns' first points of the quarter. The Kings responded quickly off of a poor pass from Brooks as LaVine scored easily in transition. The Suns, as they did all night long, continued to attack Acuff Jr. defensively, and it was Bridges' turn to isolate and get downhill and score. After an offensive foul on Monk, Green drove hard to the rim and missed the layup but got the offensive rebound; he quickly found Kennard in the corner, who swung it to Booker, then back to a cutting Kennard, who swung it to Brooks, and then to Green in the corner for a wide-open 3 that went in to give the Suns a 14-point lead.

LaVine and Acuff Jr. continued to keep the Kings in it as they each made difficult midrange jumpshots to cut the deficit back to 10. Finally, Kennard connected on a wide-open 3, and then got a stop on the other end. The Suns went right back to what had been working all night with Brooks in the post, and he shimmied his way free for a midrange fadeaway that was all net. Acuff Jr. finally started missing, and Booker took advantage, drawing a foul in transition and hitting both free throws; then on the next possession he cut backdoor for a wide-open layup with Bridges getting the assist from the mid-post. The Suns led 115-96 with 5:22 to play.

The Suns were not finished either, as they went right back to Brooks in the post against Acuff Jr., forcing a Kings double that resulted in another swing-swing-swing where Green knocked down another corner 3. The very next possession Brooks posted up Acuff Jr. again, and the Kings doubled again. This time Brooks spun baseline away from the double for the layup to score his 29th point of the night. The Kings could not stop the Suns' mid-post offense tonight, and it led to big games from Brooks and Green.

The Suns won 128-107, and for the first time this season are at .500 with a 7-7 record.

The Suns travel to Salt Lake City to face the Utah Jazz on Friday at 7:30 Arizona time.

#2888. Player Grades: Lakers vs. Jazz

After stringing together a series of great nights and victories, the Lakers finally saw their winning streak come to an end on Wednesday.

Not every night can be perfect and, unfortunately for the purple and gold, nearly every Laker had an off night at the same time. The result was a loss to a two-win Jazz team, making it the ugliest loss of the season — albeit a short list — right after one of the best wins of the year.

These types of performances happen in an 82-game season. The challenge will be learning and bouncing back from it. But it doesn't make it any less frustrating in the moment.

So, let's dive into the loss. As always, grades are based on expectations for each player. A "B" grade represents the average performance for that player.

21 minutes, 2 points, 1 rebound, 3 assists, 4 steals, 1 block, 1 turnover, 1-4 FG, 0-2 3PT, -12

Another great night defensively for Thybulle, but he's really testing the limits of how little he can do offensively and still be seen as a positive.

18 minutes, 7 points, 2 rebounds, 4 fouls, 1 turnover, 2-3 FG, 1-2 3PT, 2-2 FT, -23

Another night where he gets into foul trouble. It's not clear why he was moved back into the starting lineup but he didn't make a great impression to stay there.

30 minutes, 10 points, 11 rebounds, 1 assist, 2 fouls, 1 turnover, 3-7 FG, 4-10 FT, -7

In his first game against the Jazz, Kessler said he let his emotions get the best of him. It felt like that moment was in the rearview mirror and it wouldn't be a big thing playing them moving forward. Well, tonight was another bad game against them. Good thing the Lakers play them one more time next week and no more the rest of the season.

35 minutes, 20 points, 7 rebounds, 4 assists, 2 fouls, 1 turnover, 8-20 FG, 4-10 3PT, -10

It wasn't an efficient game from Grimes, but he was still one of the best players in purple and gold. If you're looking for a positive takeaway, though, the hesitation on what to do when the ball is swung to him is gone. Now he just has to make the shots.

36 minutes, 29 points, 5 rebounds, 7 assists, 2 steals, 3 fouls, 9 turnovers, 10-28 FG, 2-12 3PT, 7-7 FT, -18

I don't feel nearly as bad about giving Luka a bad grade here. Those audacious threes he made earlier in the year aren't falling and on a night when the Lakers needed some kind of life offensively, they felt especially costly when they missed.

20 minutes, 6 points, 3 rebounds, 1 foul, 1-10 FG, 0-4 3PT, 4-4 FT, -4

Mamu is in quite the nasty slump at this point, which is especially unfortunate considering it comes on the heels really seeming to have figured things out.

30 minutes, 4 points, 6 rebounds, 2 assists, 3 steals, 2 fouls, 1 turnover, 2-3 FG, -3

This was a fairly standard LaRavia game. He stuffed the stat sheet, turned down an open three and hustled around the floor.

12 minutes, 5 rebounds, 3 assists, 1 steal, 1 foul, 0-1 FG, 0-2 FT, -9

When the rest of the offense isn't working, Vando's lack of offense feels a bit more noticeable. It's unfair to Vando because he's just not going to provide much on that end, but the Lakers just had a host of wings who couldn't score on Wednesday.

It's also harder to have him on the court when you're playing from behind and need points.

19 minutes, 12 points, 1 assist, 2 fouls, 3 turnovers, 4-8 FG, 1-2 3PT, 3-3 FT, -9

This is becoming something of the norm for Sexton. He'll give you low double-digit points, get to the line a couple times and make a couple loud errors.

Thiero got one shift in the first half where he made a nice play on a steal and assist but did little else. The rest of the players only got in during garbage time.

The move from LaRavia to Williams in the starting lineup is a real head-scratcher. Williams played well in that role to start the year but nothing about LaRavia's recent play felt like it warranted being removed from the starting lineup.

It didn't lose the team the game as the players did that themselves. But it set them back.

Wednesday's inactives: Austin Reaves, Arthur Kaluma, Chris Mañon

#2884. Snubbed Bill Belichick a Pro Football Hall of Fame finalist again

Bill Belichick and Robert Kraft, who won six Super Bowls together with the New England Patriots, have advanced a step closer to reuniting in Canton as members of the Pro Football Hall of Fame's Class of 2027.

Both were denied earlier this year for the Hall's most recent class, and in the case of Belichick – a first-ballot finalist in 2026 and second to Don Shula for most career coaching victories (333) – the omission ignited a furor that prompted the Hall of Fame to overhaul its selection process.

Now they are back in the mix for a final round, with Belichick advancing over four others – Tom Coughlin, Mike Holmgren, Dan Reeves and Mike Shanahan (in alphabetical order) – in voting by the coaches subcommittee.

Kraft – one of the NFL's most powerful and accomplished team owners, and a key figure in striking massive media rights deals for the league and in negotiating the collective bargaining agreement with players that ended a lockout in 2011 – was forwarded again as the candidate in the contributor category. He was chosen over four others – Ralph Hay, Virginia McCaskey, Doug Williams and John Wooten (in alphabetical order) – by a separate contributors subcommittee.

The Hall's full selection committee will consider Belichick and Kraft as part of a slate that will include 13 former players, during an in-person meeting in the weeks before Super Bowl 61. The 15 candidates will be reduced to 10, then 7. At that point, any candidate receiving 80% approval would be elected to the Class of 2027.

The new class will be unveiled during the NFL Honors show in Los Angeles in February.

At this point, balloting to reduce the pool of players under consideration is ongoing, with the full selection committee – reduced this year from 50 to 25 (plus 3 alternates) – engaging in multiple reduction votes in the coming weeks.

The Hall's bylaws stipulate that between three and seven new Hall of Famers will be elected.

Time (and voting) will tell whether that class includes Belichick and Kraft.

This article originally appeared on USA TODAY: Snubbed Bill Belichick a Pro Football Hall of Fame finalist again

#2883. Anthropic IPO, AI wealth spawn a new, leaner kind of family office

A version of this article appeared in CNBC's Inside Wealth newsletter, a weekly guide to the high-net-worth investor and consumer. Sign up to receive future editions straight to your inbox.

The Anthropic IPO is expected to mint hundreds of centimillionaires and spur the creation of new investment vehicles to manage these windfalls.

Anthropic-related wealth could lead to 15 to 20 new single-family offices over the next three years, according to exclusive data provided to CNBC by Altss, a global private markets intelligence platform. Altss also estimates about 70 households related to the Anthropic wealth boom will start working with multifamily offices or outsourced family offices. Altss' CEO Dawid Siekiera added that the firm expects the launch of a flurry of other personal investment companies, venture funds and investor partnerships.

Lawyers and family-office consultants told CNBC that they have received inquiries from current and former Anthropic employees and early investors about potentially setting up their own firms. Many of these newly wealthy tech professionals are interested in leaner operations with few in-house staff and leveraging technology and outside consultants to manage their fortunes, they said.

"Why would they be building a traditional office when they can probably see inherently by where they built their wealth how to make it more efficient?" consultant Kirby Rosplock told CNBC. "I think there's a sense that they can do it smarter, and that may not mean having more headcount, but more horsepower."

Anthropic beneficiaries, like their peers in tech, tend to skew younger, with some even in their late 20s, experts told CNBC. They are typically more focused on hands-on investing than preserving generational wealth compared to traditional family office principals, according to Rosplock. (A Deloitte survey estimated the average age of family office principals at 68.)

"This is very different than having a multi-generational family business that is acquired by some other larger firm," she said. "These people are likely staying engaged in the space, wanting to take the next bite at the apple and continue to build wealth."

While these Silicon Valley clients often have better access to hot startups than traditional investment advisors, they typically aren't familiar with a broad range of asset classes, according to Chris Mays of Armanino, an accounting and consulting firm.

"When you look at the financial services folks, a lot of times they would build the investment function inside of their family offices. I don't see that as a likely outcome inside of these [tech] family offices," he said. "They're going to rely on third-party investment advisors or multi-family offices. They're not going to go and hire a buy side guy out of x, y, z hedge fund or private equity fund to run their money."

Jennifer Katrulya, CPA and partner at PKF O'Connor Davies, said she recommends relying on outside advisors rather than building out an in-house investment team.

"You're going to get the deeper expertise from the wider bench. The minute you try to hire someone for that expertise, unless you're investing in their growth, they're becoming stale every day after you hire them," said Katrulya, co-leader of the accounting and advisory firm's family office group.

For young, first-time wealth creators, a founder's office may be more appropriate than a traditional family office, according to partner William Kambas of the law firm Withers. Founder's offices often have similar legal infrastructure as family offices but are typically smaller, with fewer levels of management and a focus on assisting the principal with personal needs and investments.

"A lot of young entrepreneurs have ideas and they want the flexibility to execute on their vision," Kambas said. "A family office or a management office simply gets in the way because they're going to do it all themselves."

Michael Kosnitzky, a partner at Pillsbury Winthrop Shaw Pittman, said that the costs of a traditional family office aren't necessarily worth it to most Anthropic beneficiaries. He considers $200 million in investable assets to be the bare minimum for starting a family office and that it is still hard to justify with fewer than $500 million in assets.

Instead, he recommends a "starter family office," with a legal vehicle called a family limited partnership that holds investments. The family members hold limited-partner interests while a separate company, typically structured as a C-corporation, provides investment services, hires staff and coordinates advisors to the partnership. The setup confers many of the same tax benefits as a full-blown family office without the cost and complexity, he said.

Rosplock also encourages starting small, perhaps by hiring a chief of staff or executive assistant to coordinate outsourced services. She also cautioned that IPOs typically come with lockups and that high-flying tech stocks can stumble.

"Don't build the Taj Mahal of family office structures when you may have years potentially to realize the position," she said. "What we have to be very mindful of is that new wealth can be a flash in the pan if external things happen that we don't control. We see it all the time with IPOs."

The Inside Wealth newsletter by Robert Frank is your weekly guide to high-net-worth investors and the industries that serve them.

#2881. Top Wall Street analysts are confident about these 3 stocks for the long haul

Global stock markets have been under pressure as geopolitical tensions have resurfaced in the Middle East. Moreover, investors remain concerned about the sustainability of AI-driven demand and infrastructure spending.

Nonetheless, those looking for attractive stock picks amid the ongoing volatility can gain key insights by tracking the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts. These experts assign ratings after in-depth analysis of a company's fundamentals, growth opportunities, and risks.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

E-commerce and cloud computing giant Amazon (AMZN) is this week's first pick. Heading into the company's second-quarter earnings, TD Cowen analyst John Blackledge reiterated a buy rating on AMZN stock, citing strength in the Amazon Web Services cloud unit as well as the e-commerce and advertising businesses. The analyst lowered his price target on AMZN stock to $340 from $350 as he revised his estimates and slightly raised his capex projections.

Specifically, Blackledge expects Amazon to report revenue of $200.1 billion, 2% above the Street's consensus, driven by acceleration in AWS and advertising revenue. He also expects the company's e-commerce business to reflect the shifting of Prime Day in the U.S. and other key markets to the second quarter this year, compared with third quarter of last year.

In particular, Blackledge expects AWS revenue to grow 35.5% year-over-year in Q2 2026, marking an acceleration from 28.4% in the prior-year quarter and 3.4% above the Street's expectations. The 5-star analyst expects revenue to be driven by rising generative AI workloads as the company's significant AI infrastructure spending helps to ease supply constraints.

Regarding third-quarter outlook, Blackledge said, "Our rev and Op Income estimates are 0.3% and 3.2% above consensus, driven by further AWS revenue growth acceleration led by AI demand."

Blackledge ranks No. 771 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 11.2%. See Amazon Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving on to semiconductor company Marvell Technology (MRVL). Following several meetings with management, RBC Capital analyst Srini Pajjuri reiterated a buy rating on MRVL stock with a price target of $360.

"Overall, the meetings reinforced our conviction that MRVL can sustain 40%+ growth for the next 3 years, driven by strong AI demand, optical connectivity leadership, and expanding Custom pipeline," said Pajjuri.

The 5-star analyst added that robust demand and limited supply are giving greater revenue visibility. Pajjuri noted that Marvell's data center business is on track to deliver more than 50% growth this year and next. Also, the growth in the company's networking business is outpacing compute, driven by agentic AI and inferencing workloads.

Meanwhile, Pajjuri noted that optical product lead times have extended to more than six months, while XPU customers are placing purchase orders 12 months in advance. While the analyst kept his estimates unchanged, he sees the possibility of upside for the second half of 2026 from the Optical business, with more significant upside potential for 2027 and 2028 estimates.

Furthermore, Pajjuri noted that Marvell's scale-across offering is emerging as an additional growth catalyst for 2027, while scale-up networking is expected to present a multibillion-dollar greenfield serviceable addressable market. Also, management is upbeat about Marvell's custom business, with the company targeting more than $10 billion in revenue for 2028, driven by existing programs with Amazon's AWS and Microsoft and multiple XPU attach wins.

Pajjuri ranks No. 88 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 75% of the time, delivering an average return of 51.5%. See Marvell Options Activity on TipRanks.

Chipmaker Advanced Micro Devices (AMD) is scheduled to announce its second-quarter earnings on Aug. 4. Shares have seen a strong jump year-to-date due to demand for the company's AI GPUs and server CPUs.

Ahead of second-quarter earnings, Wells Fargo analyst Aaron Rakers reaffirmed a buy rating on AMD stock and raised his price target to $615 from $505, citing "increasing focus on path to +$20/sh. EPS in CY28." The analyst expects AMD to reiterate its confidence in the MI450 series and Helios ramp beginning in the third quarter of 2026.

The 5-star analyst increased his estimates for AMD server CPU revenue to $16.0 billion (up 68% year-over-year), $20.5 billion (up 28%), and $25.0 billion (up 22%) for 2026, 2027, 2028, respectively. Rakers noted that in the previous quarter, AMD increased its server CPU total addressable market estimates to $120 billion by 2030, representing a more than 35% compound annual growth rate.

Rakers expects AMD to comment on an additional rise in server CPU demand since its first-quarter results. He sees upside driven by agentic AI demand momentum, cloud demand, and traditional enterprise modernization. In this regard, the analyst highlighted that Micron increased its 2026 server shipment guidance recently. Also, checks indicate continued upside to the average selling price.

Meanwhile, Rakers' data center GPU estimates remain above consensus at $15.6 billion, $40.6 billion, and $63.0 billion for 2026, 2027, and 2028, respectively, while estimates for the client and gaming businesses are below the Street's consensus. Overall, the analyst projects EPS of $7.15, $13.40, and $18.75 for 2026, 2027, and 2028, respectively.

Rakers ranks No. 5 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 73% of the time, delivering an average return of 56.8%. See AMD Insider Trading Activity on TipRanks.

#2878. Utah Jazz vs. Los Angeles Lakers: Recap and Final Score

The 2-13 Utah Jazz knock off the 13-2 Lakers, who had the best record in the NBA heading into tonight, with a final score of 109-97. What a huge win for this Jazz team!

The Lakers were missing Austin Reaves, but the Jazz were also missing key players in Lauri Markkanen, Jaren Jackson Jr, and Jusuf Nurkic.

The first half was a little bit of a rollercoaster. Utah's offense was looking smooth and the defense was solid. Svi Mykhailiuk finally had a good shooting night, hitting 4 threes in the first half, although he wasn't great on defense and struggled with turnovers. Darryn Peterson struggled against the Lakers full court pressure, and had some early foul trouble which took him out of the game for most of the first half.

Although Luka Doncic looked unstoppable, getting to the rim and drawing fouls whenever he felt like it, Keyonte George fueled Utah's 2-point lead heading into halftime. Doncic must be so annoying to officiate. He gets plenty of foul calls but still argues endlessly over every little detail.

Doncic finished the half with 22 points and 3 rebounds, and George had 18 points and 6 assists.

In the third quarter, the Lakers turned up the intensity, flying around for loose balls and forcing Jazz turnovers. The Jazz were extremely sloppy, but managed to calm down and take back control.

Ace Bailey continued his shooting slump, but still found ways to contribute to winning basketball. His defense and rebounding have been so impactful this year, not to mention how he has become a great connective passer.

Utah held onto their 2-point lead heading into the fourth. The Jazz weren't even particularly playing well, but still managed to be ahead. However, the game was far from over, and Doncic is inevitable. Or maybe he isn't?

With the game tied at 83 a piece, Utah went on a 15-0 run to essentially put the game away. After committing 21 turnovers in the first three quarters, Utah took care of the ball in the fourth, only committing two.

Doncic couldn't get going, finishing the game with 9 turnovers and hitting only 2/12 from three. You've got to give a lot of credit to Josh Green, who not only scored an efficient 11 points, but also held Luka to just 7 points in the second half.

Darryn Peterson had an okay night for his standards, scoring 11 points. It's too bad he could only play 18 minutes because of the foul trouble. Hopefully the knee injury isn't bothering him too much anymore. His potential is through the roof.

The unsung hero from this game was Mo Bamba, who finished the game with 6 points, 16 rebounds and 2 blocks. Shout out to Walker Kessler ($32.5M AAV) for getting outplayed by Mo Bamba ($3.5M AAV) and shooting 40% at the charity stripe!

Mo Bamba and Josh Green are both looking like great moves by the front office. Utah would've been in a very dark place without their play this season.

Ace Bailey finished with 19 points and 8 rebounds.

Keyonte George had his second straight 30-points, 10+ assists game, shooting 9/17 from the field and 5/10 from distance. He was undoubtedly the best player on the floor, and deserves to be in All-Star considerations.

Just two games ago, this team was looking distraught and desperate. The Jazz defense has been good, and just held the Lakers, one of the best offenses in the league, to 97 points. It also looks like they're beginning to figure out the offense.

Now they've come out of LA with two wins, and a lot of confidence. When Jaren and Lauri come back, things could get very interesting.

Utah plays Phoenix at home on Friday, November 20th. Can they extend the winning streak to three?

#2876. Denmark ratifies Trump security deal over Greenland after months of diplomatic tensions

Danish lawmakers on Thursday approved a security agreement with U.S. President Donald Trump covering Greenland, seeking to end months of strained diplomatic tensions over the island's future.

Trump and the prime ministers of Denmark and Greenland signed the agreement in September. It allows Washington to expand its military presence on the island, paving the way for two new bases and the construction of a "Golden Dome" missile defense system.

Thursday's approval follows a protracted period in which Trump repeatedly threatened to take control of the self-governing Danish territory. Tensions reached fever pitch in January as the president repeatedly insisted that the U.S. needed Greenland for its national security, at times declining to rule out using military force to acquire it.

A parliamentary tally showed 109 Danish lawmakers unanimously backed the agreement on Thursday, per Reuters. Greenland's parliament ratified the deal earlier this month. It is not subject to a vote in the U.S. Senate.

The Danish government can now formally notify the U.S. that both parliaments have consented to complete the ratification process.

The deal supplements an existing 1951 defense agreement. It also maintains Danish sovereignty and Greenlanders' right to self-determination.

Trump's proposed Golden Dome is a multibillion-dollar initiative designed to shield the U.S. from a range of aerial and missile threats. It was rolled out in May last year and is often compared with Israel's "Iron Dome" system.

Trump's threats alarmed Greenlanders and other Europeans and opened a rift between the U.S. and NATO, of which Denmark and the U.S. are both members. Greenland has maintained that it is not for sale, and the European Union earlier this year united to rebuke Trump.

— CNBC's Kevin Breuninger & Anniek Bao contributed to this report.

#2875. Top Wall Street analysts suggest these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

The ongoing earnings season, investor concerns about the durability of AI demand and spending, and geopolitical risks are key factors that have been contributing to stock market volatility in recent trading sessions.

In this scenario, investors seeking steady income can consider adding dividend stocks to their portfolios. Recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help them pick attractive dividend stocks that are backed by solid cash flows to support consistent payments.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Oil and gas exploration and production company ConocoPhillips is this week's first dividend pick. With a dividend of 84 cents per share (annualized dividend of $3.36 per share), COP offers a dividend yield of 3%. The company is scheduled to announce its second-quarter results on Aug. 6.

Ahead of second-quarter results, Wells Fargo analyst Sam Margolin reiterated a buy rating on COP stock with a price target of $183. Despite the pressure on oil prices from an increase in OPEC production quota, the analyst finds ConocoPhillips and Shell stocks appealing as the earnings season approaches. He cited their operational visibility and resilience as factors backing their appeal.

The 5-star analyst expects ConocoPhillips to meet its production guidance of 2.2 million barrels of oil equivalent per day at the mid-point. He expects lower Waha natural gas prices in the Permian Basin to be offset by stronger Brent crude premiums. Margolin expects capital expenditure to remain within COP's prior guided range of $12.2 billion annualized, with no significant impact on spending on the Northfield East project in Qatar despite the Strait of Hormuz disruption.

Overall, Margolin expects COP to generate about $3.5 billion in free cash flow (before working capital) and earnings per share of $2.94. He expects continued strength in COP's free cash flow and regular dividend growth through the completion of the Willow project in 2028/2029. Prior to the Willow project coming online, the analyst expects free cash flow to grow by about $2 billion in 2027 and 2028, assuming Brent crude averages around $60 per barrel.

"COP's track record of capital efficiency and strong Permian well productivity underpins its ability to pursue long-cycle developments," said Margolin.

Margolin ranks No. 457 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 70% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.3%. See ConocoPhillips Financials on TipRanks.

Energy Transfer is a limited partnership that operates 140,000 miles of pipeline and associated energy infrastructure. With a quarterly cash distribution of 33.75 cents per common unit ($1.35 per unit on an annualized basis), ET offers a yield of 6.8%.

Heading into Energy Transfer's Q2 earnings on Aug. 4, Jefferies analyst Julien Dumoulin-Smith reaffirmed a buy rating on ET stock with a price target of $23. The analyst noted that his adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization estimate of $4.46 billion is 1% below the Street's consensus of $4.49 billion.

The 5-star analyst noted that Energy Transfer has slightly outperformed Enterprise Products Partners recently. However, it still trades at a relative discount of 19% compared to EPD, which is below its historical discount range of 17%-20%. Smith believes that ET stock could be re-rated higher if the company provides a clearer long-term strategy for natural gas growth.

Furthermore, Smith expects the current energy market to support a stronger outlook for natural gas liquids and crude oil. "The current energy macro backdrop positions ET to benefit in all three commodities," said the analyst.

He expects Energy Transfer's adjusted EBITDA to grow at a 4.8% compound annual growth rate in 2027-2030, which is 1%-3% above Wall Street's expectations. In fact, Smith sees the possibility of additional upside if ET announces more natural gas projects. He added that investors will await details on final investment decisions on new natural gas projects and any clues about additional projects in the pipeline. The analyst noted that ET has announced new gas projects consistently in recent quarters.

Smith ranks No. 550 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.4%. See Energy Transfer Statistics on TipRanks.

Finally, let's look at energy giant Chevron, which is scheduled to announce its second-quarter results on July 31. Last month, the company paid a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share. At an annualized dividend of $7.12, CVX offers a dividend yield of 3.92%.

Ahead of Q2 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on Chevron stock and lowered his price target to $216 from $236. Byrne expects the company to report adjusted EPS of about $5.86 per share, nearly 9% above the Street's expectations.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the challenges seen in Chevron's upstream business in the first quarter due to the disruption at the Tengizchevroil joint venture in Kazakhstan, Storm Fern downtime, and the Middle East conflict have largely been resolved. Consequently, Byrne expects production to recover in the second quarter to about 4,033 mboepd. He expects the upstream business to generate adjusted earnings of about $8.1 billion in Q2 2026.

Meanwhile, Byrne expects Chevron to generate downstream adjusted earnings of about $4.4 billion in Q2, with strength in both domestic and international markets. The downstream business benefited from higher crack spreads and strong refining performance.

Additionally, the analyst expects Chevron to generate $18.2 billion in cash flow from operations (before working capital changes), driven by stronger earnings and about $2.2 billion in dividends from affiliated companies. Unlike the first quarter, Chevron is not expected to make a TCO loan repayment in Q2, providing an additional boost to cash flow.

Byrne ranks No. 409 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 56% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.5%. See Chevron Ownership Structure on TipRanks.
