#2873. Seahawks All-22 Review: An unsung hero stands out in Raiders game

The Seattle Seahawks got the win, but there isn't much to celebrate when you consider the level of competition against the Las Vegas Raiders. Still, this victory was important. It gives the Seahawks some breathing room heading into the bye week, allowing them to get healthy and prepare for the final stretch of the season.

There was good, there was bad, and there are still plenty of questions to answer.

The first half against the Raiders exposed some concerning issues with Mike Macdonald's defense. Even against a rookie quarterback making his first career start, Seattle took far too long to make him uncomfortable. Context matters here: Las Vegas entered the game with the NFL's 31st-ranked rushing offense, averaging just 71.3 yards per game. The Raiders also lost Brock Bowers, one of their most important offensive weapons, during the first half.

Fernando Mendoza completed his first seven passes. His first incompletion didn't come until the second quarter. Seattle didn't record its first sack until late in the first half, when Devon Witherspoon brought him down after Mendoza had already attempted 12 passes.

At halftime, the rookie was 11-of-13 for 90 passing yards, with 21 rushing yards and a rushing touchdown. He had no interceptions. To make matters worse, the Raiders converted seven of their ten third-down attempts, a 70% conversion rate.

One thing that helped Mendoza was Seattle's tendency to overcommit to the run despite facing an offense with such poor rushing production. That opened the door for Klint Kubiak's familiar play-action concepts, taking pressure off the quarterback and creating opportunities for the tight ends. On the play above, both tight ends end up wide open.

Fernando Mendoza pulled off one of the most memorable plays in College Football Playoff history with a quarterback draw in the national title game. Seattle wasn't prepared for this look. The Raiders spread the defense out of an empty formation, forcing a man-coverage look. Murphy executes a stunt, but Reed is supposed to be the looper and fails to fulfill his assignment, making life easier for the offense as it attacks Ernest Jones. Okada also takes a poor angle and ends up getting screened by his own teammate.

One area I've found particularly concerning is Seattle's intermediate coverage. The defense has repeatedly struggled against dig routes attacking the space behind the linebackers. Here, the Seahawks are bailed out by Derick Hall winning with pure power and getting to the quarterback.

If we assume Bradford has settled into a backup role, Seattle essentially played with three backup offensive linemen after Bobby Hart was forced into action. The offensive line continued to show recurring execution and communication problems.

Maxx Crosby adjusts his alignment before the snap, and the communication between Zabel and Jones breaks down. Crosby gets a free path into the backfield and makes the tackle. These issues with identifying pressure, adjusting protection, and coordinating assignments at the second level have become recurring problems.

Josh Jones doesn't seem to recognize the play being called here. He initially blocks alongside Zabel before releasing to the second level as if this were a running play. That mistake wipes out what should have been a big reception for JSN.

Bobby Hart was thrown into a difficult situation, but considering his recent level of play, things could have been much worse. Once again, the Raiders use their pre-snap alignment changes to move Crosby around. Hart fails to adjust his angle and allows Crosby to penetrate the backfield.

As mentioned earlier, these aren't just isolated individual mistakes. There are broader execution problems across the unit. Hart fails to create any movement and gets driven backward. Sundell climbs to the second level but gets caught between linebackers No. 7 and No. 17, ultimately blocking neither. Bradford also gets badly beaten, and the play ends in a tackle for loss. On the left side, Zabel and Jones don't execute their assignments particularly well either.

It's worth mentioning that there were some genuinely good run-blocking reps, even if they came against the Raiders. All three tight ends deliver excellent blocks, particularly Kallerup and Barner. Josh Jones seals the edge and even helps Zabel. Sundell delivers one of his best blocks of the season, climbing to the linebacker and taking him to the ground.

This section is essentially a good-and-bad breakdown, because Fleury could easily appear in either category. The offensive coordinator was extremely conservative and methodical, failing to consistently challenge a bad defense in the first half. Seattle went into halftime with just seven points.

Route spacing continues to be a recurring issue. Watch where the receivers are positioned as Lock begins to leave the pocket. Kupp and Shaheed are occupying the middle of the field, while JSN and Barner are near the sideline. They're effectively crowding the same areas and shrinking the quarterback's options.

Here we get a look at a page from Klint Kubiak's playbook: the Yankee concept. It's a deep-shot play-action design featuring two vertical routes while the rest of the offense stays in protection. Kubiak used this concept frequently, but Fleury hasn't shown the same willingness to call it.

JSN's route is excellent, and the safety gets held in place by the threat of Shaheed. As for the alleged OPI: if this is offensive pass interference, Mike Evans, DeAndre Hopkins, and plenty of other receivers wouldn't have the production they do.

Seattle has also been using JSN out of the backfield. The play design could be better, but aligning him there makes it harder for the defense to double-cover him and complicates the leverage and angles for defenders #3 and #31. The inside-breaking man-coverage routes should create space for JSN. And when he gets his one-on-one matchup, he does what he does best: wins and picks up the crucial first down.

This was easily one of the most encouraging plays of the game. Lock manipulates the Raiders' defense, and Seattle clearly wants to run the ball in this situation. He keeps it, takes advantage of Crosby's aggressive upfield rush, and finds a clear path to the first down. It's reminiscent of Peyton Manning's famous naked bootleg touchdown against the Cowboys.

Lock may have made his first truly costly mistakes of the season, with two turnovers that had a direct impact on the game.

The defense doesn't respect the play-action fake, and nobody bites. Instead, defenders immediately pursue the quarterback. Abe Lucas is slow to recognize the blitz, allowing a Raiders defender to pressure Lock. Shaheed is open on the dig route, with JSN creating space for his teammate.

Lock needs to climb the pocket, but it collapses around him. This is a situation where he simply needs to eat the sack and move on to the next play. It's the opening drive; there's no reason to force something. This was the most Sam Darnold-esque play possible.

Not long after his first turnover, Lock delivers a huge touchdown. The play design is questionable for a long third down, with the routes initially breaking well short of the sticks.

Lock buys time, and this is where you see the importance of Kupp's mentality. He never gives up on the play, while No. 5 momentarily does. Lock keeps his eyes downfield, resists the temptation to scramble, and eventually delivers the long touchdown.

This appears to be the same concept Seattle used on the touchdown against the Chargers. It's a heavy formation, and the quarterback should have the option to throw to Horton. The receiver is running an option route between a fade and a slant.

This should have been a touchdown, but Lock's dropback is too far and Charbonnet's action aren't synchronized. Seattle misses the opportunity and ends up committing its second turnover.

This is a good play call on third-and-three. The Raiders appear to be playing a Tampa 2-Invert look, which they had shown several times earlier.

Lock has JSN available for the first down, with Shaheed sitting a little deeper. However, JSN slips, and Lock holds onto the ball. The receiver improvises and ends up isolated against a linebacker in space, an ideal matchup.

The QB delivers an excellent throw, understanding that the linebacker cannot afford to turn his back and look for the ball. Doing so would be fatal to the coverage.

Seattle's defense responded much better after halftime. After allowing 136 net yards in the first half, the Seahawks limited the Raiders to just 27 net yards after the break. They recorded four sacks, forced five three-and-outs, and held Las Vegas to 0-for-7 on third down.

The pass rush finally started making Mendoza uncomfortable, taking away his ability to operate freely and making it much harder for the Raiders to sustain drives.

The one major blemish was a significant coverage bust. In the fourth quarter, Mendoza found Ashton Jeanty on a short pass over the middle, and the running back turned it into a 38-yard gain, all the way to Seattle's 15-yard line. That single play accounted for more yardage than the Raiders' entire net offensive production in the second half. Remove that gain, and Las Vegas finishes the half with negative 11 net yards.

Seattle's run defense showed a very different level of discipline. Murphy wins his matchup quickly, and Nick Emmanwori, who had an outstanding game, isn't displaced by the tight end. He disengages, adjusts his angle, and makes the immediate tackle without allowing extra yards, with Ernest Jones aggressively closing in as well.

Seven defenders show along the line of scrimmage. Seattle drops its defensive tackles into the middle of the field, a Mike Macdonald staple designed to take away the short passing windows while giving the free rusher—in this case, Fowler—time to get home.

Okada also works as a late blitzer after Michael Mayer stays in to block.

For me, this was Macdonald's best defensive call of the game. Seattle showed this overload blitz to one side of the formation several times, including on the snap discussed above. The idea is to force the offensive line to slide toward the side showing the most threats.

But the pressure actually comes from the opposite side, with Spoon doing an excellent job disguising his intentions. Nobody picks up Witherspoon, while the five offensive linemen are left dealing with the two defensive tackles attacking the interior gaps and creating a free path for the blitz.

It's not unusual to see Spoon used as a blitzer. What is unusual is seeing him come from an outside alignment rather than his usual nickel position. That catches the offensive line—and even the running back, who is supposed to provide additional protection—completely off guard.

Another excellent job disguising the pressure before the snap.

I wanted to use this section to highlight Kallerup's work. He finished the game injured, and the snap above appears to show something around his ankle. According to Mike Macdonald, however, it wasn't anything serious.

His impact doesn't show up in the box score. This play would have been an opportunity for him to make a statistical contribution, but Lock went with his first and safer read, Barner.

He's crucial in helping Barner initially handle Crosby (Barner executes the block very well). Kallerup then works outside, drives his defender backward, and creates space for the running back to gain yardage.

He does something similar to what he did on Emmanuel Wilson's touchdown against the Chiefs. He helps take down the defensive tackle before continuing to look for work, climbing to linebacker Quay Walker and creating a running lane for a solid gain.

It wasn't a pretty win, and it certainly wasn't a mistake-free performance. The opponent was weak, so ultimately the most important thing was getting the victory and heading into the bye week with some breathing room to recover injured players and prepare for the final stretch.

The next game against the San Francisco 49ers is arguably the defining matchup of the season. A loss would essentially eliminate Seattle's remaining hopes of winning the division and make the Wild Card race considerably more difficult.

The focus now has to be on using the bye week properly. We have the blueprint for making life miserable for a rookie quarterback, but the first-half issues we saw against the Raiders could become much more costly against better teams.

#2869. Trump, Xi, Putin said they plan to boost oil production, revamp UN after trilateral talks

The U.S., China and Russia on Thursday vowed to boost production of petroleum products and reaffirmed their commitment to reducing nuclear risks following their leaders' historic trilateral summit.

President Donald Trump, Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin "had an in-depth exchange of views on the current international situation and committed to maintaining strategic stability and international security," the countries said in identical statements shared after the meeting in Shenzhen, China.

The statements were brief and broad, containing few details about what the leaders agreed on during their talks. They did not include any mention of Russia's war against Ukraine, which continues to rage in eastern Europe despite recent U.S. efforts to broker a ceasefire. They also made no reference to the U.S.' war in Iran, in which Russia and China have both been accused of aiding Tehran.

"The three countries plan to increase the production and supply of refined petroleum products for the benefit of all nations. They reaffirmed the importance of stable energy flows," according to the statements, described as a "Heads of State Declaration toward Peace."

The three presidents also "reaffirmed that a nuclear war cannot be won and must never be fought," and committed to a "dialogue on reducing nuclear risks and preventing the proliferation of nuclear weapons."

And they committed to "reform" the United Nations so it "can respond more effectively to global challenges and conform to its fundamental mission of international peace and security," according to the statements.

Trump holds talks separately with Putin and Xi ahead of their historic trilateral meeting

Takaichi gets chilly reception in China on first Japan leader visit since 2019

I watched Trump, Xi and Putin gather in China. Here are my top takeaways

Why the Middle East is watching the Trump-Xi-Putin summit

Trump arrives in China for historic meeting with Xi, Putin

China's Xi calls for removing trade and investment barriers

Putin says EU is losing industry, falling behind competition

Three things to watch as Trump, Xi and Putin meet in China

The leaders also agreed that "the International Criminal Court and other similar bodies must not abuse their jurisdiction," the declarations stated. The Trump administration has heavily criticized the ICC, and Secretary of State Marco Rubio last month announced new U.S. sanctions against what he called a "rogue court."

In opening remarks earlier Thursday, Trump said the U.S., Russia and China are "uniquely positioned" to reduce the world's nuclear weapons stockpile.

"As the world's three largest nuclear powers by far, the United States, Russia and China are uniquely positioned to discuss how to reduce the number of nuclear weapons on earth and also to lessen the risk that those weapons will ever be used," Trump said.

"The power is unbelievable, unthinkable," he said, adding that it's "a very hard thing" for countries to reduce nuclear weapons.

That would include not letting Iran have nuclear weapons, Trump said.

He said the three leaders would also discuss the war between Russia and Ukraine, and criticized "rogue globalist organizations," including the International Criminal Court.

The closely watched meeting lasted about an hour and a half late Thursday afternoon China time, following a working lunch between Trump and Xi that started at least an hour later than originally planned.

Trump told Xi and Putin they were "really making history" with the first-ever trilateral meeting between the three countries' leaders.

He said it was an "honor" to be at the meeting with Putin and Xi, whom he congratulated on a "very successful APEC summit." The three were among the world leaders attending the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit.

"Too often past leaders have avoided talking to nations with whom they had disagreements," Trump added. "We feel the opposite."

At the trilateral, Xi said that for large nations, having good interactions is not a choice but a necessity, according to a translation by state media.

In the report, Xi noted China's partnership with Russia, work towards constructive strategic stability in U.S. relations, and called on the three countries to respond to the world's challenges.

Xi laid out four "correct" ways for large countries to interact: mutual respect, peaceful coexistence, win-win cooperation, and adherence to dialogue, according to state media.

The Chinese president echoed Trump's comments on nuclear weapons, calling for such arms to be comprehensively prohibited and destroyed, adding that a nuclear war cannot be won and must never be fought.

Putin said Russia, China and the U.S. can strengthen global stability if they cooperate constructively, calling for the three leaders to discuss energy security and global and regional issues — including the war in Ukraine.

Beijing only confirmed the closely watched meeting on Monday. The three leaders had briefly gathered Wednesday evening as part of a welcome banquet for the APEC leaders more broadly.

Major indexes remain volatile as investors assess key earnings releases and geopolitical tensions in the Middle East. Investors with a long-term horizon should look beyond the ongoing noise and focus on stocks that are well-positioned to generate strong returns.

In this regard, recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist stocks with robust growth opportunities. These experts conduct an in-depth analysis of all the macro and micro factors affecting a company, focusing on its long-term growth potential.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

CrowdStrike Holdings is a cloud-native cybersecurity provider. The company recently announced an expanded partnership with Schwarz Digits to bring its Falcon platform to European enterprises.

After hosting several European investor meetings with the CrowdStrike CFO, Stifel analyst Adam Borg reiterated a buy rating on CRWD stock and increased his price target to $230 from $220.

The five-star analyst said that the meetings increased his confidence about CrowdStrike being an "important, well-positioned cybersecurity platform vendor that is an AI beneficiary given its expansive portfolio, unique data set, and ability to both secure AI and use AI to improve security."

Among the key takeaways, Borg noted that AI is driving a major shift in cybersecurity demand as it presents software vulnerabilities that can be exploited by less sophisticated hackers with capabilities that were once limited to government-backed hacking groups. This has led to companies making cybersecurity a top priority, driving customer interest in CrowdStrike's new AI Detection & Response solution and its broader security platform.

Borg added that AI-driven demand is expanding CrowdStrike's pipeline and supporting confidence in the company's higher fiscal 2027 net new annual recurring revenue guidance issued last quarter. Overall, Borg believes that CrowdStrike has multiple growth drivers that can help maintain revenue growth in at least the high-teens and drive improved profitability in the years ahead.

Borg ranks No. 651 among more than 12,300 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 65% of the time, delivering an average return of 15%. See CrowdStrike Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving on to AST SpaceMobile, a company building a global cellular broadband network in space. Recently, Piper Sandler analyst Alexander Potter initiated coverage of space-related stocks. Finally, let's look at Broadcom, a provider of custom AI chips and networking solutions. Recently, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reiterated a buy rating on Broadcom stock with a price target of $502.

The five-star analyst is surprised by the underperformance of AVGO stock so far this year compared to its peers, especially given continued momentum in AI-driven demand. Moore cited concerns that competitor MediaTek could gain market share from Broadcom in Google's tensor processing unit chip business and investor preference for faster-growing AI chip names as reasons for the underperformance.

While Moore acknowledged that MediaTek's role in Google's TPU business is growing, he contends that it is unlikely to disrupt Broadcom's position significantly. He expects Broadcom to continue to be the major TPU supplier, with about 80% market share. "AVGO should retain majority share, sustain strong AI growth, and remain a core compute winner, close behind NVDA," said Moore.

The analyst explained that his bullish outlook on Broadcom is based on the company's solid advantage in high-bandwidth memory supply, chip packaging capabilities, and large-scale production. Moore also highlighted AVGO's dominance in AI ASIC chips, strong networking business, and new customer wins.

#2867. Attrition finally gets best of Lakers in loss to Jazz

LOS ANGELES — The Lakers' league-leading nine-game win streak came to an end in Wednesday's 109-97 loss to the Utah Jazz.

Following a stretch where they seemed unaffected by streaky opponents or a grueling schedule, things finally caught up to them. Legs were heavy, shots came up short, and the Lakers succumbed to an ongoing war of attrition early this season.

Los Angeles had its worst offensive game of the season, scoring just 97 points — the lowest since putting up 95 points in the loss to Golden State that ignited its streak — on 34-for-89 (38.2%) shooting from the field and 9-for-33 (27.3%) from range.

"Frankly, you're going to play 82 games, you're going to have some poor shooting nights," head coach JJ Redick said postgame. "We'll take a look at everything, how we can be better offensively."

Redick and the Lakers refused to make an excuse for fatigue, but it evidently affected the way they played.

"Who in here feels like they played well tonight," Redick said he asked the team postgame. No one raised their hand, including Luka Dončić, who has been bearing the weight of the offense all season, which becomes even heavier when Austin Reaves is out.

Dončić's 11-game streak of 30-point games came to an end as the league's scoring leader scored just 29 points on 28 shot attempts, making just two of his 12 3-point attempts. He also tacked on nine turnovers.

"[Fatigue] can't be an excuse when all NBA teams play 82 games," Dončić said. "I got to do way better. I know that."

It's the balance the Lakers have to live by when they roster two high-usage guards that are critical to their success. Redick pointed to a stretch Reaves endured last season similar to the one Dončić is on now.

In December on the road against Toronto while Dončić was in Slovenia, Reaves scored 44 points and willed the Lakers to a 123-120 win. The next game, in Boston with both Dončić and LeBron James out, Reaves put up another 36 points in a 126-105 loss. Dončić and James returned to the court the following game in Philadelphia, but by then, Reaves was gassed. He scored just 11 points and made 3 of 16 shot attempts.

"The shot creation that is required from [Dončić], it takes its toll," Redick said. "That's part of it when Austin's out, for sure. … That's the luxury of having two really elite creators, and obviously when they're both in the lineup, it makes things a little bit easier."

At the expense of creating the excuse that Redick and Dončić and the rest of the roster wouldn't give the time of day, Wednesday's game against Utah was the Lakers' third in four nights and came the day after an 8 p.m. tip-off in a game that went to overtime. Even Quentin Grimes, who remains the hottest shooter on the team, needed 20 shots to score 20 points the night after scoring 47 on 24 attempts against the Hornets.

Los Angeles' season has been magical. Their 13-3 record is still tied with the 76ers for the best in the NBA. But, finally, for at least one night, the Lakers couldn't avoid the inevitable.

#2863. Gold's bad year is getting worse. Why its price keeps falling

Precious metals investors can be forgiven if they feel like the past year has given them whiplash.

Following a torrid run, which saw the price of gold hit new records 53 times in 2025, gold hit a historic high of $5,589.38 an ounce on Jan. 28, only to take an almost equally historic nosedive. On Wednesday, gold fell below $3,800 for the first time since September 2025, roughly a third below its peak.

A convergence of factors pushed gold to historic highs earlier this year, according to Suki Cooper, global head of commodities research at Standard Chartered, the London-based multinational bank. Now a different convergence is bringing the price down.

"Gold can behave as a commodity or a currency," Cooper said.

Which role it plays depends on the factors dominating the market and the psyche of investors, and right now, it's the currency argument that is bringing the precious metal down.

Cooper said gold's climb began with strong physical demand that started in 2022 and raised the floor under prices. A second phase followed as investors rethought gold's place in their portfolios. With the cost of holding a non-yielding asset falling, they started thinking about the metal for the longer term. That, Cooper said, marked a turnaround.

Until early 2025, Cooper said, long-term investors, especially those in gold-backed exchange-traded funds, had been cutting their exposure, which effectively added supply to the market. Last year, that reversed: ETF holdings, led by the SPDR Gold Shares (GLD), rose by roughly 800 tons and helped absorb the market's excess supply.

"There was a lot of one-way interest, a lot of appetite to buy but not to sell," Cooper said.

With selling in the secondary market staying muted, prices kept rising. By the end of 2025, net ETF holdings had passed 4,000 tons. Globally, gold ETFs had their strongest year ever for inflows, $89 billion, led by North America, according to the World Gold Council, with assets more than doubling to $559 billion in total.

A weaker dollar, lower bond yields and worries about global debt "set the foundation for gold to hit highs in 2026," Cooper said.

Investors who had been under-exposed to the metal kept buying.

Evan Mills, a financial advisor with Scholar Financial Advising, a fee-only firm in Winston-Salem, N.C., said the lower interest rates, expectations of further rate cuts and a weakening dollar, were all reasons for investors to favor gold.

"It was a perfect spot to be in if you were an investor in gold and commodities as a whole," Mills said. And investors did extremely well, especially with lower interest rates. "Cash doesn't do well in that environment, so a lot of people looked toward other places to hold their cash and maintain purchasing power," Mills said.

The speed of the rally made some investors uneasy, and there was growing talk that the Fed could tighten. Then, Cooper noted, the Middle East conflict broke out and gold changed roles. It stopped acting as a portfolio safe haven and became "a liquidity provider."

Cooper explained that because it was liquid and easy to sell, investors sold it to raise cash, which set off the first correction. Since then, she said, markets have been pricing in what the Fed will do.

"In 2026, the Fed has leaned more toward higher interest rates," Mills said.

This week, the Federal Reserve Board of Governors member Christopher Waller said a 50-basis point rate hike may be necessary to "shock people."

"There are higher Treasury yields and a strengthening dollar, so the opportunity cost of holding an asset that doesn't produce income is much higher," Mills said.

Mills said that in the current situation, gold investors are missing out on an environment in which cash actually does well, with money market funds that can pay 4% to 5%.

"Cash maintains purchasing power, and you don't need to put it in an illiquid asset or one where you're relying on another person to see the value," Mills said.

From 2025 to 2026, gold didn't become any less scarce; the alternatives to owning it became more attractive.

"Stocks, bonds, the classic portfolio mix, all became a lot more attractive, so people looked at the opportunity cost and decided it wasn't worth holding gold anymore," Mills said.

This has been the case for other metals in 2026 as well, with silver plummeting from an all-time high price.

After an extraordinary rally, it's easy to start questioning gold. Mills said investors should start by remembering that expectations were extremely high. "When expectations are 20% or 30% returns in a year, then of course when returns drop, people start to panic," Mills said.

Panic shouldn't be the watchword for gold investors, but caution should remain.

"In the short term, with real yields going up and the dollar likely strengthening with future Fed hikes, I'd be cautious about gold in the overall portfolio," Mills said.

For investors, the key is to understand why gold is in your portfolio to begin with.

"If you're looking for it to be a short-term purchase, then gold likely doesn't belong in your portfolio at all," Mills said. If the metal is seen as a hedge, gold can remain a valuable part of a portfolio, but it can still be a poor investment depending on the price you pay to get in.

"If you're looking at gold to be the growth section of your portfolio, you're likely going to be disappointed," he said. "Gold is insurance, not an engine, and the mistake is buying insurance when its price has skyrocketed," he added.

For investors who bought at January's peak as a modest, long-term hedge, a sharp downturn doesn't defeat the purpose, he said. "You have nothing to really worry about, because that's what the whole goal of gold was."

Gold tends to do well when the dollar weakens or when markets and geopolitics turn uncertain, Mills said, and when those pressures fade, the rest of a portfolio should pick up the slack.

"That's the whole point of insurance, and the whole point of diversification," he said. "Gold does well when the other parts of your portfolio might be slacking, and when they flip-flop, that's a good thing."

Other commodities experts say gold remains a good long-term bet. Amy Gower, head of metals and mining strategy at Morgan Stanley, told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe" that there are "still lots of reasons" for investors to hang on to their gold despite the correction this year.

Philip Diehl, former director of the U.S. Mint and now president of U.S. Money Reserve, an Austin, Texas-based distributor of government-issued gold, silver and platinum coins, said the long-term case for gold is still in place, but the combination of abrupt shifts in U.S. trade policy and geopolitical alliances, along with the Iran war, qualifies as a black swan event for the precious metal.

"The fundamentals that drove that bull market remain in place. They were interrupted by the Cat 5 hurricane that was unleashed in the Persian Gulf in an unwise war," Diehl said.

He expects that to change after American voters "issued a mandate" to end that war two weeks ago.

"I'm confident gold prices will return to their upward trajectory in 2027 as oil prices normalize and prices fall at the pump, allowing interest rates to stabilize," Diehl said.

Central banks continue to buy gold, with the highest monthly total in September, according to the World Gold Council, though overall purchasing is 7% lower than the same period last year.

Marc Chandler, managing partner and chief market strategist at Bannockburn Capital Markets, said surveys suggest that, in aggregate, that will not change. "However, don't only think about China or U.S. rivals," Chandler said, noting that the World Gold Council has identified Poland, a NATO member, as one of the larger buyers for most of this year.

"Retail has been burned," Chandler said. "Higher interest rates have made non-interest-bearing assets less attractive, including bitcoin."

Chandler said that the price of gold is headed to $3,500 to $3,600. Gold hit a fresh low below $3,700 on Thursday morning.

"There is nothing on the charts that says a low is in place," Chandler said. "For the anxious bottom pickers, I urge caution and being patient to see some reversal pattern. For the most part, the decline looks fairly orderly."

"Gold is still very expensive compared to anything but the last two years," Chandler said, noting that gold's 10-year (120-month) moving average is about $2,127 and its 20-year (240-month) moving average is about $1,666.

#2862. Rams captain has blunt message for LA’s defense before Packers showdown

The Los Angeles Rams spent millions of dollars assembling a loaded defense in the offseason.

They signed an All-Pro cornerback in Trent McDuffie, acquired the reigning Defensive Player of the Year winner in Myles Garrett, and lured future Hall of Famer Aaron Donald out of retirement.

Now, as they head into their bye week, just one week away from a primetime Thanksgiving Eve showdown with the Green Bay Packers, it would be a good time for that investment to start paying dividends.

Trent McDuffie of the Los Angeles Rams stands on the field before an NFL football game against the Los Angeles Chargers in Inglewood, Calif., Nov. 1, 2026. Getty Images

Against Green Bay, and with their playoff hopes potentially hanging in the balance, the Rams can't afford to just stop Jordan Love and the Packers' offense, they need to create turnovers and McDuffie knows it.

"Our defense is sitting somewhere in the 20s for turnover ratio," McDuffie told The California Post in an exclusive interview. "So it's a big mindset this week: turnovers, turnovers."

He's close. The Rams are currently tied for 16th in opponents' turnovers in the NFL, and that's before the rest of the league plays their Week 11 slate of games.

But that message of needing to force turnovers carries even more weight heading into a nationally televised Thanksgiving Eve showdown between two teams tied for the NFC's seventh and final playoff spot.

The winner would seize control of the head-to-head tiebreaker. The loser could find itself chasing not only victories but favors from other teams over the final stretch.

The Rams' current injury crisis puts even more pressure on their defense heading into this crucial matchup.

Trent McDuffie #22 of the Los Angeles Rams stands on the sidelines prior to an NFL game against the Los Angeles Chargers in Inglewood, Calif., Nov. 1, 2026. Getty Images

The Rams will take the field against Green Bay without star receivers Puka Nacua and Davante Adams, along with starting center Coleman Shelton. Left guard Steve Avila and tight end Terrance Ferguson are also dealing with hamstring injuries, leaving their availability uncertain.

The Rams' offense has continued to find creative ways to replace those weapons, but if McDuffie, Garrett and Donald can help that offense with stops, sacks, turnovers, and field position, it could be the difference between making the playoffs and watching them from home come January.

Quentin Lake #37 of the Los Angeles Rams celebrates with teammates after an interception against the Los Angeles Chargers, Sunday, Nov. 1, 2026, in Inglewood, Calif. Getty Images

"W​​hether it's picks, whether it's forced fumbles, whether it's turnovers on fourth down, whatever we can do to get the ball back to the offense and, like I said, produce this win," McDuffie said.

McDuffie, whose 98.9 impact grade leads all NFL cornerbacks according to Pro Football Network, understands the difference between a good defense and one capable of changing a season.

"As a defense, we're coming with the right mindset and attitude and understanding of what this game is," said McDuffie. "We have a really good opportunity. Everybody's kind of in this mode where it's 'fight for a win.' And you've got to love being in those battles."

The Rams' defense is coming off a 34-6 victory over the Arizona Cardinals in which they held them to no touchdowns and just two field goals. They will need that performance to carry over against the Packers if they want to keep their playoff hopes alive.

"This is a huge one for everybody," McDuffie said.

#2861. Game Preview: San Antonio Spurs vs. Los Angeles Clippers

The win streak was nice to watch while it lasted, but the San Antonio Spurs will look to quickly rebound from their speed bump when hosting the Los Angeles Clippers on Thursday. The Spurs only lost back-to-back games five times last year, and it happened once to start this season.

It hasn't been smooth for the Clippers as they are one of two winless teams on the road (0-5), the other being the Milwaukee Bucks. This season, they are one of the bottom-third passing teams, which makes them predictable and easier to guard without too many players who can bend the defense on the bounce. Their reliance on screen rolls hasn't produced good results either, scoring 0.83 points per possession.

The Spurs have the third-ranked defense, mainly by doing a good job of helping the helper and neutralizing the action in the lane, permitting just 44.9 points. If they are keyed in, not taking the Clippers lightly, and hit the glass as hard as usual, it could be an easy night so the starters don't work too hard and the role players get plenty of burn.

Spurs Injuries: Dylan Harper: out – left hip soreness; Tobias Harris: out – left calf strain; Maliq Brown: out – G League/two-way; Ja'Kobi Gillespie: out – G League/two-way; Jayden Quaintance : out – right knee surgery.

Clippers Injuries: Bradley Beal: out – right knee management; Brandon Ingram: out – right heel surgery; Nick Martinelli: out – G League/two-way; Jordan Miller: out – left rotator cuff surgery.

One of the Clippers' weaknesses is allowing 15.4 open 3-point attempts per game. Notably, the Spurs allow the fewest open 3-pointers (10.7) and are top six in limiting the wide-open attempts, too. Additionally, some of the Spurs' best 3-point shooting nights have come against weak defensive teams, so it wouldn't be unreasonable to expect at least 14 makes.

Brook Lopez is no slouch, having broken 20 points thrice in his last five games, so Victor Wembanyama has to guard him. The idea is not letting him get comfortable when the Clippers run their pick-and-pop. On top of that, this big-man battle will be worth the watch since Lopez is making 80.8 of his attempts at the rim, and Wembanyama is averaging 4.1 blocks. Even if Lopez is stronger, Wemby should be able to take care of him without help, which should help protect the arc.

Stephon Castle will presumably get most of the minutes guarding Darius Garland. This might be a matchup that Castle dominates with his length and strength since Garland is significantly smaller and has been careless with the ball this year, averaging 4.6 turnovers per game.

The Clippers' bench is the second-highest scoring unit in the league (45.8). Despite most of their reserves logging low shooting percentages, the Spurs cannot lose track of Max Strus and Keaton Wagler. Most of the work here will be ensuring that they don't receive too many clean catches.

Wemby put up numbers against the Detroit Pistons, but his efficiency was unremarkable. Lopez is the only player with the size to guard him, but he doesn't want to come out on the perimeter too much, so expect to see open jumpers galore.

Castle or De'Aaron Fox will take on more of the playmaking duties following Dylan Harper's absence caused by nasty slip in the Detroit loss. Each option getting more usage is dependable and should be able to get a step past almost every defender. Considering Julian Champagnie's fit with the main rotation, he will presumably start the game.

#2860. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 stocks for their solid growth potential

Investors are closely monitoring earnings releases of artificial intelligence companies amid concerns about the durability of high spending and demand. While quarterly results provide key insights into a company's performance and execution, it is prudent to focus on long-term prospects and look beyond short-term fluctuations.

Top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick stocks with attractive long-term prospects as their assessments are backed by in-depth research of a company's fundamentals and growth opportunities.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

AI-powered data analytics provider Palantir Technologies is this week's first pick. The company reported market-beating second-quarter results. Moreover, Palantir raised its full-year guidance and now expects U.S. commercial business revenue to grow by at least 134%.

Following the Q2 print, Bank of America analyst Mariana Perez Mora reiterated a buy rating on PLTR stock with a price target of $255. Mora said that Palantir's performance reflects the success of its AI strategy, which focuses on delivering the right infrastructure to customers and ensuring better outcomes.

"The main driver continues to be the U.S. commercial business, which now represents almost 40% of total (vs. 30% a year ago and just 20% a couple of years back)," noted Mora.

The five-star analyst highlighted the 149% growth in Palantir's U.S. commercial business in Q2 2026 and the expansion in its customer base and engagement levels, with clients pursuing more integrated relationships. Mora noted that the U.S. commercial customer count increased 35% year-over-year to 653, with trailing twelve months revenue per customer surging 76% to $3.5 million in the second quarter.

Citing strength across Palantir's commercial and government segments, Mora increased her sales and earnings per share estimates for 2026-2028. The analyst attributed the increase in her estimates to the growth in the commercial business, backed by stronger total contract value in the second quarter.

Mora ranks No. 444 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 32.6%. See Palantir Insider Trading Activity on TipRanks.

E-commerce and cloud computing giant Amazon impressed investors with its strong second-quarter results. Revenue from the company's Amazon Web Services (AWS) cloud business surged 37%, marking the unit's fastest growth since 2021.

In reaction to the Q2 2026 performance, JPMorgan analyst Doug Anmuth reiterated a buy rating on Amazon stock and raised his price target to $365 from $330, saying that it remains the firm's Best Idea. Anmuth highlighted that the company's forex-neutral top-line growth accelerated to 20% in Q2 2026, the fastest in 20 quarters. He added that the top line gained from acceleration across both AWS and stores.

The five-star analyst noted that AWS growth of 37% was the fastest in 18 quarters, with backlog jumping nearly 2.5x year-over-year and 36% sequentially to $496 billion. Amazon's backlog gained from robust demand across both core workloads and AI adoption. Anmuth also observed the strength in AWS' AI and chips businesses, Graviton revenue commitments, and AWS' core business.

Notably, Anmuth believes that the core AWS business is highly correlated to AI revenue, and he expects further strengthening of this relationship as more AI workloads move into full-scale production and fuel higher demand. Overall, the analyst is more optimistic on Amazon following Q2 results and raised his 2026 and 2027 sales estimates by about 1% to 2% and operating income by about 5% to 7%.

"Importantly, management emphasized that the [return on invested capital] on its AI investments is compelling and it has a clear line-of-sight to strong financial returns," said Anmuth.

Anmuth ranks No. 717 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.5%. See Amazon Statistics on TipRanks.

Moving on to Lam Research, a supplier of semiconductor manufacturing equipment and services. Thanks to AI-driven demand, the company delivered better-than-anticipated fiscal fourth quarter results.

In reaction to the solid results, Oppenheimer analyst Edward Yang reaffirmed a buy rating on LRCX stock with a price target of $400. Yang stated that Lam Research exceeded June-quarter expectations despite the high bar, driven by strong performance of the Customer Support Business Group and doubling of NAND revenue from the prior quarter. He also cited the improvement in Q4 FY26 gross margin.

The five-star analyst highlighted that Lam Research's September-quarter revenue and EPS outlook exceeded the Street's expectations by mid-teens percentages. Yang added that the company increased its wafer fabrication equipment spending outlook to the low-$150 billion range, up from its prior $140 billion estimate.

Importantly, Yang noted that Lam Research called calendar year 2027 "extraordinary," citing persistent supply shortages and the addition of 8 to 10 new fabrication plants. The analyst raised his 2027 revenue and EPS estimates by 7% and 9% to $33 billion and $8.88, respectively. Yang also increased his 2028 revenue and EPS estimates by 7% and 8% to $36.6 billion and $9.89, respectively.

"It's the cleanest way to play AI-driven 3D scaling across memory, foundry/logic, and packaging," said Yang about LRCX.

Yang ranks No. 256 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 71% of the time, delivering an average return of 56.4%. See Lam Research Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2859. Jensen Huang's — and Nvidia's — legal exposure balloons

The still-unraveling scandal of former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's extramarital affairs and the tech giant's potential role in its advanced semiconductor chips ending up in China present a legal minefield for Huang and Nvidia.

Federal and state prosecutors and Nvidia employees may target Huang and the company in court, where some shareholders have already taken action.

That's on top of a rapidly growing pile of probes by Republicans who control Congress now and Democrats who will have majorities in both the House and Senate come January.

"It's not going to get any better for Jensen Huang; it's going to get far worse," said Salvatore Graziano, a New York securities litigation attorney at Bernstein, Litowitz, Berger & Grossmann. His firm is looking at shareholder lawsuit opportunities against Nvidia and Huang.

[Huang] "faces the Department of Justice, the Securities and Exchange Commission and private litigation," Graziano said. "The criminal exposure is very serious. The SEC could bar him from being an officer. And the potential liability [from shareholder] lawsuits can be very large."

Huang's and Nvidia's legal exposure stems from three issues that led up to his shock resignation Nov. 12.

There's his alleged affair with suspected Chinese spy Alice Huang, who worked for Megaspeed, a Singapore-based company under investigation for diverting advanced Nvidia semiconductor chips to China in violation of U.S. export rules. That, in turn, has raised questions about whether Huang and Nvidia were aware of any diversions.

There are Huang's two other alleged affairs with Nvidia employees.

And there is the sharp drop in the company's stock price in the wake of the scandal. Nvidia's share price has fallen 19% since Nov. 8, when Huang sent a company-wide apology acknowledging extramarital affairs.

Huang last week retained the law firm Paul, Weiss and its attorneys Melinda Haag and Walter Brown to represent him. Haag is a former U.S. attorney in San Francisco, and Brown is also a former federal prosecutor.

Huang's spokesman at Trident, a crisis communications firm, declined to comment when asked if Haag and Brown are representing him in any potential criminal case and other questions about his legal exposure.

Nvidia did not respond to a request for comment. Last week, Nvidia retained the law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to conduct an independent investigation into Huang allegedly being targeted by a spy, as U.S. intelligence reportedly has concluded.

The civil liability Huang and Nvidia could face is underscored by a hearing Graziano, the securities attorney, attended Tuesday in U.S. District Court in San Francisco. A judge at the hearing approved a $175 million settlement from Seagate Technology Holdings to resolve a shareholder lawsuit alleging it concealed sales of hard-disk drives to Huawei in China in violation of export bans.

The Seagate settlement came three years after the Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security — which is investigating Megaspeed's business with Nvidia — imposed a $300 million penalty on Seagate for its sales to Huawei.

Graziano said his law firm is "actively looking at securities law liability for Nvidia, Jensen Huang and other senior executives in the company given all the recent developments regarding their [potential] knowledge and facilitation of illegal chip sales to Chinese entities."

There is already a pending class-action shareholder lawsuit in San Francisco federal court against Nvidia and Huang by the Rosen Law Firm, for allegedly misleading and false statements related to Megaspeed and Alice Huang.

"Several hundreds of investors have contacted us and expressed an interest to join the case — investors of all walks of life, from mom and pops to investors with very large losses," Rosen attorney Phil Kim told CNBC in an email.

Katie Sweeten, a former federal prosecutor in the DOJ's National Security Division, said the question of "who knew what and when, and what was [Huang's] role" is potentially the biggest risk for Nvidia and him.

"Now DOJ can leverage their criminal authorities," Sweeten, a partner at Scale LLP, told CNBC's Kelly Evans on "The Exchange."

"They can bring grand jury subpoenas, search warrants for emails, bank accounts, personal devices, computers, things like that," Sweeten said.

While it is "not illegal to, you know, sleep with a spy ... it is illegal to pass certain types of information to a counterintelligence officer," she said.

Sweeten also pointed to potential export control violations, which "could be very serious."

She noted the DOJ can indict a company as well as its officials if there is evidence of criminality. Export control violations carry a maximum possible criminal sentence of 20 years in prison and "really large fines," she said.

"You can't send the company to prison, but they can face fines, civil penalties, debarment — where you can't export items down the line," she said.

One example of that came in July 2025, when the DOJ obtained a guilty plea and an agreement to pay more than $140 million from Cadence Design Systems for violating the Export Control Reform Act by unlawfully exporting semiconductor design tools to China's National University of Defense Technology, a school led by China's Central Military Commission.

Another potential legal risk for Huang comes from his planned appearances before Congress.

Huang is scheduled to give a closed-door interview Dec. 1 to three House committees. Senate banking committee Chair Tim Scott, R-S.C., wants Huang's testimony before that panel to be public, and told CNBC Tuesday he hopes to have that happen "before Christmas."

Sweeten said Huang "has to be very careful" about what he says there because of the risk of being criminally charged with lying to Congress, and of saying something "that absolutely can become part of a criminal or civil investigation."

"I expect Huang to take the Fifth on most questions" from Congress, Jonathan Kanter, a former assistant attorney general in the DOJ's Antitrust Division, told CNBC, meaning Huang will likely use his constitutional right against giving testimony that could be used against him in a criminal case.

"With a Justice Department criminal investigation into Megaspeed already underway and more investigations possible, anything he tells Congress could be used against him," Kanter said. "His lawyers are likely to advise him against taking that risk."

Ben Heineman Jr., a former senior vice president and general counsel of General Electric, and a senior fellow at Harvard University, told CNBC that Nvidia's board will be scrutinized over its actions before it pushed Huang to resign.

Heineman cited the board's apparent lack of succession planning and what looks like weak oversight of Huang, including relating to personal conduct such as the two alleged affairs with Nvidia employees and with an executive of an Nvidia customer.

"When there is a sort of reasonable suspicion, they need to investigate it, and they don't appear to have done that," Heineman said.

Nvidia and Huang could face investigation from state attorneys general, including in California, where the company is headquartered.

On Tuesday, California Attorney General Rob Bonta told Bloomberg Television that the situation at Nvidia with Huang is "something that we're interested in." Bonta's office later told CNBC, "We are monitoring the situation."

Huang's alleged affairs with Nvidia employees also open the possibility of workplace litigation.

Ari Wilkenfeld, a Washington-area employment lawyer, said a consensual relationship between a chief executive and a subordinate "is not in and of itself illegal."

"The liability never comes from the existence of the relationship alone. It comes from whether it was genuinely welcome, what job benefits flowed from it, and what it did to everyone else in the building," Wilkenfeld said. "California law recognizes that so-called sexual favoritism can create a hostile environment for an entire workforce.

"The affair is Huang's problem," Wilkenfeld said. "What, if anything, Nvidia did once it knew is the company's problem."

#2858. Kyrie Irving showed love to his former fan, Warriors rookie Yaxel Lendeborg

Kyrie Irving showed love to his former fan, Warriors rookie Yaxel Lendeborg originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

The Warriors continue to struggle without star guard Steph Curry, losing their fourth straight game on Wednesday, a 116-94 loss to the Dallas Mavericks.

Warriors rookie Yaxel Lendeborg, who scored 13 points with five rebounds and four assists in 30 minutes, came into the game with extra motivation, in large part, because he faced his childhood idol, Mavericks guard Kyrie Irving.

Irving and Lendeborg have built a mutual respect in recent months, which led to Irving gifting Lendeborg a signed jersey after the game as a symbol of his appreciation for the Warriors rookie's lifelong support.

Speaking to the San Francisco Chronicle's Luca Evans after the game, Irving praised Lendeborg, while declaring his admiration for his game.

"We had a conversation in the summertime after he got drafted," Irving said. "He's been an advocate and a supporter of Kyrie FC, it's like a fan club that I have, for a minute. But just unconditional support, unconditional love before he got into the league. That was cool. I didn't really know much about him. I watched him in college a little bit and fell in love with his game and his skill set and what he's able to do out there. And obviously, we have [guard Morez Johnson Jr.] here as well like, both of them playing for the championship team.

"It's interesting to see the dynamics. But yeah, it was great to play against him finally. I could tell he was a little nervous. I think he's learning on the fly and he has a lot of pressure on his back right now to be the number one guy, number two guy. I know he has a lot of pressure on his shoulders so I just hope he takes it easy a little bit."

Lendeborg, the No. 11 overall pick in the 2026 NBA Draft, spoke to the media after his selection, and said that as a fan of Irving, he used to "hate" Curry. However, as one of his newest teammates, he has learned to admire and learn from both.

Lendeborg's first season has been impressive, with the 24-year-old among the early Rookie of the Year candidates after averaging 20.3 points, 7.3 rebounds and 3.5 assists per game in 15 contests.

He has multiple 30-point games, and his early season success has cemented his status as Golden State's second star to complement Curry.

Lendeborg and the Warriors face a quick turnaround, traveling to Memphis to take on the Grizzlies on Thursday night. Tip-off is at 5 p.m. PT at FedEx Forum.

#2857. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

The stock market continues to grapple with volatility amid geopolitical tensions in the Middle East and concerns about the sustainability of the AI boom. Given this uncertainty, investors looking for steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick the right dividend stocks, as these experts assign their ratings after an in-depth analysis of a company's financials and ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Downstream energy company Phillips 66 (PSX) is this week's first dividend pick. With a quarterly dividend $1.27 per share (annualized dividend of $5.08), PSX offers a yield of 2.25%. The company recently reported solid Q2 earnings, as the Middle East conflict affected global supplies and drove up refining margins.

Following the Q2 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Phillips 66 and raised his price target to $255 from $240, reflecting higher 2026 earnings expectations and lower interest expense next year.

Gabelman highlighted the quarter-over-quarter reduction in Phillips 66's net debt, noting management's optimism at achieving its estimated $15.5 billion net debt target one year ahead of schedule. The 5-star analyst expects the company to end 2026 with a net debt of $14.6 billion.

"The [balance sheet] improvement could re-establish PSX as a go-to defensive refiner," said Gabelman.

Management agreed that PSX's payout ratio lagged year-to-date, but expects buybacks to increase in the second half. Gabelman said Phillps indicated the possibility of a larger dividend hike following an annual increase of 5% over the past two years.

Gabelman ranks No. 554 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Phillips 66 Statistics on TipRanks.

Crescent Energy (CRGY) is an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian and Uinta Basins. Earlier this month, the company reported better-than-expected Q2 earnings and announced a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share, payable on August 31. At an annualized dividend of $0.48 per share, CRGY boasts a dividend yield of about 4%.

In reaction to Q2 results, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reaffirmed a buy rating on Crescent Energy stock with a price target of $18. He noted continued strength in the company's performance, with second-quarter oil production and cash flow surpassing the Street's expectations.

"CRGY's cash flow exceeded expectations by 10%, reinforcing its trajectory of capital efficiency," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that Crescent raised its full-year oil production guidance, citing the effective integration of the Vital Energy acquisition. Specifically, Crescent tripled its synergy target from the Vital Energy deal to as much as $300 million, significantly reducing the effective purchase price. This reflects Crescent's solid execution following the acquisition, Richardson said.

Additionally, the analyst noted that Crescent's capital spending is and heading toward the lower end of management's prior guidance, indicating disciplined financial controls.

Richardson ranks No. 579 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 65% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.5%. See Crescent Energy Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Finally, Viper Energy (VNOM) is effectively controlled by Diamondback Energy. Viper owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests in oil-weighted basins, mainly the Permian in West Texas. Viper recently announced its Q2 2026 results and declared a 32% hike in its base dividend, effective in the third quarter of 2026. The new base dividend indicates a 4.5% annualized yield.

Viper also removed its quarterly commitment to return at least 75% of cash available for distribution. The company believes that this move will ensure increased flexibility to make opportunistic share repurchases and pursue accretive mergers and acquisitions.

Following the Q2 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper, slightly raising his price target to $59 from $58. Bilkoski said Viper's solid Q2 performance supports his view that the strength in oil prices and rising operator activity are driving higher production.

"Viper has delivered, and we forecast will continue to deliver, one of the highest production per share growth profiles in our royalty universe" through the end of 2027, said Bilkoski. He believes Viper deserves a premium valuation, given its above-average growth.

The 5-star analyst said that while the biggest surprise in the quarter was Viper's revision to its shareholder return framework, he doesn't believe that the move indicates a significant change to its underlying strategy. Under the new framework, a larger portion of excess free cash flow is expected to be allocated to share repurchases than to variable dividends, Bilkoski said.

Bilkoski ranks No. 719 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Viper Energy Financials on TipRanks.
